Representations of Kronecker Quivers and Steiner Bundles on Grassmannians
Daniel Bissinger and Rolf Farnsteiner

Abstract
. Let 𝕜 \mathbbm{k} be an algebraically closed field. Connections between representations of the generalized Kronecker quivers K r K_{r} and vector bundles on ℙ r − 1 \mathbb{P}^{r-1} have been known for quite some time. This article is concerned with a particular aspect of this correspondence, involving more generally Steiner bundles on Grassmannians Gr d ⁡ ( 𝕜 r ) \Gr_{d}(\mathbbm{k}^{r}) and certain full subcategories rep_{proj} ⁡ ( K r , d ) \repp(K_{r},d) of relative projective K r K_{r} -representations. Building on a categorical equivalence first explicitly established by Jardim and Prata [ 40 ] , we employ representation-theoretic techniques provided by Auslander-Reiten theory and reflection functors to organize indecomposable Steiner bundles in a manner that facilitates the study of bundles enjoying certain properties such as uniformity and homogeneity. Conversely, computational results on Steiner bundles motivate investigations in rep_{proj} ⁡ ( K r , d ) \repp(K_{r},d) , which elicit the conceptual sources of some recent work on the subject. From a purely representation-theoretic vantage point, our paper initiates the investigation of certain full subcategories of the, for r ≥ 3 r\!\geq\!3 , wild category of K r K_{r} -representations. These may be characterized as being right Hom-orthogonal to certain algebraic families of elementary test modules.



Introduction

Let
k
be an algebraically closed field of characteristic
p\!>\!0
. In their groundbreaking article
[
32
]
, Friedlander and Pevtsova associated vector bundles to representations of infinitesimal group schemes by
means of the so-called universal nilpotent operators. In subsequent work
[
18
]
, Carlson-Friedlander-Suslin computed some of these bundles for the second Frobenius kernel
\mathbb{G}_{a(2)}
of the additive group

\mathbb{G}_{a}
. The bundles considered in
[
18
]
are kernels of the nilpotent operators associated to the so-called
W
-modules. These modules are graded with respect to the standard grading of the “group algebra”

k\mathbb{G}_{a(2)}
, and the grading induces a decomposition of the associated vector bundles.

The vector bundles studied in
[
18
]
are defined on the projective line
\mathbb{P}^{1}
and hence decompose into a direct sum of Serre shifts of the structure sheaf. For group schemes of type
\mathbb{G}_{a(r)}
there are natural
generalizations of
W
-modules, whose bundles have base space
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
. When studying their graded pieces as mentioned above, one is naturally led to bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
that are associated to certain
representations of the
r
-Kronecker quiver
K_{r}
. The observation that their duals are examples of the so-called Steiner bundles motivated the investigations of this paper.

Since the introduction of the notion of Steiner bundles by Dolgachev-Kapranov
[
23
]
, the original definition has been generalized to include vector bundles over projective varieties with short resolutions or
coresolutions given by certain exceptional pairs. We will confine our attention to Steiner bundles of Grassmannians, whose defining resolution is given by the universal bundle and trivial bundles, cf.
[
1
]
. In the
aforementioned context of group schemes, the study of the interplay between representations of elementary abelian
p
-groups and vector bundles on Grassmannians was initiated in
[
16
]
. Being based on
morphisms between Serre shifts of trivial vector bundles, the approach in loc. cit. differs from the point of view taken here, which can also be adapted to representations of elementary abelian groups.

The by now numerous connections between vector bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
and representations of the generalized Kronecker quiver
K_{r}
, were apparently first systematically exploited in Hulek’s article

[
39
]
.
1
1

1

Hulek considers representations of dimension vectors
(n,n)
, whereas our approach is based on dimension vectors
(m,n)
with
m\!<\!n
.
While in most cases, the authors are working over the
complex numbers, the examples alluded to above guide us to work in greater generality:
Throughout,
\mathbbm{k}
is assumed to be an algebraically closed field of arbitrary characteristic. All vector spaces are assumed to be
finite-dimensional over
\mathbbm{k}
.

Our paper is organized as follows. After recalling basic features of representations of Kronecker quivers, we introduce in Section
2
the full subcategories
\repp(K_{r},d)
of the category
\rep(K_{r})
of
representations of the Kronecker quiver that turn out to correspond to Steiner bundles on the Grassmannian of
d
-planes of
\mathbbm{k}^{r}
. Their objects may be described as those having trivial rank varieties; they may also be
thought of as relative projective modules of certain ring extensions. The category
\repp(K_{r},1)
coincides with the category
\EKP(K_{r})
of equal kernels representations, whose definition was inspired by
[
18
]
, and
which was studied in our context in a number of papers including
[
64
,
8
]
. Our first main result, Theorem
2.1.5
, describes the objects
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
via a family
(E(\mathfrak{v}))_{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(\mathbbm{k}^{r})}
of
elementary test modules. This observation is the gateway for our applications of the shift functor
\sigma_{K_{r}}:\rep(K_{r})\longrightarrow\rep(K_{r})
and Auslander-Reiten theory. We summarize some of our findings as follows:

Theorem A
.

Suppose that
r\!\geq\!3
and
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

A representation
M\in\rep(K_{r})
belongs to
\repp(K_{r},d)
if and only if
\Hom_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}),M)\!=\!(0)
for every
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(\mathbbm{k}^{r})
.

(2)

\repp(K_{r},d)\subseteq\EKP(K_{r})
is a torsion-free class containing all preprojective representations.

(3)

We have
\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)\subseteq\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!2)\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\repp(K_{r},2)\subseteq\repp(K_{r},1)\!=\!\EKP(K_{r})
.

(4)

We have
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(\repp(K_{r},d))\subseteq\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
.

Generalizing a consequence of Westwick’s fundamental result
[
63
]
, we introduce, for a given
M\!=\!(M_{1},M_{2},(M(\gamma_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq r})\in\rep(K_{r})
and
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, the invariant
\Delta_{M}(d)\!:=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}
and show that
\Delta_{M}(d)\!\geq\min\{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1},d\}(r\!-\!d)
whenever
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
. Moreover, if
(V_{1},V_{2})
is a pair of vector spaces such that

\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d)\!\geq\!d(r\!-\!d)
, then there always exists
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
such that
(M_{1},M_{2})\!=\!(V_{1},V_{2})
.

Given
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\},
we introduce in Section
3
the functor
\TilTheta_{d}:\rep(K_{r})\longrightarrow\Coh(\Gr_{d}(\mathbbm{k}^{r}))
which assigns to a representation of
K_{r}
a coherent sheaf on
\Gr_{d}(\mathbbm{k}^{r})
. The essential
image of
\repp(K_{r},d)
under
\TilTheta_{d}
coincides with the full subcategory
\StVect(\Gr_{d}(\mathbbm{k}^{r}))
of Steiner bundles. By definition (see
[
1
]
), each Steiner bundle
\mathcal{F}
affords a short resolution

(0)\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}^{s}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(\mathbbm{k}^{r})}^{t}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow(0),

where
\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}
denotes the universal subbundle on
\Gr_{d}(\mathbbm{k}^{r})
. Since
\repp(K_{r},d)\subseteq\repp(K_{r},e)
for
e\!\leq\!d
, one can in fact associate Steiner bundles
\TilTheta_{e}(M)\in\StVect(\Gr_{e}(\mathbbm{k}^{r}))
to each
M\in\repp(K_{,}d)
(
e\!\leq\!d
). The functor
\TilTheta_{e}:\repp(K_{r},d)\longrightarrow\StVect(\Gr_{e}(\mathbbm{k}^{r}))
is full and faithful and
\TilTheta_{d}:\repp(K_{r},d)\longrightarrow\StVect(\Gr_{d}(\mathbbm{k}^{r}))
is an
equivalence, cf.
[
40
]
. We record a few basic properties of
\TilTheta_{d}
and, by way of illustration, interpret the results of Section
2.3
in the context of Steiner bundles.

The present understanding of the wild category
\rep(K_{r})
mainly rests on Kac’s Theorem
[
43
]
concerning the distribution of the dimension vectors of indecomposable representations and the Auslander-Reiten
quiver
\Gamma(K_{r})
, which describes
\rep(K_{r})
modulo the square of its radical. In Section
4
we introduce these tools and provide first applications concerning exceptional Steiner bundles, which have also
been referred to as Fibonacci bundles, cf.
[
14
]
. Much of our subsequent work on non-exceptional Steiner bundles builds on Ringel’s determination
[
56
]
of the regular Auslander-Reiten
components of
\Gamma(K_{r})
. This enables us to identify the parts of AR-components belonging to
\repp(K_{r},d)
and thus yields a way to efficiently organize the corresponding Steiner bundles of
\Gr_{d}(\mathbbm{k}^{r})
, see
Section
4.3
.
2
2

2

In case
r\!=\!2
, it is well-known that
\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{1})
is a
\Hom
-finite abelian
\mathbbm{k}
-category with Serre duality. It therefore affords almost split sequences, see
[
33
, §2]
.

In the next two sections, we apply the aforementioned concepts in the context of homogeneity, uniformity and stability, with the latter two only being discussed in the classical setting of projective spaces. Since the
inhomogeneity of an indecomposable Steiner bundle is inherited by all other members of its component, one can easily construct families of inhomogeneous bundles that are uniform of certain type. There are various
notions of equivariance for modules and vector bundles, which are equivalent to homogeneity in some cases, such as modules with trivial space endomorphisms or over fields of characteristic
0
. We intend to address
these connections along with those involving rational representations of parabolic subgroups of
\SL(A_{r})
in future work, where it turns out to be expedient to interpret vector bundles as locally trivial vector space fibrations.

Given
M\in\EKP(K_{r})\!\smallsetminus\!\{(0)\}
one defines the slope
\mu(M)\!:=\!\frac{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}{\Delta_{M}(1)}
and observes that
\mu(\TilTheta_{1}(M))\!=\!\mu(M)
for the standard slope on
\Vect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
. This
motivates the investigation of stability for modules and Steiner bundles and our main result in Section
6.1
shows that modules corresponding to stable Steiner bundles are stable. Non-projective modules
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
of minimal type are stable and our results of Section
6.2
concerning the distribution of slopes relative to AR-components imply that elementary
\EKP
-modules also enjoy this property. This is applied in
Section
6.3
, where we provide a sufficient condition on a pair
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
involving the Tits form for
\EKP
-modules of dimension vector
(a,b)
to afford a filtration by elementary
\EKP
-modules. Specializing to
r\!=\!3
, we use methods by Drezet
[
24
]
to give a sufficient condition for the stability of a bundle corresponding to a stable Kronecker representation. In particular, we verify:

Theorem B
.

Let
r\!\geq\!3
.

(1)

Suppose that
M\in\EKP(K_{r})\!\smallsetminus\!\{(0)\}
.

(a)

If
\TilTheta_{1}(M)
is (semi)stable, so is
M
.

(b)

Let
r\!=\!3
and
\Delta_{M}(2)\!\geq\!0
(
\Delta_{M}(2)\!>\!0
). If
M
is semistable (stable), so is
\TilTheta_{1}(M)
.

(c)

Let
r\!=\!3
. If
M
is elementary, then
\TilTheta_{1}(M)
is stable.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Gamma(K_{3})
be an Auslander-Reiten component containing an elementary module. Then every Steiner bundle
\mathcal{F}\in\TilTheta_{1}(\mathcal{C}\cap\EKP(K_{3}))
possesses a Harder-Narasimhan filtration
by Steiner bundles, whose filtration factors are stable.

The final section is concerned with an application pertaining to restrictions of Steiner bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}(\mathbb{C})
to linear hyperplanes
H\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{r-1}(\mathbb{C})
. We provide conditions on the rank and the first Chern
class ensuring that a generic Steiner bundle
\mathcal{F}
with these data is stable with none of its restrictions
\mathcal{F}|_{H}
being semistable.

The authors would like to thank Chiara Brambilla for patiently explaining her work to them, Adrian Langer for bringing reference
[
11
]
to their attention, and Enrique Arrondo and Simone Marchesi for correspondence.

1.
Preliminaries

1.1.
Representations of
K_{r}

Let
r\!\geq\!2
be a natural number. We denote by
K_{r}
the (generalized) Kronecker quiver with two vertices
1
and
2
and
r
arrows:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2.}
⋮
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{r}}

(For
r\!=\!1
one obtains the quiver
K_{1}
with underlying Dynkin diagram
A_{1}
.)

It will be convenient to view representations of
K_{r}
in the following equivalent ways:

(i)

A
representation

M
of the quiver
K_{r}
is a triple
(M_{1},M_{2},(M(\gamma_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq r})
, where
M_{1},M_{2}
are
finite-dimensional vector spaces and each
M(\gamma_{i}):M_{1}\longrightarrow M_{2}
is a
\mathbbm{k}
-linear map. A homomorphism
f:M\longrightarrow N
is a pair
(f_{1},f_{2})
of
\mathbbm{k}
-linear maps
f_{j}:M_{j}\longrightarrow N_{j}\ (j\in\{1,2\})
such that,
for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M(\gamma_{i})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(\gamma_{i})}

commutes.

(ii)

Let
A_{r}\!:=\!\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbbm{k}\gamma_{i}
be the
arrow space
of
K_{r}
. We consider the triangular matrix algebra

\mathbbm{k}K_{r}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbbm{k}&0\\
A_{r}&\mathbbm{k}\end{pmatrix},

which is isomorphic to the path algebra of
K_{r}
. Accordingly,
\rep(K_{r})
is equivalent to the category
\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
of finite-dimensional
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
-modules. In view of
[
4
, (III.2.2)]
, the category
\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
is equivalent to the category
\mathcal{K}_{r}
, whose objects are triples
M\!=\!(M_{1},M_{2},\psi_{M})
, where
M_{1},M_{2}
are finite-dimensional
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces and
\psi_{M}:A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}\longrightarrow M_{2}
is
\mathbbm{k}
-
linear. A morphism
M\longrightarrow N
is a pair
(f_{1},f_{2})
of
\mathbbm{k}
-linear maps
f_{i}:M_{i}\longrightarrow N_{i}
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!N_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{A_{r}}\otimes f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{N}}

commutes.

(iii)

One can equally well consider triples
(M_{1},M_{2},\psi_{M})
, where
\psi_{M}:A_{r}\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(M_{1},M_{2})
is
\mathbbm{k}
-linear. The condition for a morphism
(f_{1},f_{2})
then reads

\psi_{N}(a)\circ f_{1}=f_{2}\circ\psi_{M}(a)\ \ \ \ \forall\ a\in A_{r}.

(iv)

For future reference we also record the interpretation as triples
(M_{1},M_{2},\varphi_{M})
, where
\varphi_{M}:M_{1}\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r},M_{2})
is given by
\varphi_{M}(m)(a)\!:=\!\psi_{M}(a\otimes m)
for all

m\in M_{1}
and
a\in A_{r}
.

We denote by
\rep(K_{r})
the category of all (finite-dimensional) representations of
K_{r}
and refer the reader to
[
4
]
and
[
2
]
for further details.

Every subspace
\mathfrak{v}\subseteq A_{r}
gives rise to a subalgebra

\mathbbm{k}.\mathfrak{v}:=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbbm{k}&0\\
\mathfrak{v}&\mathbbm{k}\end{pmatrix}

of
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
. If
M
is a
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
-module, the restriction
M|_{\mathbbm{k}.\mathfrak{v}}
corresponds to
(M_{1},M_{2},\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})
, where
\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\!=\!\psi_{M}|_{\mathfrak{v}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}M}
. By choosing a basis of
\mathfrak{v}
one obtains an
equivalence
\modd\mathbbm{k}.\mathfrak{v}\cong\rep(K_{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathfrak{v}})
, which depends on the choice of the basis.

Given a pair
(V_{1},V_{2})
of
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces, we let
\udim(V_{1},V_{2})\!\>=\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1},\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{2})
be its
dimension vector
. For
M\in\rep(K_{r})
, we write
\udim M\!:=\!\udim(M_{1},M_{2})
. The category

\rep(K_{r})
has two simple objects (representations)
S_{1},S_{2}
of dimension vectors
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
, respectively. The object
S_{1}
is injective, while
S_{2}
is projective. The projective cover
P_{1}(r)
of

S_{1}
has dimension vector
\udim P_{1}(r)\!=\!(1,r)
. We will also write
P_{0}(r)\!:=\!S_{2}
.

Note that

\omega_{r}:\mathbbm{k}K_{r}\longrightarrow\mathbbm{k}K_{r}\ \ ;\ \ \left(\begin{smallmatrix}\alpha&0\\
a&\beta\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\beta&0\\
a&\alpha\end{smallmatrix}\right)

is an anti-automorphism of order
2
. There results a duality
D_{K_{r}}:\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}\longrightarrow\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}\ \ ;\ \ M\mapsto M^{\ast}
given by

(x.f)(m):=f(\omega_{r}(x).m)\ \ \ \ \ \ \forall\ f\in M^{\ast},m\in M,x\in\mathbbm{k}K_{r}.

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
. For
a\in A_{r}
, we denote by

a_{M}:M_{1}\longrightarrow M_{2}\ \ ;\ \ m\mapsto a.m

the multiplication effected by
a
. Then the
dual

D_{K_{r}}(M)
of
M\in\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
corresponds to
D_{K_{r}}(M)\!=\!(M_{2}^{\ast},M_{1}^{\ast},\psi_{D_{K_{r}}(M)})
, where

\psi_{D_{K_{r}}(M)}(a\otimes f)=f\circ a_{M}\ \ \ \ \ \forall\ a\in A_{r},f\in M_{2}^{\ast}.

We denote by

\langle\,,\,\rangle_{r}:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\!\times\!\mathbb{Z}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\ \ ;\ \ (x,y)\mapsto x_{1}y_{1}\!+\!x_{2}y_{2}\!-\!rx_{1}y_{2}

the
Euler-Ringel bilinear form
. The corresponding quadratic form

q_{r}:\mathbb{Z}^{2}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}\ \;\ \ x\mapsto\langle x,x\rangle_{r}

is referred to as the
Tits quadratic form
of
K_{r}
.

The dimension vectors give rise to an isomorphism

\udim:K_{0}(\rep(K_{r}))\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{2},

which identifies the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(\rep(K_{r}))
of
\rep(K_{r})
with
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Recall that

\langle\udim M,\udim N\rangle_{r}\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(M,N)\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(M,N)

for all
M,N\in\rep(K_{r})
. Moreover, as the path algebra
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
is hereditary, we have
\Ext^{i}_{K_{r}}(-,-)\!=\!(0)
for all
i\!\geq\!2
.

Let
M\in\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
. Then
\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(M,\mathbbm{k}K_{r})
is a right
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
-module, so that

\tau_{K_{r}}(M)\!:=\!\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(M,\mathbbm{k}K_{r})^{\ast}

is a left
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
-module. There results a (covariant) endofunctor

\tau_{K_{r}}:\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}\longrightarrow\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}

which, by virtue of
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
being hereditary, is left exact. By the same token,
\tau_{K_{r}}
is exact when restricted to the full subcategory of those modules which have no non-zero projective direct summands.

The functor
\tau_{K_{r}}
induces an endofunctor on
\rep(K_{r})
, which we will also denote by
\tau_{K_{r}}
. General theory
[
4
, (VIII.2.2)]
implies that

\udim\tau_{K_{r}}(M)=\Phi_{r}.\udim M

for every non-projective indecomposable
M\in\rep(K_{r})
,
3
3

3

When multiplying matrices with vectors, we will always implicitly assume that the vector has the correct shape and thus dispense with transposing vectors.

where

\Phi_{r}\!=\!\begin{pmatrix}r^{2}\!-\!1&-r\\
r&-1\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

is the
Coxeter matrix
of the quiver
K_{r}
. An indecomposable representation
M\in\rep(K_{r})
is called
preprojective
, provided
\tau^{n}_{K_{r}}(M)\!=\!(0)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Similarly, we consider the functor

\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}:\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}\longrightarrow\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}\ \ ;\ \ M\mapsto\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(M^{\ast},\mathbbm{k}K_{r}).

Then we have

\udim\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)=\Phi^{-1}_{r}.\udim M

for every non-injective indecomposable representation
M\in\rep(K_{r})
. An indecomposable representation
M\in\rep(K_{r})
is called
preinjective
, provided
\tau^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M)\!=\!(0)
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Indecomposable representations that are neither preprojective nor preinjective are said to be
regular
.

The preprojective
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
-modules are well understood. For
r\!\geq\!3
, we put
L_{r}\!:=\!\frac{r+\sqrt{r^{2}-4}}{2}
and set

a_{i}(r):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{(L_{r})^{i}-(\frac{1}{L_{r}})^{i}}{\sqrt{r^{2}-4}}&r\!\geq\!3\\
i&r\!=\!2\end{array}\right.\ \ \ \ \forall\ i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}.

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there is, up to isomorphism, exactly one preprojective module
P_{i}(r)
such that

(a)

P_{0}(r)\!=\!S_{2}
;
P_{1}(r)
is the projective cover of
S_{1}
, and

(b)

\udim P_{i}(r)\!=\!(a_{i}(r),a_{i+1}(r))
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

For every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there is an almost split sequence

(0)\longrightarrow P_{i}(r)\longrightarrow rP_{i+1}(r)\longrightarrow P_{i+2}(r)\longrightarrow(0).

In particular,
\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(P_{i}(r))\cong P_{i+2}(r)
.

In case the module category to be considered is clear from the context, we will write
P_{i}
instead of
P_{i}(r)
.

1.2.
Module varieties

In this section, we recall a number of general results from the representation theory of quivers, most of which can be found in
[
43
]
.

Let
A
be an
n
-dimensional associative
\mathbbm{k}
-algebra,
V
be a
\mathbbm{k}
-vector space. We denote by
\modd(A;V)
the affine variety of
A
-module structures on
V
. When
convenient we will identify
M\in\modd(A;V)
with its representation
\varrho_{M}:A\longrightarrow\End_{\mathbbm{k}}(V)
. If
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
is a basis of
A
, then

\iota_{V}:\modd(A;V)\longrightarrow\End_{\mathbbm{k}}(V)^{n}\ \ ;\ \ M\mapsto(\varrho_{M}(a_{1}),\ldots,\varrho_{M}(a_{n}))

is a closed embedding.

If
X
is a variety and
x\in X
, then
\dim_{x}X
denotes the
local dimension of
X
at
x
.

Lemma 1.2.1
.

Let
V
and
W
be
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces. The map

d:\modd(A;V)\!\times\!\modd(A;W)\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}\ \ ;\ \ (M,N)\mapsto\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{A}(M,N)

is upper semicontinuous.
\square

In analogy with the above, one can consider the variety
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
of representations of
K_{r}
on a pair
(V_{1},V_{2})
of vector spaces. There is an isomorphism

\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\modd(\mathbbm{k}K_{r};V_{1}\!\oplus\!V_{2})^{\rightarrow}

between
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
and the closed subset
\modd(\mathbbm{k}K_{r};V_{1}\!\oplus\!V_{2})^{\rightarrow}
of the module variety of the path algebra
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
, given by conditions
(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix})\boldsymbol{.}(v_{1}\!\oplus\!v_{2})\!=\!v_{1}
and
(\begin{smallmatrix}0&0\\
0&1\end{smallmatrix})\boldsymbol{.}(v_{1}\!+\!v_{2})\!=\!v_{2}.

Recall that
M\in\rep(K_{r})
is referred to as a
brick
, provided
\End_{K_{r}}(M)\cong\mathbbm{k}
. Preprojective and preinjective representations are known to be bricks.

Corollary 1.2.2
.

Suppose that
r\!\geq\!3
and let
V_{1},V_{2}
be vector spaces with
V_{1}\!\oplus\!V_{2}\!\neq\!(0)
and such that
q_{r}(\udim(V_{1},V_{2}))\!\leq\!1
. Then

\mathcal{B}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}:=\{M\in\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\ ;\ M\ \text{is a brick}\}

is a dense open subset of
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
.

Proof.

Since
r\!\geq\!3
, the assumption
q_{r}(a,b)\!=\!0
implies
a\!=\!0\!=\!b
.

If
q_{r}(\udim(V_{1},V_{2}))\!\leq\!0
, we may apply
[
43
, Prop.1]
(see also
[
20
, (3.6)]
for an elementary proof valid in our context) in conjunction with the observation above to see that the set

\mathcal{B}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}
is not empty. Alternatively, general theory provides
M\in\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
that is preprojective or preinjective (see Theorem
4.1.1
below). In particular,
M
is a brick.

Lemma
1.2.1
implies that the map
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}\ ;\ M\mapsto\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\End_{K_{r}}(M)
is upper semicontinuous, so that
\mathcal{B}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}
is open. Since the variety
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\cong\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})^{r}
is irreducible,
\mathcal{B}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}
thus lies dense in
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
. ∎

Let
V_{1},V_{2}
be non-zero
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces. The algebraic group
G\!:=\!\GL(V_{2})\!\times\!\GL(V_{1})
acts on the irreducible variety
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})^{r}
via

(g_{2},g_{1})\boldsymbol{.}(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq r}:=(g_{2}\circ f_{i}\circ g_{1}^{-1})_{1\leq i\leq r}.

Upper semicontinuity of fiber dimension implies that the map

\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})^{r}\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}\ \ ;\ \ x\mapsto\dim G\boldsymbol{.}x

is lower semicontinuous. Thus, if
s\!:=\!\max\{\dim G\boldsymbol{.}x\ ;\ x\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})^{r}\}
, then the
open sheet

\mathcal{O}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}:=\{x\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})^{r}\ ;\ \dim G\boldsymbol{.}x\!=\!s\}

for the action of
G
on
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})^{r}
is a non-empty open subset of
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})^{r}\!=\!\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
.

The following result, which was first proved by Kac
[
43
, Prop.1]
, actually shows that for those dimension vectors for which
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
contains indecomposable modules, the modules belonging to the open
sheet are just the bricks.

Corollary 1.2.3
.

Let
r\!\geq\!3
. Suppose that
V_{1},V_{2}
are vector spaces with
V_{1}\!\oplus\!V_{2}\!\neq\!(0)
and such that
q_{r}(\udim(V_{1},V_{2}))\!\leq\!1
. Then we have
\mathcal{B}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!=\!\mathcal{O}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}
.

Proof.

Given any
M\in\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
, the stabilizer
G_{M}
of
M
coincides with
G\cap\End_{K_{r}}(M)
, and thus is a dense open subset of
\End_{K_{r}}(M)
.

By Corollary
1.2.2
, there is
M_{0}\in\mathcal{B}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\cap\mathcal{O}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}
, so that

s=\dim G\boldsymbol{.}M_{0}=\dim G\!-\!\dim G_{M_{0}}=\dim G\!-\!1.

Let
M\in\mathcal{B}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}
. Then
\dim G_{M}\!=\!1
, so that
\dim G\boldsymbol{.}M\!=\!s
. This implies
\mathcal{B}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!\subseteq\!\mathcal{O}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}
.

On the other hand, if
M\in\mathcal{O}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}
, then
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\End_{K_{r}}(M)\!=\!\dim G_{M}\!=\!\dim G\!-\!s\!=\!1
, whence
M\in\mathcal{B}_{(V_{1},V_{2})}
. ∎

1.3.
Restrictions

For
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, we let
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
be the Grassmannian of
d
-planes of
A_{r}
. This is an irreducible projective variety of dimension
\dim\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)
.
For
M\in\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
and
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, we denote by
M|_{\mathfrak{v}}
the restriction of
M
to
\mathbbm{k}.\mathfrak{v}\subseteq\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
.

Given
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces
V_{1},V_{2}
, we consider the set

\Iso(K_{d};V_{1},V_{2})\!:=\!\rep(K_{d};V_{1},V_{2})/(\GL(V_{2})\!\times\!\GL(V_{1}))

of isomorphism classes together with the projection map
\pi:\rep(K_{d};V_{1},V_{2})\longrightarrow\Iso(K_{d};V_{1},V_{2})
. We equip
\Iso(K_{d};V_{1},V_{2})
with the final topology relative to
\pi
. This renders
\pi
a continuous map
such that
U\subseteq\Iso(K_{d};V_{1},V_{2})
is open if and only if
\pi^{-1}(U)\subseteq\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
is open.

Let

\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r}):=\{\alpha\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\ ;\ \alpha\ \text{is injective}\}.

This is a conical, open subset of the affine space
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
. In particular,
\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
is a quasi-affine variety.

The algebraic groups
\GL(A_{d})
and
\GL(A_{r})
act on the affine variety
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
via

g.\alpha:=\alpha\circ g^{-1}\ \ \ \forall\ g\in\GL(A_{d})\ \ \text{and}\ \ h.\alpha:=h\circ\alpha\ \ \ \forall\ h\in\GL(A_{r}),

respectively. The subvariety
\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\subseteq\Hom_{k}(A_{d},A_{r})
is stable with regard to these commuting actions.

Every
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
defines an injective homomorphism

\alpha:\mathbbm{k}K_{d}\longrightarrow\mathbbm{k}K_{r}\ \ ;\ \ \left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
a&y\end{smallmatrix}\right)\longrightarrow\left(\begin{smallmatrix}x&0\\
\alpha(a)&y\end{smallmatrix}\right)

of
\mathbbm{k}
-algebras. In particular,
\GL(A_{r})
can be considered a group of automorphisms of
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
.

Let
M\in\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
. Given
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
, we let
\alpha^{\ast}(M)\in\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{d}
be the pull-back of
M
along
\alpha
. Thus, we have

u\boldsymbol{.}m:=\alpha(u)m\ \ \ \ \ \forall\ u\in\mathbbm{k}K_{d},m\in M.

In particular,
\GL(A_{r})\subseteq\Aut_{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathbbm{k}K_{r})
acts on
\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
via pull-back: If
M\in\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
, and
g\in\GL(A_{r})
, then

M^{(g)}:=(g^{-1})^{\ast}(M).

We say that
M
is
homogeneous
, provided
M^{(g)}\cong M
for every
g\in\GL(A_{r})
.

Example
.

Let
P_{i}\!=\!(k[X,Y]_{i-1},k[X,Y]_{i},X\cdot,Y\cdot)
be the preprojective
\mathbbm{k}K_{2}
-module of dimension vector
(i,i\!+\!1)
. Then
\GL(A_{2})\!=\!\GL(\mathbbm{k}X\!\oplus\!\mathbbm{k}Y)

acts of
\mathbbm{k}[X,Y]
via algebra homomorphisms of degree zero in such a way that

g(\gamma f)=g(\gamma)g(f)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \forall\ g\in\GL(A_{2}),\gamma\in A_{2},f\in\mathbbm{k}[X,Y]_{i-1}.

Consequently,
g
induces a
\mathbbm{k}
-linear isomorphism
P_{i}^{(g)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}P_{i}
such that

g(\gamma\boldsymbol{.}f)=g(g^{-1}(\gamma)f)=\gamma g(f).

As a result, the
\mathbbm{k}K_{2}
-module
P_{i}
is homogeneous. (In fact, the
P_{i}
are examples of equivariant
K_{r}
-representations.)

We let
\GL(A_{r})
act on
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
via
h\boldsymbol{.}\mathfrak{v}\!:=\!h(\mathfrak{v})
for all
h\in\GL(A_{r})
and
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. This action is transitive.

Following
[
64
]
, we denote by
\EKP(K_{r})
the full subcategory of
\rep(K_{r})
such that
a_{M}
is injective for every
a\in A_{r}\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\}
.
4
4

4

The notation derives from the
correspondence with the category of equal kernels modules studied in
[
18
]
.
Note that
\EKP(K_{r})
is closed under taking subobjects.

The classification of indecomposable objects in
\rep(K_{2})
implies that every
N\in\EKP(K_{2})
decomposes into a direct sum

N\cong\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}n_{i}P_{i}.

Lemma 1.3.1
.

Let
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
. Then we have

\alpha^{\ast}(M)\cong\beta^{\ast}(M)

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
such that
\im\alpha\!=\!\im\beta
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
. Since
\alpha
is injective, it follows that
\alpha^{\ast}(M)\in\EKP(K_{2})
. By virtue of the foregoing remark and the example above, we conclude that
\alpha^{\ast}(M)

is homogeneous.

If
\beta\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
is such that
\im\alpha\!=\!\im\beta
, then
g\!:=\!\beta^{-1}\circ\alpha\in\GL(A_{2})
, so that for
m\in(\alpha^{\ast}(M))^{(g)}
and
\gamma\in A_{2}
, we obtain

\gamma\boldsymbol{.}m=g^{-1}(\gamma).m=\alpha(g^{-1}(\gamma))m=\beta(\gamma)m.

By the example above, this shows that
\beta^{\ast}(M)\cong(\alpha^{\ast}(M))^{(g)}\cong\alpha^{\ast}(M)
. ∎

Let
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
,
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
. In view of the foregoing result, we may define the isomorphism class
[M|_{\mathfrak{v}}]
via

[M|_{\mathfrak{v}}]:=[\alpha^{\ast}(M)]\ \ \ \ \ \alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\ ;\ \im\alpha\!=\!\mathfrak{v}.

Less formally, we will write

M|_{\mathfrak{v}}\cong\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}n_{i}(M,\mathfrak{v})P_{i}(2)

to indicate that the above isomorphism holds for
\alpha^{\ast}(M)
, where
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
is such that
\im\alpha\!=\!\mathfrak{v}
.

Given
X\in\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})
, we write

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{2}}(X,M|_{\mathfrak{v}}):=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{2}}(X,\alpha^{\ast}(M)).

for some
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
with
\im\alpha\!=\!\mathfrak{v}
.

Proposition 1.3.2
.

Let
r\!\geq\!2
,
M\in\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

The map

\res_{M}:\Gr_{2}(A_{r})\longrightarrow\Iso(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})\ \ ;\ \ \mathfrak{v}\mapsto[M|_{\mathfrak{v}}]

is continuous.

(2)

If
X\in\rep(K_{2};U_{1},U_{2})
, then the map

d_{X,M}:\Gr_{2}(A_{r})\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}\ \ ;\ \ \mathfrak{v}\mapsto\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{2}}(X,M|_{\mathfrak{v}})

is upper semicontinuous.

Proof.

(1) It follows from
[
17
, (1.1)]
that the map

\msim:\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\longrightarrow\Gr_{2}(A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ \alpha\mapsto\im\alpha

is a principal
\GL(A_{2})
-bundle (a
\GL(A_{2})
-torsor). In particular,

\overline{\msim}:\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})/\GL(A_{2})\longrightarrow\Gr_{2}(A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ \bar{\alpha}\mapsto\im\alpha

is a homeomorphism.

Consider the map

\widetilde{\res}_{M}:\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\longrightarrow\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})\ \ ;\ \ \alpha\mapsto\alpha^{\ast}(M).

We identify
\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})
with
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})^{2}
and let
\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\}
be a basis of
A_{2}
. Then

\widehat{\res}_{M}:\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})^{2}\ \ ;\ \ \alpha\mapsto(M(\alpha(\gamma_{1})),M(\alpha(\gamma_{2})))

is a linear map and hence in particular a morphism. (Here we have written
M(\alpha(\gamma_{i}))\!:=\!\psi_{M}(\alpha(\gamma_{i})\!\otimes-)
.) Consequently,

\widetilde{\res}_{M}\!=\!\widehat{\res}_{M}|_{\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})}
is also a morphism.

In view of Lemma
1.3.1
,
\widetilde{\res}_{M}
gives rise to a continuous map

\iso_{M}:\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})/\GL(A_{2})\longrightarrow\Iso(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})\ \ ;\ \ \bar{\alpha}\mapsto[\alpha^{\ast}(M)].

Since

\res_{M}\circ\overline{\msim}=\iso_{M},

we obtain (1).

(2) Thanks to Lemma
1.2.1
, the map

d_{X}:\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}\ \ ;\ \ Y\mapsto\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{2}}(X,Y)

is upper semicontinuous. Since images of
(\GL(V_{2})\!\times\!\GL(V_{1}))
-stable open sets under
\pi:\rep(K_{2};V_{2},V_{2})

\longrightarrow\Iso(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})
are open, the map

\bar{d}_{X}:\Iso(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}\ \ ;\ \ [Y]\mapsto\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{2}}(X,Y)

enjoys the same property. In view of (1),
d_{X,M}\!=\!\bar{d}_{X}\circ\res_{M}
is upper semicontinuous as well. ∎

Corollary 1.3.3
.

Let
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
. Then there exists a sequence
(n_{i}(M))_{i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{\mathbb{N}_{0}}
such that

O_{M}:=\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{2}(A_{r})\ ;\ n_{i}(M,\mathfrak{v})\!=\!n_{i}(M)\ \ \ \forall\ i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}

is a dense open subset of
\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
.

Proof.

For
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we consider the map
d_{i}\!:=\!d_{P_{i}(2),M}
. Proposition
1.3.2
provides
c_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

O_{i}:=\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{2}(A_{r})\ ;\ d_{i}(\mathfrak{v})=c_{i}\}

is a dense open subset of
\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
. Since
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{2}}(P_{i}(2),P_{j}(2))\!=\!\max\{j\!-\!i\!+\!1,0\}
, it follows that

n_{i}(M,\mathfrak{v})=d_{i}(\mathfrak{v})\!-\!2d_{i+1}(\mathfrak{v})\!+\!d_{i+2}(\mathfrak{v})

for every
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
and
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Consequently,

n_{i}(M,\mathfrak{v})=c_{i}\!-\!2c_{i+1}\!+\!c_{i+2}

for every
\mathfrak{v}\in O_{i}\cap O_{i+1}\cap O_{i+2}
. There exists
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
n_{i}(M,\mathfrak{v})\!=\!0\!=\!d_{i}(\mathfrak{v})
for all
i\!\geq\!\ell
and
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
. We define

n_{i}(M):=c_{i}\!-\!2c_{i+1}\!+\!c_{i+2}.

for every
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and obtain
n_{i}(M)\!=\!0\!=n_{i}(M,\mathfrak{v})
for all
i\!\geq\!\ell
. Consequently,
\bigcap_{i=0}^{\ell-1}O_{i}\subseteq O_{M}
.

Suppose that
\mathfrak{v}\in O_{M}
. Then we have

d_{i}(\mathfrak{v})\!-\!2d_{i+1}(\mathfrak{v})\!+\!d_{i+2}(\mathfrak{v})=n_{i}(M,\mathfrak{v})=c_{i}\!-\!2c_{i+1}\!+\!c_{i+2}\ \ \ \ 0\!\leq\!i\!\leq\!\ell\!-\!1.

Since the
(\ell\!\times\!\ell)
-matrix

A=\begin{pmatrix}1&-2&1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&1&-2&1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&1&-2&1\\
0&0&\cdots&0&1&-2\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}

is invertible, it follows that
d_{i}(\mathfrak{v})\!=\!c_{i}
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Consequently,
\mathfrak{v}\in\bigcap_{i=0}^{\ell-1}O_{i}
, so that
O_{M}\!=\!\bigcap_{i=0}^{\ell-1}O_{i}
is a dense, open subset of
\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
. ∎

Definition
.

Let
M\in\EKP(K_{r})\!\smallsetminus\!\{(0)\}
. We call

M_{\gen}:=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}n_{i}(M)P_{i}

the
generic decomposition
of
M
.

We let
\mathbb{P}(A_{r})
be the projective space of lines in
A_{r}
. The basis
\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}\}
defines an isomorphism
\mathbb{P}(A_{r})\cong\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
of projective varieties. For
x\in\mathbb{P}(A_{r})
the number

\rk(x_{M})\!=\!\rk(a_{M})\ \text{for some}\ a\in x\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\}
is well-defined and we refer to

\rk(M):=\max\{\rk(x_{M})\ ;\ x\in\mathbb{P}(A_{r})\}

as the
generic rank
of
M
. We say that
M
has
constant rank
, provided
\rk(x_{M})\!=\!\rk(M)
for all
x\in\mathbb{P}(A_{r})
. The full subcategory of
\rep(K_{r})
, whose objects have constant rank, will be denoted

\CR(K_{r})
. It contains the full subcategory
\EIP(K_{r})
, whose objects
M
satisfy the condition
\rk(M)\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}
. We have a duality
D_{K_{r}}:\CR(K_{r})\longrightarrow\CR(K_{r})
that sends
\EKP(K_{r})
to
\EIP(K_{r})
and
vice versa. The aforementioned classification of indecomposable
K_{2}
-representations implies that
\CR(K_{2})\!=\!\add(\EKP(K_{2})\cup\EIP(K_{2}))
, so that every
M\in\CR(K_{r})
affords a generic decomposition

M_{\gen}=\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}n_{i}(M)P_{i}(2)\!\oplus\!\bigoplus_{j\geq 0}m_{j}(M)I_{j}(2),

where
I_{j}(2)\!:=\!D_{K_{r}}(P_{j}(2))
is indecomposable preinjective.

2.
Categories of relative projective modules

Let
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. In this section we introduce the full subcategory
\repp(K_{r},d)
of
\EKP(K_{r})
, that will turn out to be equivalent to the category of Steiner bundles on the Grassmannian
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.
The objects of
\repp(K_{r},d)
may be characterized via rank varieties, whose definition is analogous to those investigated in
[
16
]
. By contrast to the setting in
[
16
]
, their complements may also be
defined as being the objects of the right
\Hom
-orthogonal category of a class of elementary
K_{r}
-modules.

2.1.
Rank varieties and families of elementary modules

As noted above, the map

\msim:\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\longrightarrow\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ \alpha\mapsto\im\alpha

is a
\GL(A_{d})
-torsor with respect to the canonical right
\GL(A_{d})
-action
\alpha.h\!:=\!\alpha\circ h
, cf.
[
17
, (1.1)]
.

Lemma 2.1.1
.

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

The subset

\mathcal{P}(K_{r},d)_{M}:=\{\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\ ;\ \alpha^{\ast}(M)\ \text{is not projective}\}

is closed and
\GL(A_{d})
-stable.

(2)

The set

\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}:=\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ \exists\ \alpha\in\msim^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})\ \text{such that}\ \alpha^{\ast}(M)\ \text{is not projective}\}

is a closed subset of
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.

Proof.

(1) Recall that a
\mathbbm{k}K_{d}
-module
N
is projective if and only if
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{K_{d}}(N,S_{2})\!=\!0
. In view of the upper semicontinuity of the map
\rep(K_{d};M_{1},M_{2})\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}\ ;\ N\mapsto\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{K_{d}}(N,S_{2})
,
5
5

5

This follows for instance from Lemma
1.2.1
and
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{K_{d}}(N,S_{2})\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{d}}(N,S_{2})\!-\!\langle\udim N,\udim S_{2}\rangle_{d}
.
we
conclude that

C_{d}:=\{X\in\rep(K_{d};M_{1},M_{2})\ ;\ X\ \text{is not projective}\}

is a closed subset of
\rep(K_{d};M_{1},M_{2})
. The arguments of the proof of Proposition
1.3.2
ensure the continuity of the map

\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\longrightarrow\rep(K_{d};M_{1},M_{2})\ \ ;\ \ \alpha\mapsto\alpha^{\ast}(M).

Consequently,
\mathcal{P}(K_{r},d)_{M}
is a closed subset of
\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
.

Recall that
P_{1}
and
S_{2}
are representatives of the isomorphism classes of the projective indecomposable
\mathbbm{k}K_{d}
-modules. Since their dimensions pairwise differ, it follows that
Q^{(h)}\cong Q
for

Q\in\{P_{1},S_{2}\}
and
h\in\GL(A_{d})
and hence
P^{(h)}\cong P
for every projective
\mathbbm{k}K_{d}
-module
P
. Let
\alpha\not\in\mathcal{P}(K_{r},d)_{M}
and
h\in\GL(A_{d})
. Then

(h\boldsymbol{.}\alpha)^{\ast}(M)\cong(\alpha^{\ast}(M))^{(h)},

is projective, so that
h\boldsymbol{.}\alpha\not\in\mathcal{P}(K_{r},d)_{M}
. As a result, the variety
\mathcal{P}(K_{r},d)_{M}
is
\GL(A_{d})
-stable.

(2) As before, we consider the space
\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})/\GL(A_{d})
together with the topology induced by the canonical projection

\pi:\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\longrightarrow\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})/\GL(A_{d}).

As observed in the proof of Proposition
1.3.2
, the map

\msim:\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\longrightarrow\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ \alpha\mapsto\im\alpha

induces a homeomorphism

\overline{\msim}:\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})/\GL(A_{d})\longrightarrow\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ [\alpha]\mapsto\im\alpha.

In view of (1), the open set
U_{M}\!:=\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\!\smallsetminus\!\mathcal{P}(K_{r},d)_{M}
is
\GL(A_{d})
-stable. Thus,
\pi^{-1}(\pi(U_{M}))\!=\!U_{M}
, so that
\pi(U_{M})\!=\!(\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})/\GL(A_{d}))\!\smallsetminus\!\pi(\mathcal{P}(K_{r},d)_{M})
is an open subset of
\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})/\GL(A_{d})
. It follows that
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}\!=\!\msim(\mathcal{P}(K_{r},d)_{M})\!=\!\overline{\msim}(\pi(\mathcal{P}(K_{r},d)_{M}))
is closed. ∎

Remark
.

Since
\GL(A_{d})
acts transitively on
\im^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})
, it follows from (1) that

\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}=\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ \alpha^{\ast}(M)\ \text{is not projective for all}\ \alpha\in\msim^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})\}.

Lemma 2.1.2
.

Let
(0)\longrightarrow M^{\prime}\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow M^{\prime\prime}\longrightarrow(0)
be an exact sequence in
\rep(K_{r})
.

(1)

We have

\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}\subseteq\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M^{\prime}}\cup\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M^{\prime\prime}}.

(2)

If the sequence splits, then

\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}=\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M^{\prime}}\cup\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M^{\prime\prime}}.

Proof.

(1) Since the algebra
\mathbbm{k}K_{d}
is hereditary, we have
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M^{\prime}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}
.

(2) This is a direct consequence of (1). ∎

We consider the projective
K_{r}
-representations
(0,A_{d})\cong P_{0}(r)^{d}
and
(\mathbbm{k},A_{r})\cong P_{1}(r)
, where we have
\psi_{P_{1}(r)}(a\!\otimes\!t)\!=\!ta
. An element
\alpha\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
can be viewed as a
morphism

\bar{\alpha}:(0,A_{d})\longrightarrow(\mathbbm{k},A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ \bar{\alpha}_{1}\!:=\!0\ \ \text{and}\ \ \bar{\alpha}_{2}\!:=\!\alpha.

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
. As noted in Section
1.1
,
M
may be interpreted as a linear map

\varphi_{M}:M_{1}\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r},M_{2})\ \ ;\ \ \varphi_{M}(m)(a)\!:=\!\psi_{M}(a\!\otimes m).

Given
\alpha\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
, we obtain

\varphi_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}:M_{1}\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},M_{2})\ \ ;\ \ m\mapsto\varphi_{M}(m)\circ\alpha.

A representation
M\in\rep(K_{r})
is called
regular
, if all its indecomposable constituents are regular. We recall the notion of elementary representations, which are just the simple objects in the full subcategory
\reg(K_{r})\subseteq\rep(K_{r})
of regular representations.

Definition
.

A non-zero regular representation
E\in\rep(K_{r})
is referred to as
elementary
, provided there does not exist a short exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow A\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow B\longrightarrow(0)

with
A,B
non-zero and regular.

Proposition 2.1.3
.

Let
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

The module
\coker\bar{\alpha}
is elementary with dimension vector
\udim\coker\bar{\alpha}\!=\!(1,r\!-\!d)
.

(2)

Let
\beta\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
. Then
\coker\bar{\alpha}\cong\coker\bar{\beta}
if and only if
\im\alpha\!=\!\im\beta
.

(3)

If
N\in\rep(K_{r})
is indecomposable with
\udim N\!=\!(1,r\!-\!d)
, then there is
\zeta\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
such that
N\cong\coker\bar{\zeta}
.

(4)

Given
M\in\rep(K_{r})
, we have an isomorphism of vector spaces
\coker\varphi_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}\cong\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(\coker\bar{\alpha},M)
.

(5)

Given
c\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
and
\beta\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{c},A_{d})
, there exists an epimorphism
\coker\overline{\alpha\circ\beta}\longrightarrow\coker\bar{\alpha}
.

Proof.

(1)-(3) Recall that the projective cover
P_{1}(r)
of the simple representation
S_{1}\!=\!(\mathbbm{k},0)
is given by
P_{1}(r)\!=\!(\mathbbm{k},A_{r},(P_{1}(r)(\gamma_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq r})
with
P_{1}(r)(\gamma_{i})(\lambda)\!=\!\lambda\gamma_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
. For
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
we consider the monomorphism of representations
x_{\alpha}\!=\!(0,(x_{\alpha})_{2})\colon P_{0}(r)^{d}

\longrightarrow P_{1}(r)
, given by

(x_{\alpha})_{2}\colon\mathbbm{k}^{d}\longrightarrow A_{r}\ \ ;\ \ \lambda\mapsto\sum^{d}_{i=1}\lambda_{i}\alpha(\gamma_{i}).

We get a commutative diagram

\textstyle{(0)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P_{0}(r)^{d}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{x_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{P_{1}(r)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\coker x_{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(0)}
\textstyle{(0)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(0,A_{d})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\alpha}}
\textstyle{(\mathbbm{k},A_{r})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coker\overline{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(0)}

with exact rows. In particular,
\udim\coker\overline{\alpha}\!=\!(1,r\!-\!d)
and the 5-Lemma yields
\coker x_{\alpha}\cong\coker\overline{\alpha}
. Hence (1), (2) and (3) follow from
[
8
, (2.1.4),(2.2.1),(2.2.2)]
,
respectively.

(4) The statement follows from a small modification of the proof of
[
8
, (2.3.3)]
. For the benefit of the reader we outline the changes. Since
\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}((\mathbbm{k},A_{r}),-)\!=\!(0)
, application of
\Hom_{K_{r}}(-,M)
to the
short exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow(0,A_{d})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\overline{\alpha}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\mathbbm{k},A_{r})\longrightarrow\coker\overline{\alpha}\longrightarrow(0)

yields the following diagram with exact rows

\textstyle{\Hom_{K_{r}}((\mathbbm{k},A_{r}),M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\alpha}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\Hom_{K_{r}}((0,A_{d}),M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(\coker\overline{\alpha},M)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(0)}
\textstyle{M_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},M_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\coker\varphi_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(0),}

where
f(h)\!:=\!h_{1}(1_{\mathbbm{k}})
for all
h\in\Hom_{K_{r}}((\mathbbm{k},A_{r}),M)
and
g(\eta)\!:=\!\eta_{2}
for all
\eta\colon(0,A_{d})\longrightarrow M
. Let
(h_{1},h_{2})\!=\!h\in\Hom_{K_{r}}((\mathbbm{k},A_{r}),M)
. We recall that
\psi_{M}\circ(\id_{A_{r}}\otimes h_{1})\!=\!h_{2}\circ\psi_{(\mathbbm{k},A_{r})}
and
\psi_{(\mathbbm{k},A_{r})}(a\otimes\lambda)\!=\!\lambda a
for all
\lambda\in\mathbbm{k}
and
a\in A_{r}
. For
a\in A_{d}
we obtain

\displaystyle[(\varphi_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}\circ f)(h)](a)

\displaystyle=[\varphi_{M}(h_{1}(1_{\mathbbm{k}}))\circ\alpha](a)=[\psi_{M}\circ(\id_{A_{r}}\otimes h_{1})](\alpha(a)\otimes 1_{\mathbbm{k}})

\displaystyle=[h_{2}\circ\psi_{(\mathbbm{k},A_{r})}](\alpha(a)\otimes 1_{\mathbbm{k}})=h_{2}(\alpha(a))=(h\circ\overline{\alpha})_{2}(a)

\displaystyle=[(g\circ\overline{\alpha}^{\ast})(h)](a).

Hence the left-hand square of the diagram commutes and
f,g
being isomorphisms gives us

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\coker\varphi_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(\coker\overline{\alpha},M).

(5) This follows from the proof of
[
8
, (2.2.2(c))]
. ∎

Let
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. Thanks to Proposition
2.1.3
(2), we may define

\coker\mathfrak{v}:=\coker\bar{\alpha}\ \ \ \ \ \ \alpha\in\msim^{-1}(\mathfrak{v}).

Definition
.

For
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, we put

E(\mathfrak{v}):=D_{K_{r}}(\tau_{K_{r}}(\coker\bar{\alpha}))\ \ \ \ \ \ \alpha\in\msim^{-1}(\mathfrak{v}).

In view of
[
46
, (1.1)]

E(\mathfrak{v})
is an elementary representation.

Given
M\in\rep(K_{r})
and
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, we recall the notation

\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}:=(\psi_{M})|_{\mathfrak{v}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}.

Similarly, we define
\varphi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}:M_{1}\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathfrak{v},M_{2})
via

\varphi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}(m):=\varphi_{M}(m)|_{\mathfrak{v}}\ \ \ \ \forall\ m\in M_{1}.

Proposition 2.1.4
.

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
,
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.

(1)

Let
\alpha\in\msim^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})
. Then

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\rk(\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Rad(\alpha^{\ast}(M))

is the multiplicity of
P_{0}(d)
in the decomposition of
\alpha^{\ast}(M)\in\rep(K_{d})
into indecomposable direct summands.

(2)

We have
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}),M)
.

Proof.

(1) This follows immediately from the definition.

(2) We consider the dual representation
D_{K_{r}}(M)=(M_{2}^{\ast},M_{1}^{\ast},\psi_{D_{K_{r}}(M)})
with structure map

\psi_{D_{K_{r}}(M)}\colon A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{2}^{\ast}\longrightarrow M_{1}^{\ast}\ \ ;\ \ a\otimes f\mapsto f\circ a_{M}.

There results a commutative diagram

\textstyle{M_{2}^{\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})^{\ast}}
\textstyle{(\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1})^{\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{M_{2}^{\ast}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{D_{K_{r}}(M),\mathfrak{v}}}
\textstyle{\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathfrak{v},M_{1}^{\ast}),}

with
\eta(f)(a)(m_{1})\!=\!f(a\otimes m_{1})
for all
a\in\mathfrak{v},m_{1}\in M_{1}
and
f\in(\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1})^{\ast}
: For
h\in M_{2}^{\ast},a\in\mathfrak{v}
and
m\in M_{1}
we have

\displaystyle[\varphi_{D_{K_{r}}(M),\mathfrak{v}}(h)(a)](m)

\displaystyle=[\psi_{D_{K_{r}}(M)}(a\otimes h)](m)=(h\circ a_{M})(m)

\displaystyle=(h\circ\psi_{M})(a\otimes m)=(h\circ\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})(a\otimes m)

\displaystyle=[\eta(h\circ\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})(a)](m)=[(\eta\circ(\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})^{\ast})(h)(a)](m).

Hence
\varphi_{D_{K_{r}}(M),\mathfrak{v}}\!=\!\eta\circ(\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})^{\ast}
. Consequently,
\coker\varphi_{D_{K_{r}}(M),\mathfrak{v}}\cong\ker\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}
.

Let
\alpha\in\im^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})
. We conclude with Proposition
2.1.3
(2),(4) that

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\coker\varphi_{\alpha^{\ast}(D_{K_{r}}(M))}=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\coker\varphi_{D_{K_{r}}(M),\mathfrak{v}}=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}.

Since
\coker\overline{\alpha}
is regular, the Auslander-Reiten formula
[
2
, (IV.2.13)]
in conjunction with Proposition
2.1.3
(4) now yields

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\coker\varphi_{\alpha^{\ast}(D_{K_{r}}(M))}=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(\coker\overline{\alpha},D_{K_{r}}(M))

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(D_{K_{r}}(M),\tau_{K_{r}}(\coker\overline{\alpha}))

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}),M)\hfill\qed.

For a pair
(V_{1},V_{2})
of
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces and
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, we set

\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d):=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{2}\!-\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}.

If
M\in\rep(K_{r})
, we write

\Delta_{M}(d):=\Delta_{(M_{1},M_{2})}(d).

Theorem 2.1.5
.

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
. Given
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\Hom_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}),M)\!=\!(0)
.

(2)

For every
\alpha\in\msim^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})
, the map
\psi_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}
is injective.

(3)

There is
\alpha\in\msim^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})
such that
\psi_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}
is injective.

(4)

There is
\alpha\in\msim^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})
such that
\alpha^{\ast}(M)\cong\Delta_{M}(d)P_{0}(d)\!\oplus\!(\dim_{k}M_{1})P_{1}(d)
.

(5)

\mathfrak{v}\not\in\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}
.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2). Let
\alpha\in\im^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})
. Proposition
2.1.4
(2) yields
0\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}),M)\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\psi_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}
.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(3). Trivial.

(3)
\Rightarrow
(4). Let
\alpha\in\msim^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})
be such that
\psi_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}
is injective. Then we have

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\rk(\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1})

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}=\Delta_{M}(d).

By Proposition
2.1.4
(1), we may write
\alpha^{\ast}(M)\!=\!{\Delta_{M}(d)}P_{0}(d)\!\oplus\!N
, where
N\in\rep(K_{d})
does not have
P_{0}(d)
as a direct summand. Therefore, a projective cover of
N
is given by

(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{1})P_{1}(d)\twoheadrightarrow N
. Since
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{1}\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}
and

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{2}=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\Delta_{M}(d)=d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1},

we conclude
\udim N\!=\!\udim[(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{1})P_{1}(d)]
. Hence
\alpha^{\ast}(M)\cong\Delta_{M}(d)P_{0}(d)\!\oplus\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})\!P_{1}(d)
.

(4)
\Rightarrow
(5). This is a direct consequence of the remark following Lemma
2.1.1
.

(5)
\Rightarrow
(1). Let
\alpha\in\msim^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})
. We write
\alpha^{\ast}(M)\cong aP_{0}(d)\!\oplus\!bP_{1}(d)
, so that

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Rad(\alpha^{\ast}(M))=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Rad(aP_{0}(d)\!\oplus\!bP_{1}(d))=b\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Rad(P_{1}(d))=bd.

Since
\udim P_{0}(d)
and
\udim P_{1}(d)
are linearly independent, it follows that
a\!=\!\Delta_{M}(d)
and
b\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}
. We obtain

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\!=\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!\rk(\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})\!=\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Rad(\alpha^{\ast}(M))\!=\!0
, so that Proposition
2.1.4
yields (1). ∎

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
. Theorem
2.1.5
implies

\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}=\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ \rk(\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})\!<\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\},

so that we refer to
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}
as the
d
-th rank variety
of
M
.

Remarks
.

(1) For
d\!=\!1
, we have
\Gr_{1}(A_{r})\!=\!\mathbb{P}(A_{r})
and

\mathcal{V}(K_{r},1)_{M}:=\{x\in\mathbb{P}(A_{r})\ ;\ \rk(x_{M})\!<\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\}.

In particular,
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},1)_{M}\!=\!\emptyset
if and only if
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
.

(2) Let
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
. The pull-back functor
\alpha^{\ast}:\rep(K_{r})\longrightarrow\rep(K_{d})
takes projectives to projectives. Theorem
2.1.5
implies that

\beta\in\GL(A_{d}).\alpha
if and only if for every
M\in\rep(K_{r})
we have
\alpha^{\ast}(M)\ \text{projective}\ \Leftrightarrow\ \beta^{\ast}(M)\ \text{projective}
. Hence

\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})/\GL(A_{d})
may be viewed as an analogue of the space of equivalence classes of
p
-points (in the context of abelian unipotent group schemes), cf.
[
31
]
.

2.2.
The categories
\repp(K_{r},d)

As before, we fix
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. In this section, we introduce the subcategory
\repp(K_{r},d)
of
\rep(K_{r})
that will be instrumental in our study of Steiner bundles on the Grassmannian
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.

Definition
.

We let
\repp(K_{r},d)
be the full subcategory of
\rep(K_{r})
, whose objects
M
are given by
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}\!=\!\emptyset
.

Remarks
.

(1) Note that
\repp(K_{r},1)\!=\!\EKP(K_{r})
is just the category of equal kernels representations, which usually has wild representation type, cf.
[
8
, (4.1.1)]
. The roughly analogous concept of shifted
cyclic subgroups for elementary abelian
p
-groups yields projective modules, see
[
22
, (11.8)]
.

(2) The category
\repp(K_{r},d)
is closed under taking submodules, cf. Lemma
2.1.2
.

(3) Suppose that
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
is such that
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\leq\!d
. Then
A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}\!=\!\bigcup_{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}\mathfrak{v}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}
, so that
\psi_{M}
is injective. Consequently, the direct
summand
(M_{1},\psi_{M}(M_{1}),\psi_{M})
of
M
is isomorphic to
(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(r)
and
M\cong\Delta_{M}(r)P_{0}(r)\!\oplus\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(r)
is projective.

(4) An object
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
can be characterized by saying that
\alpha^{\ast}(M)
is projective for every
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
. Equivalently, for
M\in\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
the restrictions

M|_{\mathbbm{k}.\mathfrak{v}}\in\modd\mathbbm{k}.\mathfrak{v}
are projective for every
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. The latter condition can be shown to be equivalent to
M
being
(\mathbbm{k}K_{r},\mathbbm{k}.\mathfrak{v})
-projective in the sense of relative homological
algebra, cf.
[
38
]
.

Proposition 2.2.1
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

\repp(K_{r},d)\subseteq\EKP(K_{r})
is a torsion free class that is closed under
\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}
.

(2)

\repp(K_{r},d)
contains all preprojective representations.

(3)

We have
\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)\subseteq\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!2)\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\repp(K_{r},1)=\EKP(K_{r})
.

Proof.

(1) and (2) follow from Theorem
2.1.5
and
[
8
, (2.1.3)]
.

(3) Let
M\in\repp(K_{r},b)
for some
1\!<\!b\!<\!r
and consider
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{b-1}(A_{r})
. Then there is
\mathfrak{w}\in\Gr_{b}(A_{r})
such that
\mathfrak{v}\subseteq\mathfrak{w}
. Theorem
2.1.5
yields

\ker\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\subseteq\ker\psi_{M,\mathfrak{w}}=(0),

so that
M\in\repp(K_{r},b\!-\!1)
. ∎

Remark
.

Let
X\subseteq\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
be a subset. Using Theorem
2.1.5
and
[
8
, (2.1.3)]
, one can show that (1) and (2) of the foregoing result hold for the full subcategory
\rep_{X}(K_{r},d)
, whose objects

M
satisfy
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}\subseteq X
.

In the sequel the shift functors
\sigma_{K_{r}},\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}:\rep(K_{r})\longrightarrow\rep(K_{r})
will be of major importance. These functors correspond to the BGP-reflection functors but take into account that the opposite
quiver of
K_{r}
is isomorphic to
K_{r}
, i.e.
D_{K_{r}}\circ\sigma_{K_{r}}\cong\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}\circ D_{K_{r}}
, where
D_{K_{r}}\colon\rep(K_{r})\longrightarrow\rep(K_{r})
denotes the standard duality.

Given a representation
M\in\rep(K_{r})
,
\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)
is by definition the representation

(\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)_{1},\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)_{2})=(\ker\psi_{M},M_{1}),

where we identify
\psi_{M}
by means of the basis
\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}\}
with the map
\psi_{M}:(M_{1})^{r}\longrightarrow M_{2},(m_{i})\mapsto\sum^{r}_{i=1}M(\gamma_{i})(m_{i})
. By definition,
[\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)](\gamma_{i})\colon\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)_{1}\longrightarrow\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)_{2}=\pi_{i}|_{\ker\psi_{M}}
, with
\pi_{i}\colon(M_{1})^{r}\longrightarrow M_{1}
being the projection onto the
i
-th component. If
f\in\Hom_{K_{r}}(M,N)
, then
\sigma_{K_{r}}(f)_{1}:\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)_{1}\longrightarrow\sigma_{K_{r}}(N)_{1}
is the restriction to
\ker\psi_{M}
of the map

(m_{i})_{i\leq i\leq r}\mapsto(f_{1}(m_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq r},

while
\sigma_{K_{r}}(f)_{2}\!:=\!f_{1}
.

According to
[
2
, (VII.5.3(b))]
,
\sigma_{K_{r}}
is left exact, while
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}
is right exact and the functor
\sigma_{K_{r}}
induces an equivalence

\sigma_{K_{r}}:\rep_{2}(K_{r})\longrightarrow\rep_{1}(K_{r})

between the full subcategories
\rep_{i}(K_{r})
of
\rep(K_{r})
, whose objects don’t have any direct summands isomorphic to
S_{i}
. By the same token,
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}
is a quasi-inverse of
\sigma_{K_{r}}
.
Moreover,
\sigma_{K_{r}}
and
\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}
induce quasi-inverse equivalences on the full subcategory
\reg(K_{r})\subseteq\rep(K_{r})
of regular representations. We have
\sigma_{K_{r}}\circ\sigma_{K_{r}}\cong\tau_{K_{r}}
as well as
\sigma_{K_{r}}(P_{i+1}(r))\cong P_{i}(r)
for all
i\!\geq\!0
.

Recall that
\{\udim P_{i}(r)\ ;\ i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
consists exactly of those tuples
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{0}
that satisfy
a\!<\!b
and
q_{r}(a,b)\!=\!1
. Since all irreducible morphisms between preprojective representations in

\rep(K_{r})
are injective, it follows that
P_{i}(r)
is isomorphic to a subrepresentation of
P_{j}(r)
if and only if
i\!\leq\!j
. We
also note that

\udim\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)\!=\!(r\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2},\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})

for
M
indecomposable and not isomorphic to
P_{0}(r)
, while
\sigma_{K_{r}}(P_{0}(r))\!=\!(0)
. In conjunction with the left exactness of
\sigma_{K_{r}}
this implies that
\sigma_{K_{r}}\colon\rep_{2}(K_{r})\longrightarrow\rep_{1}(K_{r})
is exact. By the same token,
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}\colon\rep_{1}(K_{r})\longrightarrow\rep_{2}(K_{r})
is exact.

Lemma 2.2.2
.

Let
1\!\leq\!d\!<\!r
. We have

\{[\sigma_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}))]\ ;\ \mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\}=\{[\coker\mathfrak{w}]\ ;\ \mathfrak{w}\in\Gr_{r-d}(A_{r})\}.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. Since
E(\mathfrak{v})
is regular indecomposable, the same holds for
\sigma_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}))
. As
\udim\sigma_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}))\!=\!\udim\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(D_{K_{r}}(\coker\mathfrak{v}))\!=\!(1,d)
, it
follows from Proposition
2.1.3
(3) that
\sigma_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}))

\cong\coker\mathfrak{w}
for some
\mathfrak{w}\in\Gr_{r-d}(A_{r})
.

If
\mathfrak{w}\in\Gr_{r-d}(A_{r})
, then the preceding argument provides
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
such that
\sigma_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{w}))\cong\coker\mathfrak{v}
. We therefore obtain

\sigma_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}))\cong\sigma_{K_{r}}(D_{K_{r}}(\tau_{K_{r}}(\coker\mathfrak{v})))\cong D_{K_{r}}(\sigma_{K_{r}}(\coker\mathfrak{v}))\cong D_{K_{r}}(\tau_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{w})))\cong\coker\mathfrak{w}.\qed

Let
M,E\in\rep(K_{r})
with
E
regular. We claim that

(i)

\Hom_{K_{r}}(E,M)\cong\Hom_{K_{r}}(\sigma_{K_{r}}(E),\sigma_{K_{r}}(M))
, and

(ii)

\Hom_{K_{r}}(E,M)\cong\Hom_{K_{r}}(\tau_{K_{r}}(E),\tau_{K_{r}}(M))
.

In order to verify (i), we write
M\!=\!S_{2}^{l}\!\oplus\!N
, with
S_{2}
not being a direct summand of
N
. Since there are no non-zero homomorphisms from non-zero regular representations to
projective representations (see
[
2
, (VIII.2.13)]
), we have
\Hom_{K_{r}}(E,S_{2})\!=\!(0)
. Moreover,
\sigma_{K_{r}}(S_{2})\!=\!(0)
implies
\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)\cong\sigma_{K_{r}}(N)
and we conclude

\displaystyle\Hom_{K_{r}}(E,M)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Hom_{K_{r}}(E,S_{2}^{l})\!\oplus\!\Hom_{K_{r}}(E,N)\cong\Hom_{K_{r}}(\sigma_{K_{r}}(E),\sigma_{K_{r}}(N))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\Hom_{K_{r}}(\sigma_{K_{r}}(E),\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)).

Since
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{2}\cong\tau_{K_{r}}
, we also get (ii).

Theorem 2.2.3
.

Let
1\!\leq\!d\!<\!r
and
M\in\rep(K_{r})
.

(1)

M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
if and only if
\Hom_{K_{r}}(\coker\mathfrak{w},\sigma_{K_{r}}(M))\!=\!(0)
for all
\mathfrak{w}\in\Gr_{r-d}(A_{r})
.

(2)

M\in\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
if and only if
\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)\in\EKP(K_{r})
.

(3)

We have
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(\repp(K_{r},d))\subseteq\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
. In particular,
\repp(K_{r},d)
is
\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}
-stable.

Proof.

(1) This is a direct consequence of Theorem
2.1.5
, Lemma
2.2.2
, and (i).

(2) In view of (i), we have
M\in\rep(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
if and only if

(0)=\Hom_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}),M)\cong\Hom_{K_{r}}(\sigma_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v})),\sigma_{K_{r}}(M))\cong\Hom_{K_{r}}(D_{K_{r}}(\sigma_{K_{r}}(\coker\mathfrak{v})),\sigma_{K_{r}}(M))

for all
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{r-1}(A_{r})
. Since
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}D_{K_{r}}(\sigma_{K_{r}}(\coker\mathfrak{v}))=(1,r\!-\!1)
for all
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{r-1}(A_{r})
, the statement follows from Lemma
2.2.2
and Proposition
2.1.3
(3).

(3) Let
M\!=\!\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(N)
for some
N\in\repp(K_{r},d)
. Let
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. Since
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}),S_{1})\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}E(\mathfrak{v})_{1}\!\neq\!0
, it follows that
N\in\rep_{1}(K_{r})
.
Then Proposition
2.2.1
(3) in conjunction with the equivalence
\rep_{2}(K_{r})\longrightarrow\rep_{1}(K_{r})
yields
\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)\cong N\in\EKP(K_{r})
, so that (2) ensures that
M\in\rep(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
.
By Proposition
2.2.1
(3), this also implies that
\repp(K_{r},d)
is
\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}
-stable.∎

Example
.

In view of
\sigma_{K_{r}}(P_{i+1}(r))\cong P_{i}(r)
for all
i\!\geq\!0
, parts (2) and (3) of Theorem
2.2.3
imply inductively that
P_{i}(r)\in\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
for all
i\!\geq\!0
.

We finally record a topological property of the set of relative projective modules of fixed dimension vector. The relevance of the technical condition (2) will be clarified in the following section.

Proposition 2.2.4
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2}
be
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces,
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

The set
\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!=\!\{M\in\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\ ;\ \mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}\!=\!\emptyset\}
is open.

(2)

If
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d)\!\geq\!d(r\!-\!d)
, then
\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
lies dense in
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
.

Proof.

(1) We interpret
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
as
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})
. In view of Theorem
2.1.5
, the relevant set is given by

\mathcal{O}_{(r,d)}:=\{\psi\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\ ;\ \psi\circ(\alpha\!\otimes\id_{V_{1}})\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\ \ \ \forall\ \alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\}.

We consider the canonical map

\msim:\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\longrightarrow\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ \alpha\mapsto\im\alpha.

As noted before, this map defines a principal
\GL(A_{d})
-bundle, and hence so does

\kappa:\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ (\psi,\alpha)\mapsto(\psi,\im\alpha).

In particular,
\kappa
is an open morphism.

For
\psi\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})
and
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, we put
\psi_{\mathfrak{v}}\!:=\!\psi|_{\mathfrak{v}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}}
. We consider the sets

\mathcal{O}_{1}\!:=\!\{(\psi,\alpha)\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\ ;\ \rk(\psi\circ(\alpha\otimes\id_{V_{1}}))\!=\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\}

and

\mathcal{O}_{2}\!:=\!\{(\psi,\mathfrak{v})\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ \rk(\psi_{\mathfrak{v}})\!=\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\}.

Since
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\ ;\ (\psi,\alpha)\mapsto\psi\circ(\alpha\otimes\id_{V_{1}})
is a morphism, lower semicontinuity of ranks
ensures that
\mathcal{O}_{1}
is an open subset, so that

\mathcal{O}_{2}=\kappa(\mathcal{O}_{1})

is open as well. As a result,

\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}\!:=\!\{(\psi,\mathfrak{v})\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ \rk(\psi_{\mathfrak{v}})\!<\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\}.

is closed.

As the projective variety
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
is complete, the morphism
\pr:\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\ ;\ (\psi,\mathfrak{v})\mapsto\psi
is closed.
Consequently,

\mathcal{X}_{(r,d)}:=\pr(\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)})

is a closed subset of the affine space
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})
.

Suppose that
\psi\not\in\mathcal{O}_{(r,d)}
. Then there is
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
such that
\rk(\psi_{\mathfrak{v}})\!<\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}
, so that
(\psi,\mathfrak{v})\in\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}
. It follows that
\psi\in\mathcal{X}_{(r,d)}
. Conversely, if
\psi\in\mathcal{X}_{(r,d)}
,
then there is
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
such that
(\psi,\mathfrak{v})\in\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}
. Thus, there is
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
such that
\rk(\psi\circ(\alpha\otimes\id_{V_{1}}))\!<\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}
, whence
\psi\not\in\mathcal{O}_{(r,d)}
.

As an upshot of the above, we obtain that

\mathcal{O}_{(r,d)}=\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\smallsetminus\!\mathcal{X}_{(r,d)}

is open.

(2) In view of (1), it suffices to show that
\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\neq\!\emptyset
. Setting
n\!:=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}
and
m\!:=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{2}
, we define for
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,n\}

\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})_{\leq\ell}:=\{f\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})\ ;\ \rk(f)\!\leq\!\ell\}.

We proceed in several steps, beginning by recalling that (see
[
54
, Chap.IV,exerc. 3]
)

(i)
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})_{\leq\ell}
is a closed, irreducible subspace of dimension
\ell(n\!+\!m\!-\!\ell)
.

Next, we verify the following claim

(ii)
The closed subset
\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}
defined in (1) is an irreducible variety of dimension

\dim\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}=rmn\!-\!1\!-\!\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d)\!+\!d(r\!-\!d).

In what follows, we let
\ell\!:=\!dn\!-\!1
. The algebraic group
\mathbbm{k}^{\times}
acts on
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
via

\alpha\boldsymbol{.}(\psi,\mathfrak{v}):=(\alpha\psi,\mathfrak{v})\ \ \ \ \forall\ (\psi,\mathfrak{v})\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r}).

Note that
\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}
is
\mathbbm{k}^{\times}
-stable, so that every irreducible component
Z
of
\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}
enjoys the same property.

We consider the surjective morphism

q:\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}\longrightarrow\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ (\psi,\mathfrak{v})\mapsto\mathfrak{v}

as well as

\iota_{0}:\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ \mathfrak{v}\mapsto(0,\mathfrak{v})

Let
Z
be an irreducible component of
\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}
. Then
Z
is a closed subset of
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. If
\mathfrak{v}\in q(Z)
, then there is
\psi\in\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})
such that
(\psi,\mathfrak{v})\in Z
, and the
\mathbbm{k}^{\times}
-stability mentioned above implies
\iota_{0}(\mathfrak{v})\!=\!(0,\mathfrak{v})\in\overline{\mathbbm{k}^{\times}\boldsymbol{.}(\psi,\mathfrak{v})}\subseteq Z
. Consequently,
q(Z)\!=\!\iota_{0}^{-1}(Z)
is closed.

Let
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. Writing
A_{r}\!=\!\mathfrak{v}\!\oplus\!\mathfrak{w}
, we obtain

q^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})\cong\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})_{\leq\ell}\!\times\!\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathfrak{w}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2}),

so that (i) ensures that
q^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})
is irreducible of dimension

\dim q^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})=\ell(m\!+\!dn\!-\!\ell)\!+\!mn(r\!-\!d)=\ell(m\!+\!1)\!+\!mn(r\!-\!d).

In view of
[
28
, (1.5)]
, the variety
\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}
is irreducible and the fiber dimension theorem
[
52
, (§I.8, Thm.3)]
yields

\displaystyle\dim\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ell(m\!+\!1)\!+\!mn(r\!-\!d)\!+\!d(r\!-\!d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(dn\!-\!1)(m\!+\!1)\!+\!mn(r\!-\!d)\!+\!d(r\!-\!d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle dmn\!+\!\!dn\!-\!m\!-\!1\!+\!mn(r\!-\!d)\!+\!d(r\!-\!d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle rmn\!-\!1\!-\!\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d)\!+\!d(r\!-\!d),

as desired.
\diamond

By virtue of (ii) and our current assumption, the fiber dimension theorem implies

\dim\mathcal{X}_{(r,d)}\leq\dim\mathcal{C}_{(r,d)}\leq rmn\!-\!1<\dim\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1},V_{2}),

so that
\mathcal{O}_{(r,d)}\!=\!\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\!\smallsetminus\!\mathcal{X}_{(r,d)}\!\neq\!\emptyset
. Hence there is
M\in\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
such that
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}\!=\!\emptyset
. ∎

2.3.
Modules of minimal type and applications

It is a consequence of work of Westwick
[
63
]
that for “most” objects
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
the inequality

\Delta_{M}(1)\geq r\!-\!1

holds. As we shall show below, the following definition naturally generalizes those
\EKP
-representations for which we have equality.

Definition
.

Let
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. We say that
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
has
minimal type
, provided
\Delta_{M}(d)\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)
.

Our approach rests on the following technical Lemma:

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
. Then

C_{M}:=\{(m,\mathfrak{v})\in M_{2}\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ m\in\im\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\}\subseteq M_{2}\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})

is a closed, irreducible subset of dimension
\dim C_{M}\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)\!+\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}
.

Proof.

We proceed in several steps.

(a)
Let
f:V\longrightarrow W
be a
\mathbbm{k}
-linear map,
C\subseteq\Gr_{q}(V)
be closed such that
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}f(\mathfrak{v})\!=\!q
for all
\mathfrak{v}\in C
. Then

\msim_{f,C}:C\longrightarrow\Gr_{q}(W)\ \ ;\ \ \mathfrak{v}\mapsto f(\mathfrak{v})

is a morphism of projective varieties.

We denote by
\mspl_{W}:\Gr_{q}(W)\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}(\bigwedge^{q}(W))
the Plücker embedding and observe that
(\mspl_{W}\circ\msim_{f,C})(\mathfrak{v})\!=\!\bigwedge^{q}(f(\mathfrak{v}))\!=\!\bigwedge^{q}(f)(\bigwedge^{q}(\mathfrak{v}))
for every
\mathfrak{v}\in C
. The
map
\bigwedge^{q}(f):\bigwedge^{q}(V)\longrightarrow\bigwedge^{q}(W)
is
\mathbbm{k}
-linear and

O_{f}\!:=\!\{[x]\in\mathbb{P}(\bigwedge^{q}(V))\ ;\ \bigwedge^{q}(f)(x)\!\neq\!0\}
is open and such that

\varphi:O_{f}\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}(\bigwedge^{q}(W))\ ;\ [x]\mapsto[\bigwedge^{q}(f)(x)]

is a morphism. Since
\mspl_{V}(C)\subseteq O_{f}
, it follows that
\mspl_{W}\circ\msim_{f,C}\!=\!\varphi\circ\mspl_{V}|_{C}
is a morphism. Hence the same holds for
\msim_{f,C}
.
\diamond

(b)
The map

\zeta_{M}:\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\longrightarrow\Gr_{d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}(M_{2})\ \ ;\ \ \mathfrak{v}\mapsto\psi_{M}(\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1})

is a morphism.

We first consider the map

\eta_{M}:\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\longrightarrow\Gr_{d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1})\ \ ;\ \ \mathfrak{v}\mapsto\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}.

Then we have

(\mspl_{A_{r}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}\circ\eta_{M})(\mathfrak{v})=\bigwedge^{d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}(\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1})\cong\bigwedge^{d}(\mathfrak{v})\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\bigwedge^{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}(M_{1})\cong\mspl_{A_{r}}(\mathfrak{v})\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\bigwedge^{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}(M_{1}),

where the second to last isomorphism holds for dimension reasons. Hence
\mspl_{A_{r}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}\circ\eta_{M}
is a morphism, so that
\eta_{M}
enjoys the same property.

Consequently,
C\!:=\!\im\eta_{M}
is closed. Since
\zeta_{M}\!=\!\msim_{\psi_{M},C}\circ\eta_{M}
, our assertion follows from (a).
\diamond

The incidence variety
\mathcal{I}_{M}\!:=\!\{(m,\mathfrak{w})\in M_{2}\!\times\!\Gr_{d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}(M_{2})\ ;\ m\in\mathfrak{w}\}
is known to be closed. By (b), the map

\id_{M_{2}}\!\times\zeta_{M}:M_{2}\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\longrightarrow M_{2}\!\times\!\Gr_{d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}(M_{2})

is a morphism. Hence
C_{M}\!=\!(\id_{M_{2}}\!\times\zeta_{M})^{-1}(\mathcal{I}_{M})
is closed as well.

We consider the projection

\pr_{2}:C_{M}\longrightarrow\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ (m,\mathfrak{v})\mapsto\mathfrak{v}.

For every
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, the fiber
\pr_{2}^{-1}(\mathfrak{v})\!\cong\!\im\psi_{M,v}
is irreducible of dimension
d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}
. Note that
\mathbbm{k}^{\times}
acts on
C_{M}
via

\alpha.(m,\mathfrak{v})=(\alpha m,\mathfrak{v})\ \ \ \ \forall\ \alpha\in\mathbbm{k}^{\times},(m,\mathfrak{v})\in C_{M}.

It follows that every irreducible component
C\subseteq C_{M}
is
\mathbbm{k}^{\times}
-stable, so that
(m,\mathfrak{v})\in C
implies
(0,\mathfrak{v})\in C
. The morphism

\iota:\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\longrightarrow C_{M}\ \ ;\ \ \mathfrak{v}\mapsto(0,\mathfrak{v})

thus yields
\pr_{2}(C)\!=\!\iota^{-1}(C)
, showing that
\pr_{2}(C)
is closed. The irreducibility of
C_{M}
now follows from
[
28
, (1.5)]
, while its dimension is given by the fiber dimension theorem. ∎

For
M\in\rep(K_{r})
we put

I_{M}:=\bigcup_{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}\im\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\subseteq M_{2}.

Theorem 2.3.2
.

Let
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
. Then we have

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\psi_{M}\leq(r\!-\!d)(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!\min\{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1},d\}).

In particular,

\Delta_{M}(d)\geq(r\!-\!d)\min\{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1},d\}.

Proof.

We consider the representation

P(M)\!:=\!(M_{1},A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1},\id_{A_{r}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}).

Since
P(M)\cong(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(r)
is projective, Lemma
2.3.1
shows that the set

C_{P(M)}=\{(x,\mathfrak{v})\in(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1})\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ x\in\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}\}

is closed, irreducible and of dimension
\dim C_{P(M)}\!=\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!+\!d(r\!-\!d)
. The projection

\pr_{1}:C_{P(M)}\longrightarrow A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}

has image
I_{P(M)}\!=\!\bigcup_{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}\subseteq A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}
. Since
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
is complete and
C_{P(M)}
is closed and irreducible,
I_{P(M)}
is a closed, irreducible
subset of
A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M
. Moreover, given
x\in I_{P(M)}
, we have
\pr_{1}^{-1}(x)\cong\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ x\in\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}\}
.

Let
\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}\}
be a basis of
M_{1}
, so that
s\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}
. Every
x\in A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}
is of the form
x\!=\!\sum_{i=1}^{s}v_{i}(x)\otimes m_{i}
. We let
\mathfrak{c}(x)\!:=\!\langle v_{i}(x)\ ,\ i\in\{1,\ldots,s\}\rangle\subseteq A_{r}
be the coefficient space of
x
and put
\ell(x)\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathfrak{c}(x)
. Writing
v_{i}(x)\!:=\!\sum_{j=1}^{r}a_{ij}(x)\gamma_{j}
and
A(x)\!:=\!(a_{ij}(x))\in\Mat_{s\times r}(k)
, we obtain
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathfrak{c}(x)\!=\!\rk(A(x))
. Since
\rk(A(x))\!\leq\!\min\{s,d\}
for all
x\in I_{P(M)}
, it follows from
\sum_{i=1}^{\min\{s,d\}}\gamma_{i}\otimes m_{i}\in I_{P(M)}
and the lower semicontinuity of
x\mapsto\rk(A(x))
that

O_{M}:=\{x\in I_{P(M)}\ ;\ \ell(x)\!=\!\min\{s,d\}\}

is a dense open subset of
I_{P(M)}
.

Let
x\in O_{M}
. Then
(x,\mathfrak{v})\in\pr_{1}^{-1}(x)
if and only if
\mathfrak{c}(x)\subseteq\mathfrak{v}
. If
s\!\geq\!d
, then
\pr^{-1}_{1}(x)\!=\!\{(x\,\mathfrak{c}(x))\}
. Alternatively,
\pr_{1}^{-1}(x)\cong\Gr_{d-s}(A_{r}/\mathfrak{c}(x))
,
so that
\dim\pr^{-1}_{1}(x)\!=\!(d\!-\!s)(r\!-\!d)
. The fiber dimension theorem now shows that

\dim I_{P(M)}=ds\!+\!(r\!-\!d)\min\{s,d\}.

Note that
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
implies that
\ker\psi_{M}\cap I_{P(M)}\!=\{0\}
. The affine dimension theorem
[
36
, (7.1)]
now ensures that

0=\dim\ker\psi_{M}\cap I_{P(M)}\geq\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\psi_{M}\!+\!\dim I_{P(M)}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}),

whence

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\psi_{M}\leq rs\!-\!\dim I_{P(M)}=(r\!-\!d)(s\!-\!\min\{s,d\}).

Finally,
