\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\geq\rk(\psi_{M})=rs\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\psi_{M}\geq rs\!-\!(r\!-\!d)(s\!-\!\min\{s,d\})=ds\!+\!(r\!-\!d)\min\{s,d\},

so that
\Delta_{M}(d)\!\geq\!(r\!-\!d)\min\{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1},d\}
. ∎

Theorem
2.3.2
has been used by the first named author
[
10
]
to determine the orbit representatives of the dimension vectors of the elementary
K_{r}
-modules relative to the actions given by

\sigma_{K_{r}}
and the duality. This extends work by Ringel
[
57
]
concerning
r\!=\!3
, where module representatives were also identified. In this regard, the case
r\!=\!3
appears to be special.

We shall see later that the following result reflects the scarcity of indecomposable vector bundles
\mathcal{F}
on
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
of rank
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!<\!d(r\!-\!d)
.

Corollary 2.3.3
.

Let
M,N\in\repp(K_{r},d)
be representations.

(1)

Suppose that
\Delta_{M}(d)\!<\!d(r\!-\!d)
.

(a)

If
M
is indecomposable, then
M\cong P_{0}(r)
, or
d\!\neq\!1
and
M\!\cong P_{1}(r)
.

(b)

M\cong\Delta_{M}(r)P_{0}(r)\!\oplus\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(r)
is projective.

(2)

Suppose that
\Delta_{M}(d)\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)
.

(a)

If
M
is not projective, then every
f\in\Hom_{K_{r}}(M,N)\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\}
is injective.

(b)

M
is a brick or projective.

(c)

If
M
is not projective, then
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\geq\!d\!+\!1
.

(d)

If
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\geq\!d\!+\!1
, then
M
is not projective.

Proof.

(1a) Suppose first that
M\not\cong P_{0}(r)
is indecomposable. Then
\Delta_{M}(r)\!\leq\!0
and

(\ast)\ \ \ \ (0)\longrightarrow-\Delta_{M}(r)P_{0}(r)\longrightarrow(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(r)\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow(0)

is a projective resolution of
M
. In view of

(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})(r\!-\!d)=\Delta_{M}(d)\!-\!\Delta_{M}(r),

our current assumption in conjunction with Theorem
2.3.2
implies
d\!>\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}
and
\Delta_{M}(d)\!\geq\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})(r\!-\!d)
, whence
\Delta_{M}(r)\!\geq\!0
and
d\!\neq\!1
. Consequently,

\Delta_{M}(r)\!=\!0
, so that (
\ast
) and the indecomposability of
M
show that
M\cong P_{1}(r)
.

(1b) Since
\repp(K_{r},d)
is closed under taking subrepresentations, the assertion follows from the Theorem of Krull-Remak-Schmidt.

(2a) Let
f\in\Hom_{K_{r}}(M,N)\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\}
. Then the terms of the short exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow\ker f\longrightarrow M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}\im f\longrightarrow(0)

belong to
\repp(K_{r},d)
and

\Delta_{M}(d)=\Delta_{\ker f}(d)\!+\!\Delta_{\im f}(d).

If
\Delta_{\ker f}(d)\!\neq\!0
, then
\Delta_{\im f}(d)\!<\!d(r\!-\!d)
, so that (1) shows that
\im f
is projective. In view of Theorem
2.3.2
, the assumption
\Delta_{\im f}(d)\!=\!0
implies
(\im f)_{1}\!=\!(0)
, whence

0\!=\!-d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}(\im f)_{2}
, which contradicts
f\!\neq\!0
. Hence
\ker f
is also projective, and so is
M\cong\ker f\!\oplus\!\im f
. As
M
is not projective, we conclude that
\Delta_{\ker f}(d)\!=\!0
, whence

\ker f\!=\!(0)
.

(2b) Suppose that
M
is not projective and let
f\in\End_{K_{r}}(M)\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\}
. In view of (2a),
f
is bijective. Hence there is a non-zero eigenvalue
\alpha\in\mathbbm{k}
and another application of (2a) yields

f\!=\!\alpha\id_{M}
.

(2c) As
M
is a non-projective brick, the exact sequence (
\ast
) yields
\Delta_{M}(r)\!<\!0
and

(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})(r\!-\!d)=\Delta_{(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(r)}(d)>\Delta_{M}(d)=d(r\!-\!d),

so that
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\geq\!d\!+\!1
.

(2d) Suppose that
M
is projective. Then
M\cong\Delta_{M}(r)P_{0}(r)\!\oplus\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(r)
, and we have

0\leq\Delta_{M}(r)=\Delta_{M}(d)\!-\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})(r\!-\!d)\leq-(r\!-\!d),

a contradiction. Hence
M
is not projective. ∎

Corollary 2.3.4
.

Suppose that
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
is non-projective and of minimal type. Then
M\not\in\repp(K_{r},d\!+\!1)
.

Proof.

Since
M
is non-projective and of minimal type, Corollary
2.3.3
(2b),(2c) provides
x\!\geq\!d\!+\!1
such that
\udim M\!=\!(x,d(r\!-\!d\!+\!x))
. We thus have

(\ast)\ \ \ \ \Delta_{M}(d\!+\!1)=d(r\!-\!d)\!-\!x.

Suppose that
M\in\repp(K_{r},d\!+\!1)
. Since
x\!\geq\!d\!+\!1
, Theorem
2.3.2
in conjunction with (
\ast
) gives
d(r\!-\!d)\!-\!x\!\geq\!(d\!+\!1)(r\!-\!d\!-\!1)
, whence
d\!+\!1\!\leq\!x\!\leq\!2d\!+\!1\!-\!r
. As this
contradicts
d\!\leq\!r\!-\!1
, we conclude that
M\not\in\rep(K_{r},d\!+\!1)
. ∎

Remarks
.

(1) It follows that every regular
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
of minimal type is a brick.

(2) Let
(V_{1},V_{2})
be a pair of
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces. If
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d)\!\geq\!d(r\!-\!d)
, then Proposition
2.1.5
ensures that
\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
is not empty. Alternatively, this set is either empty
(namely, if and only if
\Delta_{M}(r)\!<\!0
), or it consists of one
(\GL(V_{2})\!\times\!\GL(V_{1}))
-orbit of projective representations.

Since the category
\repp(K_{r},d)
is closed under taking extensions (see Theorem
2.1.5
), our next result provides a first characterization of objects in
\repp(K_{r},d)
in terms of those of minimal type.

Proposition 2.3.5
.

Let
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
be such that
\Delta_{M}(d)\!>\!d(r\!-\!d)
. There exists an exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow aP_{0}(r)\longrightarrow M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}M_{\min}\longrightarrow(0),

where
M_{\min}
has minimal type and
a\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.3.1
, the set

C_{M}=\{(m,\mathfrak{v})\in M_{2}\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ m\in\im\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\}\subseteq M_{2}\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})

is a closed, irreducible subset of dimension
\dim C_{M}\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)\!+\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}
. Note that the morphism

\pr_{1}:C_{M}\longrightarrow M_{2}\ \ ;\ \ (m,\mathfrak{v})\mapsto m

has image
I_{M}\!=\!\bigcup_{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}\im\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\subseteq M_{2}
, so that
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
being complete implies that
I_{M}
is closed.
6
6

6

Observe that
\pr_{1}
is the restriction of the projection

M_{2}\!\times\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\longrightarrow M_{2}
, which is closed.
Moreover,
I_{M}
is irreducible, conical and of dimension

\dim I_{M}\leq d(r\!-\!d)\!+\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!<\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}.

The Noether Normalization Theorem thus implies the existence of a linear subspace
X\subseteq M_{2}
of dimension
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\dim I_{M}
and such that

X\cap I_{M}=\{0\},

cf.
[
47
, (II.3.14)]
. Since
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}X\!\geq\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!d(r\!-\!d)\!=\!\Delta_{M}(d)\!-\!d(r\!-\!d)\!>\!0
, we can find a subspace
(0)\subsetneq Y\subseteq X
such that

(i)

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}Y\!=\!\Delta_{M}(d)\!-\!d(r\!-\!d)
, and

(ii)

Y\cap I_{M}\!=\!\{0\}
.

We now consider
M_{\min}\!:=\!N\!:=\!(M_{1},M_{2}/Y)
together with the canonical projection
\pi_{2}:M_{2}\longrightarrow N_{2}
and put
\psi_{N}\!:=\!\pi_{2}\circ\psi_{M}
. Then
\pi\!:=\!(\id_{M_{1}},\pi_{2})
defines a surjective morphism

\pi:M\longrightarrow N
such that
\ker\pi\cong(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}Y)P_{0}(r)
. Given
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, (ii) yields
\im\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\cap Y\!=\!(0)
, whence

\ker\psi_{N,\mathfrak{v}}=\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}(Y)=(0).

Consequently,
M_{\min}\in\repp(K_{r},d)
, while (i) yields
\Delta_{M_{\min}}(d)\!=\!\Delta_{M}(d)\!-\!\Delta_{(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}Y)P_{0}(r)}(d)\!=\!\Delta_{M}(d)\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}Y\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)
. ∎

We turn to the problem of embedding
K_{r}
-representations of minimal type into
K_{r+s}
-representations of the same type.

Lemma 2.3.6
.

Given
M\in\rep(K_{r})
such that
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\geq\!d
, we have

I_{M}=\bigcup_{W\in\Gr_{d}(M_{1})}\psi_{M}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!W)\!=:\!J_{M}.

Proof.

Recall that
P(M)\!=\!(M_{1},A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1},\id_{A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}})
. We first show that
I_{P(M)}\!=\!J_{P(M)}
. Let
x\in I_{P(M)}
. Then there is
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
such that

x\in\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}
. Given a basis
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{d}\}\subseteq\mathfrak{v}
, we write
x\!=\!\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_{i}\otimes m_{i}
, so that there is
W\in\Gr_{d}(M_{1})
such that
\langle\{m_{1},\ldots,m_{d}\}\rangle\subseteq W
.
Consequently,
x\in A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!W
. As a result,
I_{P(M)}\subseteq J_{P(M)}
. The proof of the reverse inclusion is analogous.

For general
M
, we note that
I_{M}\!=\!\psi_{M}(I_{P(M)})\!=\!\psi_{M}(J_{P(M)})\!=\!J_{M}
. ∎

Proposition 2.3.7
.

Let
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
be of minimal type such that
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\geq\!d\!+\!1
. Then there exists
N\in\repp(K_{r+1},d)
of minimal type such that

N|_{K_{r}}\cong M\!\oplus\!dP_{0}(r).

Proof.

Writing
A_{r+1}\!=\!A_{r}\!\oplus\!\mathbbm{k}a
, we first construct
Q\in\repp(K_{r+1},d)
such that

Q|_{K_{r}}\cong M\!\oplus(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{0}(r).

We put
Q_{1}\!:=\!M_{1}
,
Q_{2}\!:=\!M_{2}\!\oplus\!Y_{2}
, with
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}Y_{2}\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}
and pick an isomorphism

\lambda:\mathbbm{k}a\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}Y_{2}.

Interpreting
\psi_{M}
and
\lambda
as elements of
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r+1}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!Q_{1},Q_{2})
, we have

(\ast)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \psi_{M}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!W)\cap\lambda(\mathbbm{k}a\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!W)=(0)\ \ \text{for all}\ \ W\in\Gr_{d}(M_{1}).

We consider the representation
Q\!:=\!(M_{1},Q_{2},\psi_{M}\!+\!\lambda)\in\rep(K_{r+1})
. Let
x\in\ker\psi_{Q}\cap I_{P(Q)}
. Lemma
2.3.6
provides
W\in\Gr_{d}(M_{1})
such that

x\in\ker\psi_{Q}\cap(A_{r+1}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}W)
. Writing
x\!=\!y\!+\!z
, with
y\in A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!W
and
z\in\mathbbm{k}a\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!W
we obtain
\lambda(z)\!=\!-\psi_{M}(y)\in\psi_{M}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!W)\cap\lambda(ka\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!W)
, so that (
\ast
) implies
\lambda(z)\!=\!0\!=\!\psi_{M}(y)
. As
\lambda
is injective, we conclude that
z\!=\!0
, while Lemma
2.3.6
in conjunction with
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
yields

y\in\ker\psi_{M}\cap J_{P(M)}\!=\!\ker\psi_{M}\cap I_{P(M)}\!=\!(0)
. As a result,
x\!=\!0
, so that
Q\in\repp(K_{r+1},d)
. By construction, we have
Q|_{K_{r}}\cong M\!\oplus(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{0}(r)
.

Since
\Delta_{Q}(d)\!=\!\Delta_{M}(d)\!+\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!d\!+\!d(r\!+\!1\!-\!d)
and
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!>\!d
, Proposition
2.3.5
provides a short exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!d)P_{0}(r\!+\!1)\longrightarrow Q\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\longrightarrow}}Q_{\min}\longrightarrow(0),

where
N\!:=\!Q_{\min}
has minimal type. Let
\iota:M\longrightarrow Q|_{K_{r}}
be the given injection. Since
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M\!\geq\!d\!+\!1
, Corollary
2.3.3
(2d) ensures that
M
is not projective. As
\ker\pi|_{K_{r}}\cong(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!d)P_{0}(r)
, the map
\pi\circ\iota:M\longrightarrow N|_{K_{r}}
is not zero, and hence by Corollary
2.3.3
(2a) injective. The restriction of the sequence above yields

(0)\longrightarrow(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!d)P_{0}(r)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\binom{\alpha}{\beta}}}{{\longrightarrow}}(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{0}(r)\!\oplus\!M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\zeta,\pi\circ\iota)}}{{\longrightarrow}}N|_{K_{r}}\longrightarrow(0),

and thanks to
[
4
, (I.5.6)]
there results a push-out and pull-back diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!d)P_{0}(r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{0}(r)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N|_{K_{r}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\zeta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi\circ\iota}

Since
\pi\circ\iota
is injective, so is
\alpha
. It follows that
dP_{0}(r)\cong\coker\alpha\cong\coker(\pi\circ\iota)
(cf.
[
4
, (I.5.6)]
), so that there is an exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi\circ\iota}}{{\longrightarrow}}N|_{K_{r}}\longrightarrow dP_{0}(r)\longrightarrow(0).

Consequently,
N|_{K_{r}}\cong M\!\oplus\!dP_{0}(r)
. ∎

Remark
.

Suppose that
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
is projective and of minimal type. Since
\Delta_{P_{1}(r)}(d)\!=\!(r\!-\!d)
, there is
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,d\}
such that
M\cong\ell P_{1}(r)\!\oplus\!(d\!-\!\ell)(r\!-\!d)P_{0}(r)
.
We consider
N\!:=\ell P_{1}(r\!+\!1)\!\oplus\!(d\!-\!\ell)(r\!+\!1\!-\!d)P_{0}(r\!+\!1)\in\repp(K_{r+1},d)
. Then we have

\Delta_{N}(d)=\ell(r\!+\!1\!-\!d)\!+\!(d\!-\!\ell)(r\!+\!1\!-\!d)=d(r\!+\!1\!-\!d),

so that
N
has minimal type. Moreover,

N|_{K_{r}}\cong\ell P_{1}(r)\!\oplus\!\ell P_{0}(r)\!\oplus\!(d\!-\!\ell)(r\!+\!1\!-\!d)P_{0}(r),

showing that
N|_{K_{r}}\cong M\!\oplus\!dP_{0}(r)
. By the remark at the beginning of Section
2.2
, Proposition
2.3.7
thus also holds in case
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\leq\!d
.

2.4.
Examples: Representations and hyperplane arrangements

The representations of
\EKP(K_{r})\!=\!\repp(K_{r},1)
discussed in this section correspond to the logarithmic bundles
[
23
]
and
Schwarzenberger bundles
[
60
]
. Our notation is meant to reflect this relationship

Let
r\!\geq\!2
,
V
be an
r
-dimensional vector space and
m\!\geq\!r
. We say that
\mathfrak{f}\!:=\!(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})\in(V^{\ast})^{m}
is in
general position
, provided
V^{\ast}\!=\!\langle f_{i}\ ;\ i\in J\rangle
for every subset
J\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
such that
|J|\!=\!r
. We consider the projective space
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}\!=\!\mathbb{P}(V)
. A
hyperplane arrangement
of
V
is an
m
-tuple
\mathcal{H}\!:=\!(H_{1},\ldots,H_{m})
of linear hyperplanes of

\mathbb{P}(V)
. We say that
\mathcal{H}
is
in general position
, provided for every
\ell\!\leq\!r
and every
\ell
-element subset
J_{\ell}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,m\}
, we have
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\bigcap_{i\in J_{\ell}}H_{i}\!=\!r\!-\!1\!-\!\ell
.
(Here we put
\dim\emptyset\!=\!-1
.) By definition, each
H_{i}\>=\!Z(f_{i})
is the set of zeros for some
f_{i}\in V^{\ast}\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\}
. It follows that
\mathcal{H}
is in general position if and only if
(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})
enjoys this
property.

Let
\mathfrak{f}\!:=\!(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})\in(V^{\ast})^{m}
. Following
[
23
, §1]
, we set

I_{\mathfrak{f}}\!:=\{\lambda\in\mathbbm{k}^{m}\ ;\ \sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}f_{i}\!=\!0\}\ \ \text{and}\ \ W_{m}\!:=\!\{\lambda\in\mathbbm{k}^{m}\ ;\ \sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}\!=\!0\},

and refer to the linear map

t_{\mathfrak{f}}:V\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!I_{\mathfrak{f}}\longrightarrow W_{m}\ \ ;\ \ v\otimes\lambda\mapsto(\lambda_{1}f_{1}(v),\ldots,\lambda_{m}f_{m}(v))

as the
fundamental tensor
of
\mathfrak{f}
(viewed as an element of
V^{\ast}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!I_{\mathfrak{f}}^{\ast}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!W_{m}
).

Given
\mathfrak{f}\!:=\!(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})\in(A_{r}^{\ast})^{m}
, we define
Q_{\mathfrak{f}}\!:=\!(\mathbbm{k}^{m},\mathbbm{k}^{m},(Q_{\mathfrak{f}}(\gamma_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq r})\in\rep(K_{r})
via

Q_{\mathfrak{f}}(\gamma_{i})(\lambda)=(\lambda_{1}f_{1}(\gamma_{i}),\ldots,\lambda_{m}f_{m}(\gamma_{i}))\ \ \ \ \ \forall\ i\in\{1,\ldots,r\},\lambda\in\mathbbm{k}^{m}.

Then, setting
V\!:=\!A_{r}
, we see that

M_{\mathfrak{f}}\!:=\!(I_{\mathfrak{f}},W_{m},(Q_{\mathfrak{f}}(\gamma_{i})|_{I_{\mathfrak{f}}})_{1\leq i\leq r})

is a subrepresentation of
Q_{\mathfrak{f}}
such that
\psi_{M_{\mathfrak{f}}}\!=\!t_{\mathfrak{f}}
. If
\mathcal{H}
is a hyperplane arrangement given by
\mathfrak{f}\in((A_{r})^{\ast}\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\})^{m}
, we abuse notation and write
M_{\mathcal{H}}\!=\!M_{\mathfrak{f}}

as well as
t_{\mathcal{H}}\!=\!t_{\mathfrak{f}}
.

Lemma 2.4.1
.

Suppose that
m\!\geq\!r\!\geq\!3
, and let
\mathcal{H}\!=\!(H_{1},\ldots,H_{m})
be a hyperplane arrangement in general position.

(1)

M_{\mathcal{H}}\in\EKP(K_{r})
has dimension vector
\udim M_{\mathcal{H}}\!=\!(m\!-\!r,m\!-\!1)
. In particular,
M_{\mathcal{H}}
has minimal type.

(2)

If
m\!\geq\!r\!+\!2
, then
M_{\mathcal{H}}\not\in\repp(K_{r},2)
.

Proof.

(1) Let
a\in A_{r}\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\}
. Then we have

a_{M_{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)=\psi_{M_{\mathcal{H}}}(a\otimes\lambda)=t_{\mathcal{H}}(a\!\otimes\lambda)

for all
\lambda\in I_{\mathfrak{f}}
. We may now apply
[
23
, (1.5)]
(which holds for arbitrary ground fields) to see that the linear map
a_{M_{\mathcal{H}}}
is injective. Consequently,
M_{\mathcal{H}}\in\EKP(K_{r})
.

We write
\mathcal{H}\!:=\!(Z(f_{1}),\ldots,Z(f_{m}))
,
\mathfrak{f}\!:=\!(f_{1},\ldots,f_{m})
. By construction, we have
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}(M_{\mathcal{H}})_{2}\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}W_{m}\!=\!m\!-\!1
. Since
m\!\geq\!r
and
\mathcal{H}
is in general position, we have

A_{r}^{\ast}\!=\!\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\rangle
, so that
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{r}\}\subseteq A_{r}^{\ast}
is a basis of
A_{r}^{\ast}
. Hence the linear map

\alpha_{\mathfrak{f}}:\mathbbm{k}^{m}\longrightarrow A_{r}^{\ast}\ \ ;\ \ \lambda\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{m}\lambda_{i}f_{i}

is surjective, whence
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}I_{\mathfrak{f}}\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\alpha_{\mathfrak{f}}\!=\!m\!-\!r
.

(2) This is a direct consequence of (1), Corollary
2.3.3
(2d) and Corollary
2.3.4
. ∎

A subclass of the modules
M_{\mathcal{H}}
considered above is given by the family
(M_{\mathcal{S}}[m])_{m\geq r+1}
of
Schwarzenberger modules
. Let
X_{1},X_{2}
be indeterminates over
\mathbbm{k}
. For
m\!\geq\!r\!+\!1
, we consider the
representation
M_{\mathcal{S}}[m]\in\rep(K_{r})
, defined via

M_{\mathcal{S}}[m]_{1}:=\mathbbm{k}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{m-r-1}\ \ ;\ \ M_{\mathcal{S}}[m]_{2}:=\mathbbm{k}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{m-2}\ \ ;\ \ M_{\mathcal{S}}[m](\gamma_{i})(a):=X_{1}^{i-1}X_{2}^{r-i}a\ \ \ \forall\ a\in M[m]_{1}

for
1\!\leq\!i\!\leq\!r
. Then we have
M_{\mathcal{S}}[m]\in\EKP(K_{r})
, while
\udim M_{\mathcal{S}}[m]\!=\!(m\!-\!r,m\!-\!1)
. The modules
M_{\mathcal{S}}[m]
turn out to correspond to vector bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
that were introduced by
Schwarzenberger, cf.
[
60
]
.

2.5.
The generic canonical decomposition

Our first result provides the
canonical decomposition

[
42
, p.85]
of certain dimension vectors. For ease of notation, we write

\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!:=\!\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(1)
.

Suppose that
V_{1},V_{2}
are vector spaces such that
\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\neq\!\emptyset
. According to Theorem
2.3.2
, the assumption
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d)\!=\!0
implies

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\!=\!0
and hence
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{2}\!=\!\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d)\!=\!0
. Hence the technical condition
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!\geq\!1
of the ensuing results only excludes the trivial case.

Lemma 2.5.1
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2}
be vector spaces such that
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!\geq\!1
and write
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\!=\!j\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!+\!b
for
b\in\{0,\ldots,\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!1\}
.
Then the set

\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})_{0}:=\{M\in\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})\ ;\ M\cong(\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!b)P_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!bP_{j+1}(2)\}

is
(\GL(V_{2})\!\times\!\GL(V_{1}))
-stable, dense and open.

Proof.

By definition,
\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})_{0}
is
(\GL(V_{2})\!\times\!\GL(V_{1}))
-stable.

Since
P_{j}(2),P_{j+1}(2)
are bricks, Corollary
1.2.3
implies that these representations belong to their respective open sheets. By virtue of
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{i}\!=\!(j\!+\!i\!-\!1)\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!+\!b
and

\Ext^{1}_{K_{2}}(P_{\ell}(2),P_{k}(2))\!=\!(0)
for
\{\ell,k\}\!=\!\{j,j\!+\!1\}
, the conditions of
[
43
, Prop.3(a)]
are met, so that our assertion follows from Kac’s Theorem. ∎

Let
r\!\geq\!3
. We consider the map

\Res:\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\longrightarrow\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})\ \ ;\ \ (M,\alpha)\mapsto\alpha^{\ast}(M).

Proposition 2.5.2
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2}
be
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces such that
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!\geq\!1
, and write

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}=j\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!+\!b

for some
b\in\{0,\ldots,\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!1\}
. The following statements hold:

(1)

\Res
is a morphism.

(2)

The set
\Res((\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2}))\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r}))
is open.

(3)

Suppose that
\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\neq\!\emptyset
. Then

U:=\{(M,\alpha)\in\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\ ;\ \alpha^{\ast}(M)\cong(\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!b)P_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!bP_{j+1}(2)\}

is a dense, open subset of
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
.

Proof.

(1) We interpret
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
as
\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r},\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2}))
, so that

\Res(\varrho,\alpha)=\varrho\circ\alpha

corresponds to matrix multiplication. Consequently,
\Res
is a morphism.

(2) Note that

\Res((\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2}))\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r}))=\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})}\alpha^{\ast}(\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})).

Given
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
, we write
A_{r}\!=\!\im\alpha\!\oplus\!\mathfrak{w}\cong A_{2}\!\oplus\!\mathfrak{w}
for some
\mathfrak{w}\in\Gr_{r-2}(A_{r})
. Then we have

\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r},\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2}))\cong\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2}))\!\oplus\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathfrak{w},\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(V_{1},V_{2})),

and
\alpha^{\ast}
is the projection onto the first summand.

For varieties
X,Y,
the projection
\pr_{X}:X\!\times\!Y\longrightarrow X
is easily seen to be open. We apply this to the situation above to see that
\alpha^{\ast}:\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\longrightarrow\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})
is open.
Since Proposition
2.2.4
ensures that
\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
is open, our assertion follows.

(3) In view of (2) and Lemma
2.5.1
, our current assumption implies that

\Res((\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2}))\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r}))\cap\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})_{0}

is a non-empty, open subset of
\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})
. By (1),

U=\Res^{-1}(\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})_{0})

is thus a non-empty, open subset of
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
. Being an open subset of the irreducible variety
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
, the variety
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
is irreducible, so that
U
lies dense in
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
. ∎

Recall the notion of the generic decomposition
M_{\gen}
of
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
, which we have discussed at the end of Section
1.3
.

Corollary 2.5.3
.

Suppose that
V_{1},V_{2}
are
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces such that
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!\geq\!1
and
\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\neq\!\emptyset
. Let
(j,b)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\!\times\!\{0,\ldots,\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!1\}
be given by
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\!=\!j\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!+\!b
. Then

O_{\gen}:=\{M\in\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\ ;\ M_{\gen}\!=\!(\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!b)P_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!bP_{j+1}(2)\}

is a dense open subset of
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
.

Proof.

According to Proposition
2.5.2

\displaystyle\{(M,\alpha)\in\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\ ;\ \alpha^{\ast}(M)\!

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\!(\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!b)P_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!bP_{j+1}(2)\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle U=\Res^{-1}(\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})_{0})

is a dense, open subset of
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
. The projection
\pr_{1}:\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\longrightarrow\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
is open, so that
\pr_{1}(U)
is open and
dense in
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
. In view of Proposition
2.2.4
(1), the subset
\pr_{1}(U)\cap\EKP(K_{r})
enjoys the same properties.

If
M\in O_{\gen}
, then there is
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
such that

\alpha^{\ast}(M)\cong(\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!b)P_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!bP_{j+1}(2),

whence
(M,\alpha)\in U
and
M\in\pr_{1}(U)\cap\EKP(K_{r})
.

Conversely, let
M\in\pr_{1}(U)\cap\EKP(K_{r})
. Then there is
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
such that

(\ast)\ \ \ \ \alpha^{\ast}(M)\cong(\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!b)P_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!bP_{j+1}(2).

Let
\pi:\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})\longrightarrow\Iso(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})
be the canonical projection. Thanks to Lemma
2.5.1
,
\Iso(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})_{0}\!:=\!\pi(\rep(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})_{0})
is a non-empty open subset of
\Iso(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})
.
Owing to (
\ast
), Proposition
1.3.2
implies that

U_{M}\!:=\!\res_{M}^{-1}(\Iso(K_{2};V_{1},V_{2})_{0})=\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{2}(A_{r})\ ;\ [M|_{\mathfrak{v}}]\!=\![(\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!b)P_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!bP_{j+1}(2)]\}

is a dense open subset of
\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
. In view of Corollary
1.3.3
,
U_{M}
therefore intersects the set

O_{M}:=\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{2}(A_{r})\ ;\ n_{i}(M,\mathfrak{v})=n_{i}(M)\ \ \ \forall\ i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}.

Consequently,

n_{i}(M)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0&i\not\in\{j,j\!+\!1\}\\
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{1})}\!-\!b&i\!=\!j\\
b&i\!=\!j\!+\!1,\end{array}\right.

so that
M\in O_{\gen}
. It follows that
O_{\gen}\!=\!\pr_{1}(U)\cap\EKP(K_{r})
has the asserted properties. ∎

Corollary 2.5.4
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2}
be
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces such that
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!\geq\!\max\{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1},r\!-\!1\}
. Then we have

O_{\gen}=\{M\in\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\ ;\ M_{\gen}\!=\!(\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1})P_{0}(2)\!\oplus\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1})P_{1}(2)\}.

Proof.

Since
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!\geq\!r\!-\!1
, Proposition
2.2.4
shows that
\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\neq\!\emptyset
. If
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!\geq\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\!+\!1
, we obtain

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}=0\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!+\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1},

where
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\leq\!\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!1
. The result thus follows from Corollary
2.5.3
. Alternatively,
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\!=\!\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}
and Corollary
2.5.3
implies

M_{\gen}\!=\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1})P_{1}(2)
. ∎

Corollary 2.5.5
.

Let
V_{1},V_{2}
be
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces such that
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!\geq\!1
,
M\in\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
. If there exist
\mathfrak{v}_{0}\in\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
and
c,d,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

[M|_{\mathfrak{v}_{0}}]=cP_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!dP_{j+1}(2),

then
M_{\gen}\!=\!cP_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!dP_{j+1}(2)
and
M\in O_{\gen}
.

Proof.

By assumption, we have
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!=\!\Delta_{M}\!=\!c\Delta_{P_{j}(2)}\!+\!d\Delta_{P_{j+1}(2)}\!=\!c\!+\!d
, while
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\!=\!cj\!+\!d(j\!+\!1)\!=\!j\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!+\!d
. If
c\!\neq\!0
,
then
0\!\leq\!d\!\leq\!\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}\!-\!1
and Proposition
2.5.2
(3) implies that

U:=\{(N,\alpha)\in\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\ ;\ \alpha^{\ast}(N)\cong cP_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!dP_{j+1}(2)\}

is a dense open subset of
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
. Alternatively,
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\!=\!(j\!+\!1)\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}
and we arrive at the same conclusion. In view of Proposition
2.2.4
(1),
we conclude that

\tilde{U}:=U\cap((\EKP(K_{r})\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2}))\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r}))

is a dense open subset of
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
.

Since

\iota_{M}:\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\longrightarrow\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!\times\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ \alpha\mapsto(M,\alpha)

is continuous, our current assumption ensures that
\tilde{O}\!:=\!\iota_{M}^{-1}(\tilde{U})
is a dense open subset of
\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
. As the morphism
\msim:\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})\longrightarrow\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
is open
(see the proof of Proposition
1.3.2
),

\msim(\tilde{O})\!=\!\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{2}(A_{r})\ ,\ M|_{\mathfrak{v}}\cong cP_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!dP_{j+1}(2)\}

is a dense open subset of
\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
and Corollary
1.3.3
gives

M_{\gen}=cP_{j}(2)\!\oplus\!dP_{j+1}(2).

As a result,
M\in O_{\gen}
. ∎

By way of example, we briefly discuss Schwarzenberger modules. Our result and its succeeding remark will provide the generic splitting type of the associated vector bundles and
show that these are
\GL(A_{2})
-homogeneous, but not homogeneous (cf. Section
5.1
below).

Given
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we consider the Schwarzenberger module
M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r]
of dimension vector
\udim M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r]\!=\!(\ell,\ell\!+\!r\!-\!1)
. Then we have
(M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r])_{1}\!=\!\mathbbm{k}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{\ell-1}
and

(M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r])_{2}\!=\!\mathbbm{k}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{\ell+r-2}
. Note that these spaces are
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
-graded. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r](\gamma_{i})(f)\!=\!X_{i}^{r-1}f
for
i\in\{1,2\}
and
f\in\mathbbm{k}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{\ell-1}
. In particular, we have
\deg(M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r](\gamma_{1}))\!=\!(r\!-\!1,0)
and
\deg(M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r](\gamma_{2}))\!=\!(0,r\!-\!1)
.

Corollary 2.5.6
.

If
\ell\!-\!1\!=\!a_{\ell}(r\!-\!1)\!+\!q_{\ell}
for
q_{\ell}\in\{0,\ldots,r\!-\!2\}
, then

M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r]_{\gen}=(r\!-\!2\!-\!q_{\ell})P_{a_{\ell}}(2)\!\oplus\!(q_{\ell}\!+\!1)P_{a_{\ell}+1}(2).

In particular,
M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r]\in O_{\gen}
.

Proof.

Let
\mathfrak{v}_{0}\!:=\!\mathbbm{k}\gamma_{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbbm{k}\gamma_{2}
. Writing
M\!:=\!M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r]
, we shall first find the decomposition of
M|_{\mathfrak{v}_{0}}
.

Given
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
, we write
X^{(a,b)}\!:=\!X_{1}^{a}X_{2}^{b}
. For
q\in\{0,\ldots,r\!-\!2\}
we consider the vector spaces

M_{1,q}:=\langle\{X^{(a,b)}\in\mathbbm{k}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{\ell-1}\ ;\ a\equiv q\ \modd(r\!-\!1)\}\rangle

as well as

M_{2,q}:=\langle\{X^{(a,b)}\in\mathbbm{k}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{\ell+r-2}\ ;\ a\equiv q\ \modd(r\!-\!1)\}\rangle.

Then we have
M(\gamma_{i})(M_{1,q})\subseteq M_{2,q}
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Defining
M_{q}\!:=\!(M_{1,q},M_{2,q})
, we obtain a decomposition

(\ast)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ M|_{\mathfrak{v}_{0}}=\bigoplus_{q=0}^{r-2}M_{q}

of
\mathbbm{k}.\mathfrak{v}_{0}
-modules. Moreover, setting
t_{q}\!:=\!|\{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\ ;\ q\!+\!n(r\!-\!1)\!\leq\!\ell\!-\!1\}|
, we have
\udim M_{q}\!=\!(t_{q},t_{q}\!+\!1)
, so that
M_{q}\in\EKP(K_{2})
is of minimal type. Since
\EKP(K_{2})
is
closed under taking direct summands and
\Delta_{X}\!>\!0
for all
X\in\EKP(K_{2})\!\smallsetminus\!\{(0)\}
, this renders
M_{q}
indecomposable. We conclude
M_{q}\!\cong\!P_{t_{q}}(2)
, so that (
\ast
) actually is the
decomposition of the
\mathbbm{k}K_{2}
-module
M|_{\mathfrak{v}_{0}}
into indecomposables. For
0\!\leq\!q\!\leq\!q_{\ell}
, we have
t_{q}\!=\!a_{\ell}\!+\!1
, while
t_{q}\!:=\!a_{\ell}
for
q_{\ell}\!+\!1\!\leq\!q\!\leq\!r\!-\!2
. As a result, (
\ast
) yields

M|_{\mathfrak{v}_{0}}\cong(r\!-\!2\!-\!q_{\ell})P_{a_{\ell}}(2)\!\oplus\!(q_{\ell}\!+\!1)P_{a_{\ell}+1}(2).

Corollary
2.5.5
implies

(M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r])_{\gen}=(r\!-\!2\!-\!q_{\ell})P_{a_{\ell}}(2)\!\oplus\!(q_{\ell}\!+\!1)P_{a_{\ell}+1}(2),

and
M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r]\in O_{\gen}
. ∎

Remark
.

Let
M\!:=\!M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell\!+\!r]
and consider

\mathfrak{v}_{1}:=\mathbbm{k}\gamma_{1}\!\oplus\!\mathbbm{k}\gamma_{3},

where

M(\gamma_{1})(X_{1}^{a}X_{2}^{b})=X_{1}^{a+r-1}X_{2}^{b}\ \ ;\ \ M(\gamma_{3})(X_{1}^{a}X_{2}^{b})=X_{1}^{a+r-2}X_{2}^{b+1}\ \ \ \ (a\!+\!b\!=\!\ell\!-\!1).

Then
(\id_{\mathbbm{k}[X_{1},X_{2}]_{\ell-1}},X_{1}^{r-2}\cdot)
defines an embedding
P_{\ell}(2)\hookrightarrow M|_{\mathfrak{v}_{1}}
such that

M|_{\mathfrak{v}_{1}}\cong(r\!-\!2)P_{0}(2)\!\oplus\!P_{\ell}(2).

Thus, for
\ell\!\geq\!2
, the dense open subset
O_{M}
defined in Corollary
1.3.3
is properly contained in
\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
.

3.
Coherent sheaves on
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
and representations of
K_{r}

This section is concerned with the equivalence of the categories
\repp(K_{r},d)
of relative projective representations and Steiner bundles on the Grassmannians
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
along with some consequences thereof.
Our account builds on work by Jardim-Prata
[
40
]
, where vector bundles over fields of characteristic
0
were considered. For fields of characteristic
p\!>\!0
, Friedlander-Pevtsova
[
32
]
earlier employed
universal nilpotent operators to define functors between module categories of infinitesimal group schemes and vector bundles on their support spaces. In the context of elementary abelian
p
-groups (or equivalently
elementary abelian restricted Lie algebras), the two approaches are related via the functorial correspondence between Kronecker representations and modules of Loewy length
\leq\!2
.

3.1.
Conventions

Let
r\!\geq\!2
,
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. We denote by
\Coh(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
and
\Vect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
the categories of coherent sheaves and vector bundles (locally free coherent
sheaves) on
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, respectively. Note that
\Coh(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is an abelian category, cf.
[
34
, (7.4)]
. Let
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}
be the structure sheaf of
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, so that every
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is
an
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}
-module. In view of
[
3
]
, the category
\Vect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is a Krull-Schmidt category. Thus, the indecomposable objects of any full subcategory
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Vect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
that is closed
under taking direct summands are just the indecomposable vector bundles belonging to
\mathcal{C}
.

Given
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, we let
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r}),\mathfrak{v}}
be the local ring of
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
at
\mathfrak{v}
, whose maximal ideal we denote by
\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{v}}
. Let
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
. For
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, the
stalk

\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{v}}
of
\mathcal{F}
at
\mathfrak{v}
is an
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r}),\mathfrak{v}}
-module, and the finite-dimensional
\mathbbm{k}
-vector space
\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{v})\!=\!\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{v}}/\mathfrak{m}_{\mathfrak{v}}\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{v}}
is called the
fiber
of
\mathcal{F}
at
\mathfrak{v}
. If
\mathcal{F}\in\Vect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
, then
\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{v}}
is a free
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r}),\mathfrak{v}}
-module of rank
\rk(\mathcal{F}_{\mathfrak{v}})\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathcal{F}(\mathfrak{v})
.

If
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in\Coh(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
are sheaves, we let
\msHom_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
be the sheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}
-homomor- phisms from
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{G}
. By definition, we have
\msHom_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))\!=\!\Hom_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
, the space of homomorphisms from
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{G}
, cf.
[
34
, §7.4]
. For
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we let

\Ext^{i}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},-):\Coh(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))\longrightarrow\modd\mathbbm{k}

be the
i
-th right derived functor of
\Hom_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},-)\cong\Ext^{0}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},-)
. Setting

\HH^{i}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}),\mathcal{F}):=\Ext^{i}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})},\mathcal{F}),

we recall that
\Ext^{i}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\cong\HH^{i}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}),\msHom_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}))
for every
\mathcal{G}\in\Coh(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
.

We denote by

\chi(\mathcal{F})=\sum_{i=0}^{d(r-d)}(-1)^{i}\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\HH^{i}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}),\mathcal{F})

the
Euler characteristic
of
\mathcal{F}
(see
[
36
, (III.2.7)]
). We refer the reader to
[
36
, (III.6)]
for more details.

The following subsidiary result for “special exceptional pairs“ of coherent sheaves on an arbitrary variety
X
is inspired by
[
13
, (3.1)]
. We begin by recalling the relevant terminology:

A vector bundle
\mathcal{F}\in\Vect(X)
is referred to as
exceptional
if
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{i}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\!=\!\delta_{i,0}
for all
i\!\geq\!0
. We say that a pair
(E_{0},E_{1})
of coherent sheaves on
X
is
special
exceptional
, provided

(a)

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{n}_{X}(E_{i},E_{i})\!=\!\delta_{n,0}
for all
n\!\geq\!0
,
i\in\{0,1\}
.

(b)

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{n}_{X}(E_{0},E_{1})\!=\!0
for all
n\!\geq\!1
.

(c)

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{n}_{X}(E_{1},E_{0})\!=\!0
for all
n\!\geq\!0
.
7
7

7

Our choice of terminology derives from Rudakov’s more general notion of an exceptional pair, cf.
[
58
]
. Pairs satisfying the above conditions are
referred to as “strongly exceptional” in
[
1
]
, which differs from the definition employed in
[
15
]
.

We put
r\!:=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{X}(E_{0},E_{1})
. Suppose there are exact sequences

(\ast)\ \ \ \ (0)\longrightarrow E_{0}^{m}\longrightarrow E_{1}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow(0)

and

(\ast\ast)\ \ \ (0)\longrightarrow E_{0}^{s}\longrightarrow E_{1}^{t}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow(0).

Recall the from Section
1.1
the definition of the Euler-Ringel bilinear form:

\langle(x_{1},y_{1}),(y_{1},y_{2})\rangle_{r}=x_{1}y_{1}\!+\!x_{2}y_{2}\!-\!rx_{1}y_{2}.

Proposition 3.1.1
.

We have

\chi(\msHom_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}))=\!\langle(m,n),(s,t)\rangle_{r}.

Proof.

We proceed in several steps:

(i)
We have an exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow\Hom_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{1}^{t})\longrightarrow\Hom_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{1}^{t})\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow(0).

The assertion follows by applying
\Hom_{X}(\mathcal{F},-)
to (
\ast\ast
), once we know that
\Hom_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})\!=\!(0)\!=\!\Ext^{2}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})
.

By applying
\Hom_{X}(-,E_{0}^{s})
to (
\ast
) while observing (c), we obtain a sequence
(0)\!\longrightarrow\!\Hom_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})

\longrightarrow\!\Hom_{X}(E_{1}^{n},E_{0}^{s})\!=\!(0)
, so that
\Hom_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})\!=\!(0)
. To show exactness at

\Ext^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
, we apply
\Ext^{1}_{X}(-,E_{0}^{s})
to
(\ast)
. Then (a) and (c) yield

(0)=\Ext^{1}_{X}(E_{0}^{m},E_{0}^{s})\longrightarrow\Ext^{2}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})\longrightarrow\Ext^{2}_{X}(E_{1}^{n},E_{0}^{s})=(0),

whence
\Ext^{2}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})\!=\!(0)
.
\diamond

(ii)
We have

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{1}^{t})\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{1}^{t})\!=\!nt\!-\!rmt
.

We apply
\Hom_{X}(-,E_{1}^{t})
to (
\ast
) and obtain

(0)\longrightarrow\Hom_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{1}^{t})\longrightarrow\Hom_{X}(E_{1}^{n},E_{1}^{t})\longrightarrow\Hom_{X}(E_{0}^{m},E_{1}^{t})\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{1}^{t})\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}_{X}(E_{1}^{n},E_{1}^{t})\!=\!(0).

As
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{X}(E_{1}^{n},E_{1}^{t})\!=\!nt
, while
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{X}(E_{0}^{m},E_{1}^{t})\!=\!rmt
, our assertion follows.
\diamond

(iii)
We have

\Ext^{\ell}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\!=\!(0)
for
\ell\!\geq\!2
.

Let
\ell\!\geq\!2
. Application of
\Ext^{\ell-1}_{X}(-,E_{0}^{s})
to
(\ast)
in conjunction with (a) and (c) yields

(0)=\Ext^{\ell-1}_{X}(E_{0}^{m},E_{0}^{s})\longrightarrow\Ext^{\ell}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})\longrightarrow\Ext^{\ell}_{X}(E_{1}^{n},E_{0}^{s})=(0),

so that
\Ext^{\ell}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})\!=\!(0)
. In view of (b) and (a), application of
\Ext^{\ell-1}_{X}(-,E_{1}^{t})
to (
\ast
) gives

(0)=\Ext^{\ell-1}_{X}(E_{0}^{m},E_{1}^{t})\longrightarrow\Ext^{\ell}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{1}^{t})\longrightarrow\Ext^{\ell}_{X}(E_{1}^{n},E_{1}^{t})=(0),

so that
\Ext^{\ell}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{1}^{t})\!=\!(0)
.

Applying
\Ext^{\ell}_{X}(\mathcal{F},-)
to (
\ast\ast
), we finally arrive at

(0)=\Ext^{\ell}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{1}^{t})\longrightarrow\Ext^{\ell}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\longrightarrow\Ext^{\ell+1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})=(0),

so that
\Ext^{\ell}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\!=\!(0)
.
\diamond

By applying (iii), (i) and (ii) consecutively, we obtain

\displaystyle\chi(\msHom_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{1}^{t})\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{1}^{t})\!+\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle nt\!-\!rmt\!+\!\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s}).

Application of
\Hom_{X}(-,E_{0}^{s})
to (
\ast
) gives

(0)=\Hom_{X}(E_{1}^{n},E_{0}^{s})\longrightarrow\Hom_{X}(E_{0}^{m},E_{0}^{s})\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}_{X}(\mathcal{F},E_{0}^{s})\longrightarrow\Ext^{1}_{X}(E_{1}^{n},E_{0}^{s})=(0),

whence

\chi(\msHom_{X}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}))=nt\!-\!rmt\!+\!ms=\langle(m,n),(s,t)\rangle_{r},

as desired. ∎

3.2.
The functor
\TilTheta_{d}

For
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
we consider the
universal vector bundle

\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}
of
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. By definition,
\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}
is the locally free sheaf corresponding to the locally trivial vector space fibration
(E,p)

over
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, where

E\!:=\!\{(\mathfrak{v},a)\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\!\times\!A_{r}\ ;\ a\in\mathfrak{v}\}\ \ \text{and}\ \ p:E\longrightarrow\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ (\mathfrak{v},a)\mapsto\mathfrak{v}

denote the incidence variety and the canonical projection, respectively. Consequently,

\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}(U)=\{s:U\longrightarrow E\ ;\ p\circ s\!=\!\id_{U}\}\cong\{t:U\longrightarrow A_{r}\ ;\ t(\mathfrak{v})\in\mathfrak{v}\ \ \ \forall\ \mathfrak{v}\in U\}

for every open subset
U\subseteq\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. The vector bundle
\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}
is known to be simple and the canonical map
\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{r}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}
is locally split injective. Hence its cokernel
\mathcal{Q}_{(r,d)}

is a vector bundle (cf.
[
7
, (6.2.1)]
), referred to as the
universal quotient bundle
, cf.
[
26
, (3.2.3)]
.

Given a pair
(V_{1},V_{2})
of
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces, we put

\widetilde{V_{1}}:=V_{1}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}\ \ \text{and}\ \ \widetilde{V_{2}}:=V_{2}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}.

Let
f_{i}:V_{i}\longrightarrow W_{i}
(
i\in\{1,2\}
) be linear maps between
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces. These give rise to morphisms
\tilde{f_{i}}:\widetilde{V_{i}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{W_{i}}
, where

\tilde{f_{1}}:=f_{1}\!\otimes\id_{\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}}\ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \tilde{f_{2}}:=f_{2}\otimes\id_{\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}}.

For
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, the standard isomorphisms
\ev_{1,\mathfrak{v}}:\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}(\mathfrak{v})\longrightarrow\mathfrak{v}
and
\ev_{2,\mathfrak{v}}:\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathfrak{v})\longrightarrow\mathbbm{k}
induce commutative diagrams

(3.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{V_{i}}(\mathfrak{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{W_{i}}(\mathfrak{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{i}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathfrak{u}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W_{i}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathfrak{u}_{i},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{V_{i}}\otimes\ev_{i,\mathfrak{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f_{i}}(\mathfrak{v})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{W_{i}}\otimes\ev_{i,\mathfrak{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}\otimes\id_{\mathfrak{u}_{i}}}

where

\mathfrak{u}_{i}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathfrak{v}&i\!=\!1\\
\mathbbm{k}&i\!=\!2.\end{array}\right.

Similarly, the isomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))\cong\mathbbm{k}
allows us to identify
(\tilde{f_{2}})_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}
and
f_{2}
.

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
. In analogy with
[
7
, §8.2]
(see also
[
32
]
), we consider for every open subset
U\subseteq\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, the morphism

\TilTheta_{M,d}(U):\widetilde{M_{1}}(U)\longrightarrow\widetilde{M_{2}}(U)\ \ ;\ \ m\otimes t\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{r}\gamma_{i}\boldsymbol{.}m\otimes\gamma_{i}^{\ast}\circ t

of
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(U)
-modules.
8
8

8

Here
\{\gamma_{1}^{\ast},\ldots,\gamma_{r}^{\ast}\}\subseteq A_{r}^{\ast}
denotes the dual basis of
\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}\}.
Then
\TilTheta_{M,d}:\widetilde{M_{1}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{M_{2}}
is a morphism of vector bundles, and we define

\TilTheta_{d}(M):=\msCoker\TilTheta_{M,d}\in\Coh(\Gr_{d}(A_{r})).

The definition of
\TilTheta_{d}(M)
does not depend on the choice of the dual bases
\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}\}\subseteq A_{r}
,
\{\gamma^{\ast}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\ast}_{r}\}\subseteq A^{\ast}_{r}
. In fact, one can define
sheaves
\widetilde{M}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\widetilde{M}^{\prime}_{2}
via

\widetilde{M}^{\prime}_{1}(U):=\{s:U\longrightarrow A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}\ ;\ s(\mathfrak{v})\subseteq\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}\ \ \ \ \forall\ \mathfrak{v}\in U\}\subseteq\Mor(U,A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1})

and

\widetilde{M}^{\prime}_{2}(U):=\Mor(U,M_{2}).

Setting

\TilTheta^{\prime}_{M,d}(U)(s)=\psi_{M}\circ s\ \ \ \ \forall\ s\in\widetilde{M}^{\prime}_{1}(U),

we obtain

\TilTheta_{d}(M)\cong\coker\TilTheta^{\prime}_{M,d}.

If
f:M\longrightarrow N
is a morphism in
\rep(K_{r})
with components
f_{i}:M_{i}\longrightarrow N_{i}\ \ \ (i\in\{1,2\})
, then

\tilde{f}_{i}:\widetilde{M_{i}}\!\longrightarrow\widetilde{N_{i}}\ \ ;\ \ m\otimes g\mapsto f_{i}(m)\otimes g\ \ \ \ \ \ \ (i\in\{1,2\})

are morphisms of vector bundles and there results a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta_{d}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{N_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{N_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta_{d}(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta_{M,d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta_{N,d}}

Consequently, there is a unique morphism

\TilTheta_{d}(f):\TilTheta_{d}(M)\longrightarrow\TilTheta_{d}(N),

which completes the diagram above. We thus obtain a functor

\TilTheta_{d}:\rep(K_{r})\longrightarrow\Coh(\Gr_{d}(A_{r})).

We denote by
\reppi(K_{r},d)
the full subcategory of
\rep(K_{r})
, whose objects
M
have the property that
\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\!=\!\psi_{M}|_{\mathfrak{v}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}
is surjective for every
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. (This category
coincides with the essential image of
\repp(K_{r},d)
under the standard duality.)

Definition
.

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
. We say that
M
has
constant
d
-rank
, provided there is
\rk_{d}(M)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
\rk(\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})\!=\!\rk_{d}(M)
for all
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.
We let
\CR(K_{r},d)
be the full subcategory of
\rep(K_{r})
whose objects have constant
d
-rank.

Remark
.

The category
\CR(K_{r},d)
is the analog of the category of modules of constant
(d,1)
-radical rank that was considered in
[
17
, (4.1)]
.

We record a few basic properties:

Lemma 3.2.1
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

The functor
\TilTheta_{d}
is right exact.

(2)

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
. Then
M\in\CR(K_{r},d)
if and only if
\TilTheta_{d}(M)\in\Vect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
. In that case, we have
\rk(\TilTheta_{d}(M))\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\rk_{d}(M)
.

(3)

We have
\ker\TilTheta_{d}\cap\CR(K_{r},d)\!=\!\reppi(K_{r},d)
.

Proof.

(1) The right exactness of
\TilTheta_{d}
is a direct consequence of (the proof of) the Snake Lemma.

(2) Suppose that
M\in\CR(K_{r},d)
. Given
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M_{1}}(\mathfrak{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M_{2}}(\mathfrak{v})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{1}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathfrak{v}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M_{2},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{M_{1}}\otimes\ev_{1,\mathfrak{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta_{M,d}(\mathfrak{v})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\id_{M_{2}}\otimes\ev_{2,\mathfrak{v}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\circ\omega}

where
\omega
flips the tensor factors, yields

\rk(\TilTheta_{M,d}(\mathfrak{v}))=\rk(\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})=\rk_{d}(M).

It now follows from
[
7
, (6.2.1)]
that
\TilTheta_{d}(M)
is a vector bundle of rank
\rk(\TilTheta_{d}(M))\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\rk_{d}(M)
.

Conversely, assume that
\TilTheta_{d}(M)
is a vector bundle. Then
[
7
, (6.2.1)]
implies that there is
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

\rk(\TilTheta_{M,d}(\mathfrak{v}))=n\ \ \ \ \ \forall\ \mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r}).

The observation above thus implies that
\rk(\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}})\!=\!n
for all
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, so that
M\in\CR(K_{r},d)
.

(3) Let
M\in\CR(K_{r},d)
. By definition, we have
M\in\reppi(K_{r},d)
if and only if
\rk_{d}(M)\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}
. In view of (2), this is equivalent to
\TilTheta_{d}(M)\!=\!(0)
. ∎

Steiner bundles on projective space were first systematically studied by Dolgachev-Kapranov
[
23
]
. The following definition for Grassmannians is taken from
[
1
]
.

Definition
.

Let
(s,t)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
. A vector bundle
\mathcal{F}\in\Vect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is referred to as an
(s,t)
-Steiner bundle
if there exists an exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}^{s}_{(r,d)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}^{t}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow(0).

We denote by
\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
the full subcategory of
\Vect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
, whose objects are Steiner bundles (for some
(s,t)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{2}
).

In view of
\mathcal{U}_{(r,1)}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(A_{r})}(-1)
(cf.
[
26
, (3.2.3)]
), we retrieve the original definition of
[
23
]
.

The relationship between
\repp(K_{r})
and
\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
, which for
d\!=\!1
is implicit in Brambilla’s work
[
13
,
14
]
on Steiner bundles (for
\mathbbm{k}\!=\!\mathbb{C}
), was investigated by Jardim and
Prata
[
40
]
for
\Char(\mathbbm{k})\!=\!0
in the more general context of cokernel bundles. In our context the category of “globally injective representations“ defined in
[
40
]
coincides with
\repp(K_{r},d)
.

Since we will employ the functors
\TilTheta_{d}
extensively, we recall the basic arguments of the proof of
[
40
, (3.5)]
, thereby hopefully convincing the reader that the result does
not necessitate any assumption on the characteristic of the base field. To that end we require the following subsidiary result.

Lemma 3.2.2
.

The pair
(\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)},\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})})
is special exceptional.

Proof.

Given a partition
\alpha
, we let
|\alpha|\!=\!\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}
be its degree, and
\alpha^{\prime}
be its transpose partition. Let
\mathcal{B}_{r-d,d}
be the set of those partitions, whose corresponding Young tableaux
have at most
r\!-\!d
rows and at most
d
columns. We pick a total ordering
\prec
on
\mathcal{B}_{r-d,d}
such that
|\alpha|<|\beta|
implies
\alpha\prec\beta
.

According to
[
15
, (5.7)]
, the vector bundle

\mathcal{T}:=\bigoplus_{\alpha\in\mathcal{B}_{r-d,d}}\bigwedge^{\alpha^{\prime}}(\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)})

is a tilting object in the bounded derived category
\msD^{b}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
of
\Coh(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
. In particular,
\Ext^{i}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{T})\!=\!(0)
for all
i\!>\!0
. We consider the partitions
\alpha\!:=\!0
and

\beta\!:=\!1
. Then
\alpha\!\prec\!\beta\in\mathcal{B}_{r-d,d}
, so that
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}\!=\!\bigwedge^{\alpha}(\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)})
and
\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}\!=\!\bigwedge^{\beta}(\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)})
are direct summands of
\mathcal{T}
. Consequently,

\Ext^{i}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})\!=\!(0)
for
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\{\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)},\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}\}
and
i\!>\!0
. Since both bundles are simple, our assertion follows from the fact that
\Hom_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})},\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)})\!=\!\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))\!=\!(0)
, cf.
[
61
, (I.5.2)]
. ∎

Theorem 3.2.3
.

The following statements hold:

(1)

\mathcal{F}\in\Vect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is a Steiner bundle if and only if there is
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
such that
\mathcal{F}\cong\TilTheta_{d}(M)
.

(2)

The functor
\TilTheta_{d}:\repp(K_{r},d)\longrightarrow\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is an equivalence.

(3)

Let
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
. Then we have
\rk(\TilTheta_{d}(M))\!=\!\Delta_{M}(d)
.

Proof.

Let
M\in\CR(K_{r},d)
. The proof of Lemma
3.2.1
yields
\ker\TilTheta_{M,d}(\mathfrak{v})\cong\ker\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}
for every
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.

(1) Suppose that
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
. By virtue of Theorem
2.1.5
and the above we have

\ker\TilTheta_{M,d}(\mathfrak{v})\cong\ker\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\!=\!(0)

for all
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. According to
[
7
, (6.2.1)(c)]
, the morphism
\TilTheta_{M,d}
is locally split injective, whence
\msKer\TilTheta_{M,d}\!=\!(0)
. Consequently,
\TilTheta_{d}(M)
is a Steiner bundle.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathcal{F}
is a Steiner bundle. Then there exists a pair
(M_{1},M_{2})
of vector spaces and an exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow\widetilde{M_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Psi}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{M_{2}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow(0)

of vector bundles. It follows from
[
50
, (1.1.12)]
, which holds for fields of arbitrary characteristic, that

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)},\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})})=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\HH^{0}(\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}^{\vee})=r.

Hence the injective canonical map

\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(M_{1},M_{2})\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!A_{r}^{\ast}\longrightarrow\Hom_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\widetilde{M_{1}},\widetilde{M_{2}})

sending
\zeta\otimes f
to the map
m\otimes t\mapsto\zeta(m)\otimes f\circ t
is also surjective and thus an isomorphism. There thus exist
\mathbbm{k}
-linear maps
M(\gamma_{i}):M_{1}\longrightarrow M_{2}
such that

\Psi(m\otimes t)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}M(\gamma_{i})(m)\otimes\gamma_{i}^{\ast}\circ t\ \ \ \ \ \ \forall\ m\in M_{1},t\in\mathcal{U}_{r,d}.

Letting
M\!:=\!(M_{1},M_{2},(M(\gamma_{i}))_{1\leq i\leq r})\in\rep(K_{r})
, we see that
\mathcal{F}\!\cong\!\TilTheta_{d}(M)
, while
\msKer\TilTheta_{M,d}\!=\!(0)
. Consequently,
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
, as
\ker\psi_{M,\mathfrak{v}}\cong(\msKer\TilTheta_{M,d})(\mathfrak{v})\!=\!(0)
for every
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.

(2) Lemma
3.2.2
ensures that
\Ext^{1}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})},\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)})\!=\!(0)
and we may now proceed as in
[
40
, (3.5)]
, observing that the assumption
\Char(\mathbbm{k})\!=\!0
is not needed.

(3) This follows directly from Lemma
3.2.1
. ∎

Remark
.

For
d\!=\!1
, the bundles
\TilTheta_{1}(M_{\mathcal{S}}[m]),\ (m\!\geq\!r\!+\!1)
are those introduced by Schwarzenberger, cf.
[
60
, Prop.2]
. Using Corollary
2.5.6
one obtains an explicit formula for
the generic splitting types of these bundles.

Corollary 3.2.4
.

Let
(0)\longrightarrow A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f}}{{\longrightarrow}}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g}}{{\longrightarrow}}C\longrightarrow(0)
be an exact sequence in
\rep(K_{r})
such that
C\in\repp(K_{r},d)
. Then the sequence
(0)\longrightarrow\TilTheta_{d}(A)\longrightarrow\TilTheta_{d}(B)\longrightarrow\TilTheta_{d}(C)\longrightarrow(0)
is exact.

Proof.

We have exact sequences
(0)\longrightarrow A_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle f_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}B_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle g_{i}}}{{\longrightarrow}}C_{i}\longrightarrow(0)
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. Tensoring with
\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}
yields a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{A_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{B_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{C_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{A_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{B_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{C_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta_{A,d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta_{B,d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta_{C,d}}

with exact rows. The arguments of the proof of Theorem
3.2.3
show that

0=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\psi_{C,\mathfrak{v}}=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker(\TilTheta_{C,d}(\mathfrak{v}))

for all
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. By virtue of
[
34
, (7.3.1)]
, we obtain
\msKer\TilTheta_{C,d}\!=\!(0)
. The assertion now follows by applying the Snake Lemma. ∎

Corollary 3.2.5
.

For
M,N\in\rep(K_{r},d)
the following statements hold:

(1)

We have

\chi(\msHom_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\TilTheta_{d}(M),\TilTheta_{d}(N)))=\langle\udim M,\udim N\rangle_{r}.

(2)

We have

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(M,N)=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\TilTheta_{d}(M),\TilTheta_{d}(N)).

Proof.

(1) This is a direct consequence of Theorem
3.2.3
, Proposition
3.1.1
and Lemma
3.2.2
.

(2) Proposition
3.1.1
and its proof yield

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\TilTheta_{d}(M),\TilTheta_{d}(N))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\TilTheta_{d}(M),\TilTheta_{d}(N))

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\chi(\msHom_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\TilTheta_{d}(M),\TilTheta_{d}(N)))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(M,N)\!-\!\langle\udim M,\udim N\rangle_{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(M,N).\ \ \ \ \ \ \qed

3.3.
Direct consequences

Following
[
26
]
, we begin by collecting a few facts concerning Chow rings of Grassmannians. We emphasize that our statements are valid for the field
\mathbbm{k}
, which has arbitrary characteristic.

For
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,d(r\!-\!d)\}
, we let
Z_{\ell}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
be the free abelian group with basis given by the
\ell
-dimensional irreducible closed subsets of
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. The elements
of
Z_{\ell}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
are called
\ell
-cycles
, those of

Z(\Gr_{d}(A_{r})):=\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{d(r\!-\!d)}Z_{\ell}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))

are referred to as
cycles
.

The
Chow group
of
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
is the factor group

A(\Gr_{d}(A_{r})):=Z(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))/\Rat(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))

given by the subgroup
\Rat(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
defined in
[
26
, (1.2.2)]
. If
C
is a cycle, we write
[C]
for its residue class. The class
[\Gr_{d}(A_{r})]\in A(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is called the
fundamental class
of
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.

Given
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,d(r\!-\!d)\}
, we put

A^{\ell}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r})):=\{[C]\ ;\ C\in Z_{d(r-d)-\ell}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))\}.

We have

A(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))=\bigoplus_{\ell=0}^{d(r-d)}A^{\ell}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r})).

The group
A(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
has the structure of a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded, commutative ring, see
[
26
, (1.2.3)]
. One can show that
A^{0}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))\!=\!\mathbb{Z}\cdot[\Gr_{d}(A_{r})]\cong\mathbb{Z}
.

Definition
.

The ring
A(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is called the
Chow ring
of
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.

Let

\mathcal{V}:\ \ \ \ \ \ (0)\subseteq V_{1}\subseteq V_{2}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq V_{r-1}\subseteq V_{r}\!=\!A_{r}

be a complete flag in
A_{r}
, so that
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{i}\!=\!i
.

We let
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}(r)\!:=\!\{a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}\ ;\ r\!-\!d\!\geq\!a_{1}\!\geq\!\cdots\!\geq\!a_{d}\!\geq\!0\}
. For
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}(r)
we define the
Schubert variety

\Sigma_{a}(\mathcal{V})
via

\Sigma_{a}(\mathcal{V}):=\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ \dim_{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathfrak{v}\cap V_{r-d+i-a_{i}})\!\geq\!i\ \ \ \forall\ i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\}.

Then
\Sigma_{a}(\mathcal{V})
is smooth, closed, irreducible and of codimension
|a|\!:=\!\sum_{i=1}^{d}a_{i}
in
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, cf.
[
26
, (16.4.1)]
. Its class

\sigma_{a}:=[\Sigma_{a}(\mathcal{V})]

is the
Schubert class
of
a
. It does not depend on the choice of
\mathcal{V}
.

Given
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,r\!-\!d\}
, we write

\Sigma_{\ell}:=\Sigma_{(\ell,0,\ldots,0)},

so that

\Sigma_{\ell}(\mathcal{V})=\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})\ ;\ \mathfrak{v}\cap V_{r-d+1-\ell}\!\neq\!(0)\}.

The classes
\sigma_{\ell}\!:=[\Sigma_{\ell}(\mathcal{V})]
are referred to as
special Schubert classes
.

We have

\sigma_{\ell}\in A^{\ell}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))

for all
\ell\in\{0,\ldots,r\!-\!d\}
.

Theorem 3.3.1
.

(cf.
[
26
, (4.7)]
) The Schubert classes
\{\sigma_{a}\ ;\ a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}(r)\}
form a basis of the
\mathbb{Z}
-module
A(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
.

It follows that

\{\sigma_{a}\ ;\ a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}(r),\ |a|\!=\!i\}

is a basis of
A^{i}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
for every
i\in\{0,\ldots,d(r\!-\!d)\}
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a vector bundle on
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. We denote by

c(\mathcal{F})=1\!+\!\sum_{i=1}^{\rk(\mathcal{F})}c_{i}(\mathcal{F})t^{i}\in A(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))[t]

the
Chern polynomial
of
\mathcal{F}
. The coefficient
c_{i}(\mathcal{F})\in A^{i}(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is called the
i
-th
Chern class
of
\mathcal{F}
, cf.
[
26
, (5.2),(5.5)]
.

The Chern polynomial of the universal quotient bundle
\mathcal{Q}_{(r,d)}
on
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
is given by

c(\mathcal{Q}_{(r,d)})=1\!+\!\sum_{\ell=1}^{r-d}\sigma_{\ell}t^{\ell},

see
[
26
, (5.6.2)]
.

Corollary 3.3.2
.

Let
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
. Then we have

c_{1}(\TilTheta_{d}(M))=(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})\sigma_{1}.

Hence the dimension vector of
M
can be recovered from
c_{1}(\TilTheta_{d}(M))
and
\rk(\TilTheta_{d}(M))
(cf. Theorem
3.2.3
(3)).

Proof.

By definition, there is an exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}\longrightarrow A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}\longrightarrow\mathcal{Q}_{(r,d)}\longrightarrow(0),

so that the Whitney sum formula yields
c_{1}(\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)})\!=\!-c_{1}(\mathcal{Q}_{(r,d)})\!=\!-\sigma_{1}
. By the same token, the defining sequence

(0)\longrightarrow\widetilde{M}_{1}\longrightarrow\widetilde{M}_{2}\longrightarrow\TilTheta_{d}(M)\longrightarrow(0)

implies
c_{1}(\TilTheta_{d}(M))\!=\!-c_{1}(\widetilde{M}_{1})\!=\!-(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})c_{1}(\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)})\!=\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})\sigma_{1}
. ∎

The foregoing result allows us to view the first Chern class
c_{1}(\mathcal{F})
of a Steiner bundle
\mathcal{F}
as a number
c_{1}(\mathcal{F})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. In the sequel, we will freely use this interpretation without further notice.

We next employ the functor
\TilTheta_{d}
to translate some results of Section
2.3
to the context of Steiner bundles.

Proposition 3.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
be a Steiner bundle.

(1)

We have
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!\geq\!\min\{c_{1}(\mathcal{F}),d\}(r\!-\!d)
.

(2)

If
c_{1}(\mathcal{F})\!\leq\!d
or
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!<\!d(r\!-\!d)
, then
\mathcal{F}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}^{\rk(\mathcal{F})-(r-d)c_{1}(\mathcal{F})}\!\oplus\!\mathcal{Q}_{(r,d)}^{c_{1}(\mathcal{F})}
.

(3)

If
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)
, then
\mathcal{F}
is either as in (2), or
\mathcal{F}
is simple.

(4)

If
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)
and
c_{1}(\mathcal{F})\!\geq\!d\!+\!1
, then
\mathcal{F}
is simple.

(5)

Suppose that
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!>\!d(r\!-\!d)
. Then there exists a Steiner bundle
\mathcal{G}
such that
\rk(\mathcal{G})\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)
and a short exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}^{\rk(\mathcal{F})-d(r-d)}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow(0).

Proof.

Theorem
3.2.3
provides
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
such that
\TilTheta_{d}(M)\cong\mathcal{F}
and
\rk(\mathcal{F})\cong\Delta_{M}(d)
. In addition, Corollary
3.3.2
gives
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!=\!c_{1}(\mathcal{F})
.

(1) This is a direct consequence of Theorem
2.3.2
.

(2) In view of Corollary
2.3.3
and the remark at the beginning of Section
2.2
, the representation
M
is projective, whence
M\cong\Delta_{M}(r)P_{0}(r)\!\oplus\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(r)
.
We have
\Delta_{M}(r)\!=\!\Delta_{M}(d)\!-\!(r\!-\!d)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}=\rk(\mathcal{F})\!-\!(r\!-\!d)c_{1}(\mathcal{F})
. Moreover,
\TilTheta_{d}(P_{0}(r))\cong\mathcal{O}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}
and since
\TilTheta_{P_{1}(r),d}(t)\!=\!\sum_{i=1}^{r}\gamma_{i}\otimes\gamma_{i}^{\ast}\circ t
for all
t\in\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}(U)
and
U\subseteq\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
open, we obtain
\TilTheta_{d}(P_{1}(r))\cong\mathcal{Q}_{(r,d)}
.

(3) In view of Corollary
2.3.3
,
M
is projective or a brick. In the former case,
\mathcal{F}
is as in (2), in the latter case, Theorem
3.2.3
gives
\End_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F})\cong\End_{K_{r}}(M)\cong\mathbbm{k}
, so that
\mathcal{F}

is simple.

(4) This follows as in (3), using Corollary
2.3.3
(2d,2b).

(5) Let
n\!:=\!\Delta_{M}(d)\!-\!(r\!-\!d)
. Proposition
2.3.5
provides a short exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow nP_{0}(r)\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow(0),

where
N\in\repp(K_{r},d)
has minimal type. Observing Corollary
3.2.4
, we may apply
\TilTheta_{d}
to obtain the asserted sequence. ∎

Remarks
.

Suppose that
\Char(\mathbbm{k})\!=\!0
.

(1) In this case, part (1) was proved in
[
1
, (2.4)]
, using Porteous’ formula.

(2) For
d\!=\!1
, part (5) was obtained in
[
51
, (3.6)]
.

4.
Indecomposable Steiner bundles: Kac’s Theorem and the Auslander-Reiten quiver

In this section, we introduce our two main representation-theoretic tools: Kac’s Theorem concerning dimension vectors of indecomposable representations of
K_{r}
and the Auslander-Reiten quiver
\Gamma(K_{r})
of

\rep(K_{r})
. In combination with the functors
\TilTheta_{d}
the latter will be used to endow the set of isoclasses of indecomposable Steiner bundles with the structure of a quiver, whose connected components can be
studied in terms of those of
\Gamma(K_{r})
.

4.1.
Kac’s Theorem

Recall that an indecomposable module
M\in\rep(K_{r})
is called regular, if it is neither preinjective nor preprojective. For future reference it will be convenient to have the following version of
[
43
, Thm.B]
, which also builds on
[
55
, Thm.3]
, at our disposal:

Theorem 4.1.1

(Kac’s Theorem for
K_{r}
)
.

Let
r\!\geq\!2
and
\delta\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{0}\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\}
.

(1)

If
\delta\!=\!\udim M
for some indecomposable
M\in\rep(K_{r})
, then
q_{r}(\delta)\!\leq\!1
.

(2)

If
q_{r}(\delta)\!=\!1
, then there is a, up to isomorphism, unique indecomposable module
M\in\rep(K_{r})
such that
\udim M\!=\!\delta
. The module
M
is preprojective or preinjective.

(3)

If
q_{r}(\delta)\!\leq\!0
, then every indecomposable module
M\in\rep(K_{r})
such that
\udim M\!=\!\delta
is regular. Moreover, there are infinitely many isoclasses of these modules.

4.2.
Exceptional Steiner bundles

In this section we shall classify exceptional Steiner bundles. Throughout, we assume that
r\!\geq\!2
and
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. Our first result characterizes the
modules corresponding to exceptional Steiner bundles.

Proposition 4.2.1
.

Suppose that
r\!\geq\!3
, and let
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
be a Steiner bundle. Then the following
statements are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{F}
is exceptional.

(2)

There is
M\in\rep(K_{r})
indecomposable preprojective such that
\mathcal{F}\cong\TilTheta_{d}(M)
.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2). By definition, we have
\End_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F})\cong\mathbbm{k}
, while
\Ext^{i}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\!=\!(0)
for all
i\!\geq\!1
. Theorem
3.2.3
provides
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)\subseteq\EKP(K_{r})
such that
\TilTheta_{d}(M)\cong\mathcal{F}
. In particular,
M
is a brick and hence indecomposable. In view of
[
64
, Thm.A]
, the module
M
is not preinjective. Corollary

3.2.5
yields

\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(M,M)\cong\Ext^{1}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\TilTheta_{d}(M),\TilTheta_{d}(M))=(0),

so that Theorem
4.1.1
in conjunction with the Euler-Ringel form (cf. Section
1.1
) ensures that
M
is preprojective.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1). Since
M
is preprojective, an application of
[
4
, (VIII.2.7)]
in conjunction with Corollary
3.2.5
implies

\Ext^{1}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\cong\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(M,M)\!=\!(0).

Now Theorem
4.1.1
ensures that
M
is a brick, so that the
\mathbbm{k}
-space
\End_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\cong\End_{K_{r}}(M)
is one-dimensional. In view of Lemma
3.2.2
, part (iii) of the proof of Proposition
3.1.1

yields
\Ext^{i}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\!=\!(0)
for all
i\!\geq\!2
. As a result, the Steiner bundle
\mathcal{F}
is exceptional. ∎

Part (3) of our next result extends
[
13
, (2.1)]
, where the bundles involved were assumed to be generic. Part (5) shows that
[
14
, (6.3)]
holds for Grassmannians over fields of arbitrary characteristic.

Recall from Section
1.1
that
(P_{n}(r))_{n\geq 0}
denotes the family of preprojective
K_{r}
-representations (of dimension vectors
\udim P_{n}(r)\!=\!(a_{n}(r),a_{n+1}(r))
).

Theorem 4.2.2
.

Suppose that
r\!\geq\!3
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

For each
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
there exists an exceptional Steiner bundle
\mathcal{E}_{n,d}\in\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
such that
\rk(\mathcal{E}_{n,d})\!=\!a_{n+1}(r)\!-\!da_{n}(r)
and
c_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{n,d})\!=\!a_{n}(r)\sigma_{1}
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{E}\in\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is an exceptional Steiner bundle, then
\mathcal{E}\cong\mathcal{E}_{n,d}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(3)

An indecomposable Steiner bundle
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is exceptional if and only if
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!=\!a_{n+1}(r)\!-\!da_{n}(r)
and
c_{1}(\mathcal{F})\!=\!a_{n}(r)\sigma_{1}
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

(4)

A non-zero Steiner bundle
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
satisfies
\Ext^{1}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\!=\!(0)
if and only if there exist
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}\!\times\!\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{F}\cong\mathcal{E}_{n,d}^{a}\!\oplus\!\mathcal{E}_{n+1,d}^{b}
.

(5)

Let
(c,s)\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\!\times\!\mathbb{N}
such that
q_{r}(c,s\!+\!dc)\!>\!1
, then there exist uniquely determined elements
n,a,b\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that a generic Steiner bundle
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
of rank
s
and with first Chern class
c\sigma_{1}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{E}_{n,d}^{a}\!\oplus\!\mathcal{E}_{n+1,d}^{b}
.

Proof.

(1) Let
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and consider the preprojective indecomposable representation
P_{n}(r)
. In view of
\udim P_{n}(r)\!=\!(a_{n}(r),a_{n+1}(r))
, an application of Theorem
3.2.3
,
Proposition
4.2.1
and Corollary
3.3.2
shows that
\mathcal{E}_{n,d}\!:=\!\TilTheta_{d}(P_{n}(r))
is an exceptional Steiner bundle of rank
\Delta_{P_{n}(r)}(d)\!=\!a_{n+1}(r)\!-\!da_{n}(r)
and first Chern class

c_{1}(\mathcal{E}_{n,d})\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}P_{n}(r)_{1}\sigma_{1}\!=\!a_{n}(r)\sigma_{1}
.

(2) This follows immediately from Proposition
4.2.1
, the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{n,d}
and the above-mentioned classification of the indecomposable preprojective
K_{r}
-representations.

(3) In view of (1), exceptional Steiner bundles enjoy the stated properties. Conversely, let
\mathcal{F}
be an indecomposable Steiner bundle such that
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!=\!a_{n+1}(r)\!-\!da_{n}(r)
and
c_{1}(\mathcal{F})\!=\!a_{n}(r)\sigma_{1}
.
According to Theorem
3.2.3
, there is an indecomposable representation
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
such that
\mathcal{F}\cong\TilTheta_{d}(M)
and
\udim M\!=\!(a_{n}(r),a_{n+1}(r))
. Since
q_{r}(\udim M)\!=\!q_{r}(a_{n}(r),a_{n+1}(r))\!=\!1
, Theorem
4.1.1
implies that there is a unique indecomposable representation with dimension vector
(a_{n}(r),a_{n+1}(r))
. Hence
M\cong P_{n}(r)
and
\mathcal{F}\cong\mathcal{E}_{n,d}
is an
exceptional Steiner bundle.

(4) Let
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
be a non-zero Steiner bundle that satisfies
\Ext^{1}_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{F})\!=\!(0)
. Application of Theorem
3.2.3
implies the existence of
(0)\!\neq\!M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
such that

\mathcal{F}\cong\TilTheta_{d}(M)
. Let
N
be an indecomposable direct summand of
M
. By Proposition
2.2.1
we have
N\in\repp(K_{r},d)
. As
\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(N,N)\!=\!(0)
, Theorem
4.1.1
provides

i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
N\cong P_{i}(r)
. We fix
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
minimal subject to
P_{n}(r)
being a direct summand of
M
. Let
m\!>\!n
and assume that
P_{m}(r)
is a direct summand of
M
. We set

l\!:=\!m\!-\!n\!-\!1\!\geq\!0
and conclude with the Auslander-Reiten formula
[
2
, (IV.2.13)]

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(P_{m}(r),P_{n}(r))=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(P_{n+2}(r),P_{m}(r))

\displaystyle=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(\sigma_{K_{r}}^{n+1}(P_{n+2}(r)),\sigma_{K_{r}}^{n+1}(P_{m}(r)))=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(P_{1}(r),P_{l}(r))=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}P_{l}(r)_{1}.

Hence
l\!=\!0
and
m\!=\!n\!+\!1
. The result now follows by applying
\TilTheta_{d}
.

The other implication follows in a similar fashion using the fact that
\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(P_{i}(r),P_{j}(r))\!=\!(0)
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
with
|i\!-\!j|\!\leq\!1
.

(5) We show the statement on the level of representations and fix vector spaces
V_{1},V_{2}
of dimension
c
and
s\!+\!dc
, respectively. Note that we have
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\!<\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{2}
.
Now
[
42
, Thm 4 (b)]

9
9

9

Using the notation of
[
42
, Thm 4 (b)]
we have
\beta_{0}\!=\!\udim I_{0}(r)
and
\beta_{-i}\!=\!\udim I_{i}(r)
,
\beta_{i}\!=\!\udim P_{i-1}(r)
for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, since
\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(P_{i}(r),P_{j}(r))\!=\!(0)
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
with
|i\!-\!j|\!\leq\!1
,
[
43
, Proposition 3]
and Theorem
4.1.1
guarantee that
[
42
, Thm 4 (b)]
holds in arbitrary characteristic.
ensures the
existence of uniquely determined elements
a,b,n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that a generic representation in
N\in\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
is of the form
N\cong aP_{n}(r)\!\oplus\!bP_{n+1}(r)\in\repp(K_{r},d)
. By Theorem

3.2.3
and Corollary
3.3.2
we have
\rk(\TilTheta_{d}(N))\!=\!\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d)\!=\!s
and
c_{1}(\TilTheta_{d}(N))\!=\!c\sigma_{1}
. ∎

Remarks
.

(1) The numerical data in (3) do not imply the indecomposability of
\mathcal{F}
: Let
E\in\rep(K_{3})
be indecomposable of dimension vector
\udim E\!=\!(1,1)
. Since
\udim\tau_{K_{3}}^{-1}(E)\!=\!(2,5)
, it
follows from
[
9
, Thm.]
that
\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(E)\in\EKP(K_{3})\!=\!\repp(K_{r},1)
and hence so is
M\!:=\!\tau^{-1}_{K_{3}}(E)\!\oplus\!P_{1}(3)
. We have
\udim M\!=\!(3,8)\!=\!\udim P_{2}(3)
, but
\mathcal{F}\!:=\!\TilTheta_{1}(M)
is not indecomposable.

(2) In view of Corollary
3.2.4
, the functor
\TilTheta_{d}|_{\repp(K_{r},d)}
is exact. For
i\!\geq\!2
, the almost split sequence

(0)\longrightarrow P_{i-2}(r)\longrightarrow rP_{i-1}(r)\longrightarrow P_{i}(r)\longrightarrow(0)

thus induces an exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i,d}^{\vee}\longrightarrow r\mathcal{E}_{i-1,d}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{i-2,d}^{\vee}\longrightarrow(0)

of vector bundles. For
d\!=\!1
, this corresponds to the construction given in
[
13
, (2.1)]
(with an extra term given by
\mathcal{E}_{-1,d}\!:=\!\mathcal{O}(-1)
). As pointed out in
[
13
, (2.3)]
, the sequences are thus
related to the mutations studied in
[
35
]
.

4.3.
Decomposing
\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
via the Auslander-Reiten quiver

Let
\Gamma(K_{r})
be the Auslander-Reiten quiver of
\rep(K_{r})
. Its vertices are the isoclasses of the indecomposable representations, while its arrows correspond to the so-called

irreducible morphisms
.
10
10

10

By abuse of notation, we will henceforth usually not distinguish between indecomposable representations and the vertices of
\Gamma(K_{r})
associated to them.
Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Gamma(K_{r})
be a component. Given
M\in\mathcal{C}
, we let
(\rightarrow\!M)
and
(M\!\rightarrow)
be the sets of predecessors and successors of
M
, respectively. We refer the reader to
[
4
]
for basic facts
on Auslander-Reiten theory and continue by recording some special features of
\Gamma(K_{r})
.

Let
r\!\geq\!3
,
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. The Auslander-Reiten quiver
\Gamma(K_{r})
of
\rep(K_{r})
is a disjoint union of components. There is a preprojective component
\mathcal{P}
(containing all preprojective modules), a
preinjective component
\mathcal{I}
(containing all preinjective modules), and all other components are called
regular
. Owing to Proposition
2.2.1
the preprojective component
\mathcal{P}
is contained in

\repp(K_{r},d)
and
[
64
, Thm.A]
implies that
\EKP(K_{r})
contains no preinjective modules. Recall that
\{P_{n}(r)\ ;\ n\!\geq\!0\}
is the set of vertices of
\mathcal{P}
, so that Proposition
4.2.1
ensures that

\TilTheta_{d}(\mathcal{P})
is just the set
\{\mathcal{E}_{n,d}\ ;\ n\!\geq\!0\}
of exceptional bundles.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Gamma(K_{r})
be a regular AR-component. By Ringel’s Theorem
[
56
, (2.3)]
, which is fundamental for our methods,
\mathcal{C}
is a stable translation quiver of isomorphism type

\mathbb{Z}[A_{\infty}]
. A representation
M\in\mathcal{C}
is called
quasi-simple
, provided
M
has precisely one immediate predecessor. The quasi-simple representations are therefore located at the “bottom”
of the component.

For each
M\in\mathcal{C}
, there is a unique directed path

M_{[1]}\rightarrow M_{[2]}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow M_{[n-1]}\rightarrow M_{[n]}\!=\!M

in
\mathcal{C}
such that

(a)

for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\!-\!1\}
the irreducible morphism
M_{[i]}\longrightarrow M_{[i+1]}
is injective, and

(b)

for every
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
the factor module
M_{[i]}/M_{[i-1]}\cong\tau^{-i+1}_{K_{r}}(M_{[1]})
(
M_{[0]}\!:=\!(0)
) is quasi-simple.

In that case
\ql(M)\!:=\!n
is called the
quasi-length
of
M
, and we refer to
M_{[1]}
and
M/M_{[n-1]}
as the
quasi-socle
and
quasi-top
of
M
, respectively.
The filtration

M_{[1]}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq M_{[n-1]}\subsetneq M

is called the
quasi-composition series
of
M
with
quasi-composition factors

\{\tau^{-i}_{K_{r}}(M_{[1]})\ ;\ 0\!\leq\!i\!\leq\ql(M)\!-\!1\}
.

Proposition 4.3.1
.

For each regular component
\mathcal{C}
the set
\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\mathcal{C}
forms a non-empty cone in
\mathcal{C}
, consisting of the successors
(M_{\mathcal{C},d}\!\rightarrow)
of a uniquely determined
quasi-simple representation
M_{\mathcal{C},d}
in
\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
2.1.5
and
[
8
, (2.1.3)]
. ∎

M_{\mathcal{C},1}
M_{\mathcal{C},r-1}
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ

\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
