Figure 1.
Cones of successors of
M_{\mathcal{C},1}
and
M_{\mathcal{C},r-1}
in a regular component
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Gamma(K_{r})
with
M_{\mathcal{C},i}\neq M_{\mathcal{C},i+1}
for some

1\!\leq\!i\!<\!r\!-\!1
(cf. Theorem
2.2.3
). The Auslander-Reiten translation is indicated by dotted arrows.

Lemma 4.3.2
.

Let
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
be an indecomposable representation. Then
\TilTheta_{d}(M)
is an indecomposable object in
\Coh(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
.

Proof.

By Theorem
3.2.3
, the functor
\TilTheta_{d}
is full and faithful, so that the ring
\End_{\Gr_{d}(A_{r})}(\TilTheta_{d}(M))\cong\End_{K_{r}}(M)
is local. Consequently,
\TilTheta_{d}(M)
is
indecomposable. ∎

We denote by
\msInd\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
the full subcategory of indecomposable Steiner bundles. Theorem
3.2.3
and Lemma
4.3.2
provide a decomposition

\msInd\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))=\TilTheta_{d}(\mathcal{P})\sqcup\bigsqcup_{\mathcal{C}}\TilTheta_{d}((M_{\mathcal{C},d}\!\rightarrow)),

with
\mathcal{C}
running through the set of regular AR-components of
K_{r}
.
11
11

11

Abusing notation, we write
\msInd\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
for the set of isoclasses of the objects of
\msInd\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
.

Recall that
\Phi_{r}^{-1}\!=\!\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&r\\
-r&r^{2}\!-\!1\end{smallmatrix}\right)
, while
\udim\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)\!=\!\Phi^{-1}_{K_{r}}.\udim M
for every
M\in\rep(K_{r})
without non-zero injective direct
summands, cf.
[
4
, (VIII.2.2)]
.

If
M\not\cong P_{0}(r)
is indecomposable, then

\udim\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)=(r\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2},\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}),

while
\sigma_{K_{r}}(P_{0}(r))\!=\!(0)
and
\sigma_{K_{r}}(P_{i+1}(r))\cong P_{i}(r)
for all
i\!\geq\!0
. Similarly,

\udim\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)=(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2},r\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})

for
M\not\cong S_{1}
. Thus, viewing
M\in\EKP(K_{r})\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\}
as a
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
-module, we obtain

(\dagger)\ \ \ \ \ \ \dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M\!+\!2\Delta_{M}(1)\!\!+\!(r\!-\!2)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}>\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M.

In particular, all irreducible morphisms between preprojective representations in
\rep(K_{r})
are injective, and
P_{i}(r)
is isomorphic to a subrepresentation of
P_{j}(r)
if and only if
i\!\leq\!j
.

Lemma 4.3.3
.

Let
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
be indecomposable. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

If
M\not\cong P_{0}(r)
, then
\Delta_{\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)}(d)\!>\!\Delta_{M}(d)
.

(2)

\Delta_{\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)}(d)\!>\!\Delta_{M}(d)
.

Proof.

(1) As
M
is not isomorphic to
I_{0}(r)\!=\!S_{1}
, we have

\udim\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)=(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2},r\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}).

We thus obtain, observing
\Delta_{M}(d)\!\geq\!0
and
M\not\cong P_{0}(r)
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)}(d)\!-\!\Delta_{M}(d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!-\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!+\!d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(r\!-\!d\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\!+\!(d\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle((r\!-\!d)d\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}>0.

(2) This follows from (1), as
\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)\cong\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-2}(M)
and
\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)\not\cong P_{0}(r)
. ∎

Proposition 4.3.4
.

Let
r\!\geq\!3
,
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. The following statements hold:

(1)

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Gamma(K_{r})
be a regular AR-component.

(a)

We have
\rk(\TilTheta_{d}(M_{\mathcal{C},d}))\!<\!\rk(\TilTheta_{d}(M))
for all
M\in(M_{\mathcal{C},d}\!\rightarrow)\!\smallsetminus\!\{M_{\mathcal{C},d}\}
.

(b)

If
M_{\mathcal{C},d}
is a brick, then every
\mathcal{F}\in\TilTheta_{d}((M_{\mathcal{C},d}\!\rightarrow))
has a filtration by simple Steiner bundles, whose filtration factors are pairwise non-isomorphic.

(2)

If
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
is indecomposable of minimal type, then either
M\cong P_{1}(r)
and
d\!=\!1
, or
M\cong P_{2}(r)
and
d\!=\!r\!-\!1
, or there is a regular AR-component
\mathcal{C}
such that
M\cong M_{\mathcal{C},d}
.

(3)

If
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is simple, then either
\mathcal{F}\in\TilTheta_{d}(\mathcal{P})
, or there is a regular AR-component
\mathcal{C}
and a natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
\mathcal{F}\cong\TilTheta_{d}(\tau^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M_{\mathcal{C},d}))
.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a regular component,
f:(M_{\mathcal{C}}\!\rightarrow)\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}
be a function that is additive on almost split sequences and split exact sequences. For
M\in(M_{\mathcal{C},d}\!\rightarrow)
, we let

\mathcal{W}(M)\subseteq\mathcal{C}
be the
wing
of
M
. (This is the full subgraph of
\mathcal{C}
bounded by the triangle with vertices
M
, the quasi-socle of
M
and the quasi-top of
M
.

M
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\circ
\cdots
\cdots
\vdots

Figure 2.
Wing
\mathcal{W}(M)
of a regular representation
M
of quasi-length
4
in a regular component.

Then
\mathcal{W}(M)\subseteq(M_{\mathcal{C}.d}\!\rightarrow)
, and we put

\mathcal{W}(M)_{1}:=\{X\in\mathcal{W}(M)\ ;\ \ql(X)\!=\!1\}.

(This is the set of quasi-composition factors of
M
.) Using induction on
\ql(M)
one verifies that

(\ast)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ f(M)=\sum_{X\in\mathcal{W}(M)_{1}}f(X).

(1a) Let
M\in(M_{\mathcal{C},d}\!\rightarrow)\!\smallsetminus\!\{M_{\mathcal{C},d}\}
. According to Theorem
3.2.3
(3), the function

f:(M_{\mathcal{C},d}\!\rightarrow)\longrightarrow\mathbb{N}_{0}\ \ ;\ \ M\mapsto\rk(\TilTheta_{d}(M))

is additive on almost split sequences and split exact sequences. Consequently, identity (
\ast
) in conjunction with Lemma
4.3.3
(2) and Theorem
3.2.3
(3) yields

\rk(\TilTheta_{d}(M))=\sum_{X\in\mathcal{W}(M)_{1}}\rk(\TilTheta_{d}(X))>\rk(\TilTheta_{d}(M_{\mathcal{C},d})).

(1b) Since
M_{\mathcal{C},d}
is a brick, it follows that
\tau_{K_{r}}^{-n}(M_{\mathcal{C},d})
is a brick for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Let
M\in(M_{\mathcal{C},d}\!\rightarrow)
. The quasi-composition series of
M
has
\mathcal{W}(M)_{1}\subseteq\{\tau_{K_{r}}^{-n}(M_{\mathcal{C},d})\ ;\ n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
as set of filtration factors, with each factor occurring with multiplicity
1
. By Corollary
3.2.4
and Theorem
3.2.3
, the functor
\TilTheta_{d}:\repp(K_{r},d)\longrightarrow\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
is an exact equivalence, so that the assertion follows.

(2) Suppose that
M
is regular and let
\mathcal{C}
be the regular AR-component containing
M
. A consecutive application of Corollary
2.3.3
(2b) and
[
62
, (XVIII.2.15)]
ensures that
M
is quasi-simple.
Hence there is
n\!\geq\!0
such that
M\cong\tau_{K_{r}}^{-n}(M_{\mathcal{C},d})
. Since
M
has minimal type, Lemma
4.3.3
(2) and Corollary
2.3.3
(1a) yield
n\!=\!0
, whence
M\cong M_{\mathcal{C},d}
.

If
M
is preprojective, then
M\cong P_{i}(r)
for some
i\!\geq\!0
. As
r\!\geq\!3
, we have
\Delta_{P_{0}(r)}(d)\!=\!1\!<\!d(r\!-\!d)
, while
\Delta_{P_{1}(r)}(d)\!=\!r\!-\!d
and

\Delta_{P_{2}(r)}(d)\!-\!d(r\!-\!d)=\Delta_{\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(P_{1}(r))}(d)\!-\!d(r\!-\!d)=(r\!-\!d)^{2}\!-\!1.

Lemma
4.3.3
(1) thus implies
M\!\cong P_{1}(r)
and
d\!=\!1
, or
M\cong P_{2}(r)
and
d\!=\!r\!-\!1
.

(3) Theorem
3.2.3
provides
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
such that
\mathcal{F}\cong\TilTheta_{d}(M)
. Suppose that
M\not\in\mathcal{P}
. As
\mathcal{F}
is simple,
M
is a brick, and
[
62
, (XVIII.2.15)]
shows that
M
is quasi-simple.
Hence there is a regular AR-component
\mathcal{C}
and a natural number
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that
M\cong\tau^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M_{\mathcal{C},d})
. ∎

For future reference we record structural features of regular AR-components that are related to the functor
\sigma_{K_{r}}
. Recall that
\sigma_{K_{r}}
and
\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}
induce quasi-inverse auto-equivalences
on the full subcategory category
\mathcal{R}(K_{r})\subseteq\rep(K_{r})
of regular representations. Moreover, we have a natural equivalence
\sigma_{K_{r}}\circ\sigma_{K_{r}}\cong\tau_{K_{r}}
.

Lemma 4.3.5
.

Let
1\!\leq\!d\!<\!r
and
M\in\rep(K_{r})
be regular indecomposable. Then there exists a unique representation
M_{\sigma,d}
in the
\sigma_{K_{r}}
-orbit
\langle\sigma_{K_{r}}\rangle.M

of
M
such that

\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\langle\sigma_{K_{r}}\rangle.M=\{\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M_{\sigma,d})\ ;\ n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}.

Moreover, we have
M_{\sigma,d}\in\{M_{\sigma,1},\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M_{\sigma,1})\}
.

Proof.

In view of Proposition
4.3.1
, we have
\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\langle\sigma_{K_{r}}\rangle.M\!\neq\!\emptyset
. Let
M_{\sigma,d}\in\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\langle\sigma_{K_{r}}\rangle.M
be a vertex of minimal
dimension. Identity (
\dagger
) right before Lemma
4.3.3
in conjunction with Theorem
2.2.3
(3) yields
\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\langle\sigma_{K_{r}}\rangle.M\!=\!\{\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M_{\sigma,d})\ ;\ n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\}
.

Thanks to Proposition
2.2.1
(3), we have
M_{\sigma,d}\in\repp(K_{r},1)\cap\langle\sigma_{K_{r}}\rangle.M
and there exists
n\!\geq\!0
such that
M_{\sigma,d}\!=\!\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-n}(M_{\sigma,1})
. Moreover,
Theorem
2.2.3
(3) yields
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M_{\sigma,1})\in\repp(K_{r},d)
, whence
n\!\leq\!1
. ∎

Theorem 4.3.6
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Gamma(K_{r})
be a regular component and
M\!:=\!M_{\mathcal{C},1}
.

(1)

We have
M_{\sigma,1}\in\{M,\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)\}
.

(2)

If
M_{\sigma,1}\!=\!\sigma_{K_{r}}{(M)}
, then
(M\!\rightarrow)\!=\!\mathcal{C}\cap\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
.

(3)

If
{M}_{\sigma,1}\!=\!M
, then
(\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)\!\rightarrow)\!=\!\mathcal{C}\cap\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
.

(4)

We have

({M_{[i]}})|_{\mathfrak{v}}\cong M|_{\mathfrak{v}}\!\oplus\!\Delta_{\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M_{[i-1]})}(d)P_{0}(d)\!\oplus\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M_{[i-1]})_{1}P_{1}(d)

for all
i\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
,
1\!\leq\!d\!\leq\!r\!-\!1
and
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.

Proof.

(1) Since
\sigma^{2}_{K_{r}}(M)\not\in\EKP(K_{r})\!=\!\repp(K_{r},1)
, we have
{M}_{\sigma,1}\in\{M,\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)\}
.

(2) If
M_{\sigma,1}\!=\!\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)
, then Theorem
2.2.3
implies
M\in\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
. By Proposition
4.3.1
, the same is true for every element of
(M\!\rightarrow)\!=\!\mathcal{C}\cap\EKP(K_{r})
. Consequently,
(M\!\rightarrow)\subseteq\mathcal{C}\cap\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)\subseteq\mathcal{C}\cap\EKP(K_{r})\!=\!(M\!\rightarrow)
.

(3) If
M\!=\!M_{\sigma,1}
, then
\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)\not\in\EKP(K_{r})
and Theorem
2.2.3
(2) shows that
M\not\in\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
. On the other hand, Theorem

2.2.3
(3) implies
\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)\cong\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-2}(M)\in\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
, so that
\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)\!=\!M_{\mathcal{C},r-1}
. Consequently,
(\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)\!\rightarrow)\!=\!\mathcal{C}\cap\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
.

(4) Let
i\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 2}
,
1\!\leq\!d\!<\!r
, and
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. General theory provides a short exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow M_{[i]}\longrightarrow\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M_{[i-1]})\longrightarrow(0).

By virtue of (2), (3) and Proposition
2.2.1
(3), we have
\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M_{[i-1]})\in\repp(K_{r},d)
, so that

\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M_{[i-1]})|_{\mathfrak{v}}\cong\Delta_{\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M_{[i-1]})}(d)P_{0}(d)\!\oplus\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M_{[i-1]})_{1}P_{1}(d)

is projective. In particular, the above short exact sequence splits upon restriction to
\mathbbm{k}.\mathfrak{v}
. ∎

5.
Homogeneity and uniformity for representations and Steiner bundles

Throughout this section, we assume that
r\!\geq\!3
. We will employ AR-theory to obtain results on indecomposable modules and Steiner bundles, whose restrictions to
K_{2}
and
\mathbb{P}^{1}
all have the
same isomorphism type, respectively. We shall see that all preprojective representations as well as ”almost all“ regular indecomposable
\EKP
-representations are uniform in an even stronger sense. As a result,
uniform representations belonging to
\EKP(K_{r})
and Steiner bundles are usually not homogeneous.

5.1.
Homogeneous modules and bundles

Given
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, a linear map
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
gives rise to an injective morphism
\hat{\alpha}:\mathbb{P}(A_{d})\longrightarrow\mathbb{P}(A_{r})
. There results the inverse image functor

\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}:\Coh(\mathbb{P}(A_{r}))\longrightarrow\Coh(\mathbb{P}(A_{d})),

which is known to be right exact, cf.
[
34
, (7.11)]
. The additive functor
\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}
commutes with direct sums and the map that sends a fraction
\frac{f}{g}
of homogeneous polynomials to

\frac{f\circ\alpha}{g\circ\alpha}
induces isomorphisms
\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(j))\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{d-1}}(j)
for
j\in\mathbb{Z}
.
12
12

12

Here we interpret
f
and
g
as elements of the algebra
\mathbbm{k}[A_{r}]\cong S(A_{r}^{\ast})
of polynomial functions on
A_{r}
.
Moreover, for
\gamma^{\ast}\in A_{r}^{\ast}
, the morphism

\gamma^{\ast}\cdot:\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(j)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(j\!+\!1)\ \ ;\ \ f\mapsto\gamma^{\ast}f

is being sent to

\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\gamma^{\ast}\cdot):\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{d-1}}(j)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{d-1}}(j\!+\!1)\ \ ;\ \ g\mapsto(\gamma^{\ast}\!\circ\!\alpha)g.

For ease of notation, we will write
\TilTheta\!:=\!\TilTheta_{1}
and
\TilTheta_{M}\!:=\!\TilTheta_{M,d}
.

Lemma 5.1.1
.

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
. For
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
we have

\TilTheta(\alpha^{\ast}(M))\cong\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\TilTheta(M)).

Proof.

Let
\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{d}\}
be a basis for
A_{d}
and put
\delta_{i}\!:=\!\alpha(\gamma_{i})
. We choose
\delta_{d+1},\ldots,\delta_{r}\in A_{r}
such that
\{\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{r}\}

is a basis for
A_{r}
. For the corresponding dual bases we obtain

\delta_{i}^{\ast}\circ\alpha=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\gamma_{i}^{\ast}&i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}\\
0&\text{else.}\end{array}\right.

We compute the morphism
\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\TilTheta_{M})
. Let
U\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{d-1}
be open,
g\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{d-1}}(-1)(U)
and
m\in M
. In view of our foregoing observations we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\TilTheta_{M})(m\otimes g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}M(\delta_{i})(m)\otimes\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\delta_{i}^{\ast}\cdot)(g)=\sum_{i=1}^{d}M(\delta_{i})(m)\otimes(\delta_{i}^{\ast}\!\circ\alpha)g

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}M(\alpha(\gamma_{i}))(m)\otimes\gamma_{i}^{\ast}g=\TilTheta_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}(m\otimes g),

so that

\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\TilTheta_{M})=\TilTheta_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}.

In view of the right exactness of
\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}
, this implies

\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\TilTheta(M))=\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\coker\TilTheta_{M})\cong\coker\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\TilTheta_{M})=\coker\TilTheta_{\alpha^{\ast}(M)}=\TilTheta(\alpha^{\ast}(M)),

as desired. ∎

Recall that the general linear group
\GL(A_{r})
naturally acts on
A_{r}
and
\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
, on the latter via automorphisms of associative
\mathbbm{k}
-algebras. For every
M\in\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
and
g\in\GL(A_{r})
, the twisted
module
M^{(g)}
has underlying
\mathbbm{k}
-space
M
and action given by

a\boldsymbol{.}m:=(g^{-1}(a)).m\ \ \ \ \forall\ g\in\GL_{r}(A_{r}),a\in\mathbbm{k}K_{r},m\in M.

In this fashion,
\GL(A_{r})
acts on
\modd\mathbbm{k}K_{r}
and
\rep(K_{r})
via auto-equivalences. For
g\in\GL_{r}(\mathbbm{k})\cong\GL(A_{r})
with
g^{-1}\!=\!(a_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq r}
we obtain

M^{(g)}(\gamma_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{ij}M(\gamma_{i})\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 1\!\leq\!j\!\leq\!r.

Given
M\in\rep(K_{r})
, the stabilizer

\GL(A_{r})_{M}:=\{g\in\GL_{r}(A_{r})\ ;\ M^{(g)}\cong M\}

is a closed subgroup
[
29
, (2.1)]
, and we say that
M
is
homogeneous
, provided
\GL(A_{r})_{M}\!=\!\GL(A_{r})
.

The group
\GL_{r}(A_{r})\!=\!\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r},A_{r})
acts on
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}\!=\!\mathbb{P}(A_{r})
via automorphisms and hence on
\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
: Given
g\in\GL(A_{r})
and
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
, we define

(g^{\ast}.\mathcal{F})(U):=\mathcal{F}(g^{-1}.U)

for every open subset
U\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
. A coherent sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
is called
homogeneous
, provided
g^{\ast}.\mathcal{F}\cong\mathcal{F}
for every
g\in\GL(A_{r})
.

Corollary 5.1.2
.

Let
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
. Then
M
is homogeneous if and only if
\TilTheta(M)
is homogeneous.
13
13

13

This result can readily be seen to hold for
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
and
\TilTheta_{d}(M)\in\Vect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
as well.

Proof.

In view of
g^{\ast}.\mathcal{F}\!=\!\widehat{g^{-1}}^{\ast}(\mathcal{F})
and
M^{(g)}\!=\!(g^{-1})^{\ast}(M)
, our assertion follows directly from Lemma
5.1.1
. ∎

Remark
.

Homogeneous vector bundles of small rank are scarce: Suppose that
\Char(\mathbbm{k})\!=\!0
and let
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
be an indecomposable homogeneous Steiner bundle of rank at most
2r\!-\!3
.
In view of
[
5
, Thm.]
, we have:

(a)

r\!=\!3
and
\mathcal{F}\in\{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-1)\},S^{2}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-2)
}, or

(b)

r\!\geq\!4
and
\mathcal{F}\in\{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}},\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(-1)\}
.

Let
\{\gamma^{\ast}_{1},\ldots,\gamma^{\ast}_{r}\}\subseteq A_{r}^{\ast}
be the dual basis of
\{\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{r}\}
. For
M\in\rep(K_{r})
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, we consider the linear map

\widehat{\gamma}_{i}:A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1}\longrightarrow M_{1}\ \ ;\ \ a\otimes m\mapsto\gamma^{\ast}_{i}(a)m.

Then we have

\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)(\gamma_{i})=\widehat{\gamma}_{i}|_{\ker\psi_{M}}.

Lemma 5.1.3
.

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
.

(1)

We have
\sigma_{K_{r}}(M^{(g)})\cong\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)^{((g^{-1})^{\tr})}
for all
g\in\GL(A_{r})\cong\GL_{r}(\mathbbm{k})
.

(2)

M
is homogeneous if and only if
\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)
is homogeneous.

Proof.

(1) For
g\in\GL(A_{r})
we have

g(\gamma_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}g_{ij}\gamma_{i}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ 1\!\leq\!j\!\leq\!r.

Direct computation shows that
\ker\psi_{M^{(g)}}\!=\!(g\otimes\id_{M_{1}})(\ker\psi_{M})
.

Setting
\omega_{K_{r}}(M)\!:=\!(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1},M_{1})
and

\omega_{K_{r}}(M)(\gamma_{i})\!=\!\widehat{\gamma_{i}},

we obtain

\displaystyle\omega_{K_{r}}(M^{(g)})(\gamma_{\ell})((g\otimes\id_{M_{1}})(\gamma_{j}\otimes m))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma_{\ell}}(\sum_{i=1}^{r}g_{ij}\gamma_{i}\otimes m)=g_{\ell j}m

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\sum_{i=1}^{r}g_{\ell i}\omega_{K_{r}}(M)(\gamma_{i}))(\gamma_{j}\otimes m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{K_{r}}(M)^{((g^{-1})^{\tr})}(\gamma_{\ell})(\gamma_{j}\otimes m)

for
m\in M_{1}
. By the above, the subrepresentation
\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)\subseteq\omega_{K_{r}}(M)
is
(g\otimes\id_{M_{1}})
-stable, and hence
(g\!\otimes\!\id_{M_{1}},\id_{M_{1}})
induces an isomorphism

\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)^{((g^{-1})^{\tr})}\cong\sigma_{K_{r}}(M^{(g)})
.

(2) This is a direct consequence of (1). ∎

5.2.
Generalized uniform modules

Recall from Section
1.3
that every
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
affords a generic decomposition
M_{\gen}\!=\!\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}n_{i}(M)P_{i}(2)
, with

O_{M}:=\{\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{2}(A_{r})\ ;\ n_{i}(M,\mathfrak{v})\!=\!n_{i}(M)\}

being a dense open subset of
\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
. We say that
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
is
uniform
, provided
O_{M}\!=\!\Gr_{2}(A_{r})
. In
[
53
, Chap.1,3.3.1]
, the authors provide a notion
that encompasses those of homogeneity and uniformity. In our context the corresponding property reads as follows:

Definition
.

Let
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
. We say that a representation
M\in\rep(K_{r})
is
d
-homogeneous
, provided
\alpha^{\ast}(M)\cong\beta^{\ast}(M)
for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
.

Note that every
d
-homogeneous representation belongs to
\CR(K_{r},d)
. (Here we set
\CR(K_{r},r)\!:=\!\rep(K_{r})
.) For
d\!=\!1
, we have in fact equality. Moreover,
M
is
r
-homogeneous if and only if it is
homogeneous, while the uniform
\EKP
-representations correspond to those that are
2
-homogeneous.
14
14

14

If
M\in\rep(K_{r})
is
2
-homogeneous, then
M\in\CR(K_{r})
has constant rank. The observations at
the end of Section
1.3
show that
M
has a generic decomposition
M_{\gen}\!=\!\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}n_{i}(M)P_{i}(2)\!\oplus\!\bigoplus_{j\geq\!0}m_{j}(M)I_{j}(2)
. Hence
M
is
2
-homogeneous if and only if
\alpha^{\ast}(M)\cong M_{\gen}
for all
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
.

Remarks
.

(1) If
M
is
d
-homogeneous, then we either have
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}\!=\!\emptyset
or
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}\!=\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
. (Here
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},r)_{M}\!:=\!\emptyset
in case
M
is projective and
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},r)_{M}\!:=\!\{A_{r}\}
otherwise.)

(2) If
\mathfrak{v}\not\in\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}
, then

M|_{\mathfrak{v}}\cong\Delta_{M}(d)P_{0}(d)\!\oplus\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(d).

Consequently, every
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
is
d
-homogeneous for
1\!\leq\!d\!\leq\!r\!-\!1
.

(3) If
M
is homogeneous, then
M
is
d
-homogeneous for all
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
.

Proposition 5.2.1
.

Let
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. Then there is a brick
M\in\rep(K_{r},d)
such that
M
not
(d\!+\!1)
-homogeneous.

Proof.

We first assume that
d\!\leq\!r\!-\!2
and pick a pair
(V_{1},V_{2})
of
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces such that
\udim(V_{1},V_{2})\!=\!(2d,d(r\!+\!d))
. Then we have

(a)

\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d)\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)
, and

(b)

\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d\!+\!1)\!\geq\!0
.

We consider
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\!=\!\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})
. Given
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d+1}(A_{r})
, the restriction map

\res_{\mathfrak{v}}:\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\longrightarrow\Hom_{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2})\ \ ;\ \ \psi\mapsto\psi|_{\mathfrak{v}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}}

is linear and hence a morphism. Consequently,

\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{v}}:=\res_{\mathfrak{v}}^{-1}(\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(\mathfrak{v}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!V_{1},V_{2}))

is an open subset of
\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
, which, by virtue of (b), is not empty. In view of (a), Proposition
2.2.4
implies
\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{v}}\neq\emptyset
. Theorem
2.1.5
thus provides
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)\cap\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})
such that
\mathfrak{v}\not\in\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d\!+\!1)_{M}
. As
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
has minimal type, Corollary
2.3.3
(2b,2d) and Corollary
2.3.4
ensure that
M\not\in\rep(K_{r},d\!+\!1)
is
a brick. In particular,
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d\!+\!1)_{M}\neq\emptyset
.

Since
M
belongs to
\repp(K_{r},d)
, it is clearly
d
-homogeneous. In view of
\emptyset\neq\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d\!+\!1)_{M}\!\neq\!\Gr_{d+1}(A_{r})
, there are
\alpha,\beta\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d+1},A_{r})
such that

\alpha^{\ast}(M)
is projective, while
\beta^{\ast}(M)
is not. Consequently,
M
is not
(d\!+\!1)
-homogeneous.

If
d\!=\!r\!-\!1
, we pick the Schwarzenberger module
M_{\mathcal{S}}[r\!+\!2]\in\EKP(K_{r})
. This module has minimal type and Corollary
2.3.3
(2b,2d) implies that
M
is a regular brick. In view of Corollary
2.5.6

and its succeeding remark
M_{\mathcal{S}}[r\!+\!2]
is not homogeneous. Thanks to Theorem
2.2.3
, Lemma
5.1.3
and
\sigma_{K_{r}}
being an auto-equivalence on regular modules, the representation

N\!:=\!\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)\in\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
is an
(r\!-\!1)
-homogeneous brick, which is not
r
-homogeneous. ∎

Every
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
gives rise to a linear embedding
\hat{\alpha}:\mathbb{P}^{d-1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
.

Definition
.

Let
d\in\{0,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. A vector bundle
\mathcal{F}\in\Vect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
is said to be
d
-homogeneous
, provided

\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\mathcal{F})\cong\hat{\beta}^{\ast}(\mathcal{F})

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d+1},A_{r})
.

It directly follows from Lemma
5.1.1
that
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
is
d
-homogeneous if and only if
\TilTheta(M)
is
(d\!-\!1)
-homogeneous.

A closed subset
L\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
is called a
line
, provided
L\!=\!\im\hat{\alpha}
for some
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{2},A_{r})
. Let
\iota:L\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
be the inclusion. Given

\mathcal{F}\in\Vect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
, we put

\mathcal{F}|_{L}:=\iota^{\ast}(\mathcal{F}).

Since vector bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
are homogeneous, we have

\mathcal{F}|_{L}\cong\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\mathcal{F})\ \ \ \ \ \ \text{whenever}\ \ L=\hat{\alpha}(\mathbb{P}^{1}).

Definition
.

A vector bundle
\mathcal{F}\in\Vect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
is said to be
uniform
, if there are
a_{i}(\mathcal{F})\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
such that

\mathcal{F}|_{L}\cong\bigoplus_{i\geq 0}a_{i}(\mathcal{F})\mathcal{O}_{L}(i)

for every line
L\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
.

It follows that
\mathcal{F}
is
(r\!-\!1)
-homogeneous if and only if it is homogeneous. Moreover,
\mathcal{F}
is uniform if and only if
\mathcal{F}
is
1
-homogeneous.

Example
.

Since the projective representation
P_{1}(3)
is homogeneous, so is
M\!:=\!P_{2}(3)\cong\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(P_{1}(3))
, cf. Lemma
5.1.3
. Then
M
has dimension vector
(3,8)
and Proposition

2.2.1
(2) ensures that
M\in\repp(K_{3},2)
. Consequently,
M
is uniform with
M_{\gen}\!=\!\Delta_{M}(2)P_{0}(2)\!\oplus\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(2)
. Note that
\mathcal{F}\!:=\!\TilTheta_{1}(M)
is an indecomposable Steiner
bundle such that
c_{1}(\mathcal{F})\!=\!3
and
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!=\!5
.

Let
N\in\repp(K_{3},2)
be such that
\udim N\!=\!(3,8)
. Since
\Delta_{N}(2)\!=\!2
, (2b) and (2d) of Corollary
2.3.3
ensure that
N
is a brick and not projective. Consequently,
\udim\sigma_{K_{3}}(N)\!=\!(1,3)
,
so that
\sigma_{K_{3}}(N)\cong P_{1}(3)
and
N\cong P_{2}(3)\!=\!M
. As a result,
\mathcal{F}
is isomorphic to the dual of the bundle constructed in
[
51
, Ex.3.1]
.

Corollary
5.1.2
and Lemma
3.2.1
imply that
\mathcal{F}
is homogeneous (and hence uniform) such that

\mathcal{F}|_{L}\cong 2\mathcal{O}_{L}\!\oplus 3\mathcal{O}_{L}(1)

for every line
L\subseteq\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

According to
[
53
, Chap.1, 3.3.2]
, there exists, for every
d\!\in\{1,\ldots,\!r\!-\!2\}
, a holomorphic vector bundle
\mathcal{F}_{d}
on
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}(\mathbb{C})
such that
\mathcal{F}_{d}
is
(d\!-\!1)
-homogeneous but not
d
-homogeneous.
The following result, which slightly strengthens that of loc. cit., implies that such examples may also be found within the category of Steiner bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
.

Corollary 5.2.2
.

Given
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
, there exists a simple Steiner bundle
\mathcal{F}_{d}
that is
(d\!-\!1)
-homogeneous, but not
d
-homogeneous.

Proof.

Proposition
5.2.1
provides a brick
M\in\rep(K_{r},d)
such that
M
is not
(d\!+\!1)
-homogeneous. Consequently, the bundle
\mathcal{F}_{d}\!:=\!\TilTheta(M)
has the requisite properties. ∎

In
[
51
]
the authors introduced the notion of uniform vector bundles of
k
-type and studied the case
k\!=\!1
in detail.
15
15

15

The vector bundles studied in
[
51
]
are dual to those considered here.

Given
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we say that a
d
-homogeneous Steiner bundle
\mathcal{F}
is of
k
-type
, provided there are
n_{0},\ldots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
n_{k}\!\neq\!0
such that

\alpha^{\ast}(\mathcal{F})\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{i}[d\!+\!1]^{n_{i}}

for all
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d+1},A_{r})
. Here the
\mathcal{E}_{i}[d\!+\!1]\in\StVect(\mathbb{P}(A_{d+1}))
are the exceptional Steiner bundles discussed in Section
4.2
.

Our next result implies that, for
k\!=\!1
, we obtain the essential image of
\repp(K_{r},d\!+\!1)
.

Lemma 5.2.3
.

Let
d\in\{2,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
,
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
be a Steiner bundle. Then the following statements are equivalent:

(1)

\mathcal{F}\in\TilTheta(\repp(K_{r},d))
.

(2)

\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\mathcal{F})\cong\mathcal{O}^{c_{1}(\mathcal{F})}_{\mathbb{P}(A_{d})}\!\oplus\!\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}(A_{d})}^{\rk(\mathcal{F})-(d-1)c_{1}(\mathcal{F})}(-1)
for every
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2). By assumption, there exists
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
such that
\mathcal{F}\cong\TilTheta(M)
. Given
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
, it follows that
\alpha^{\ast}(M)
is projective. Observing Lemma

5.1.1
, we thus obtain

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}^{\ast}(\TilTheta(M))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\TilTheta(\alpha^{\ast}(M))\cong\TilTheta(P_{0}(d))^{\Delta_{M}(d)}\!\oplus\!\TilTheta(P_{1}(d))^{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(A_{d})}^{\rk(\mathcal{F})-(d-1)c_{1}(\mathcal{F})}\!\oplus\!\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}(A_{d})}^{c_{1}(\mathcal{F})}(-1).

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1). There is
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
such that
\mathcal{F}\cong\TilTheta(M)
. The foregoing calculation in conjunction with Lemma
5.1.1
and Theorem
3.2.3
now shows that

\alpha^{\ast}(M)\cong\Delta_{M}(d)P_{0}(d)\!\oplus\!(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(d)

for all
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
, so that each
\alpha^{\ast}(M)
is projective. It follows that
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
. ∎

Corollary 5.2.4
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Gamma(K_{r})
be a regular AR-component.

(1)

Every
M\in(\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M_{\mathcal{C},1})\!\rightarrow)
is
(r\!-\!1)
-homogeneous.

(2)

If
M\in(\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M_{\mathcal{C},1})\!\rightarrow)
, then
\TilTheta(M)
is
(r\!-\!2)
-homogeneous of
1
-type.

(3)

Let
M_{\mathcal{C},1}
be
d
-homogeneous for some
d\!\leq\!r
.

(a)

Every
M\in(M_{\mathcal{C},1}\!\rightarrow)
is
d
-homogeneous.

(b)

Let
d\!\geq\!2
. If
\TilTheta(M_{\mathcal{C},1})
is
(d\!-\!1)
-homogeneous of
k
-type, then
\TilTheta(M)
is
(d\!-\!1)
-homogeneous of
k
-type for every
M\in(M_{\mathcal{C},1}\rightarrow)\!\smallsetminus\!(\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M_{\mathcal{C},1})\rightarrow)
.

Proof.

(1) In view of Theorem
2.2.3
(3), we have
(\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M_{\mathcal{C},1})\!\rightarrow)\subseteq\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
, so that every
M\in(\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M_{\mathcal{C},1})\!\rightarrow)
is in particular

(r\!-\!1)
-homogeneous.

(2) This is a direct consequence of (1), Lemma
5.2.3
and Lemma
5.1.1
.

(3a) If
d\!=\!r
, then
M
is homogeneous, and our assertion follows from
[
29
, (2.3)]
in conjunction with
\GL(A_{r})
being connected. Alternatively, Theorem
4.3.6
(3) implies that
(M_{\mathcal{C},1})_{[i]}
is

d
-homogeneous for every
i\!\geq\!2
. The result thus follows from (1).

(3b) Since
\TilTheta(M_{\mathcal{C},1})

(d\!-\!1)
-homogeneous of
k
-type, Theorem
4.2.2
(2) in conjunction with Lemma

5.1.1
and Theorem
3.2.3
(2) implies that there are
n_{0},\ldots,n_{k}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,
n_{k}\!\neq\!0
such that

\alpha^{\ast}(M_{\mathcal{C},1})\cong\bigoplus_{i=0}^{k}n_{i}P_{i}(d)\ \ \ \ \ \ \forall\ \alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r}).

Parts (3) of Theorem
4.3.6
now shows that for every
M\in(M_{\mathcal{C},1}\rightarrow)\!\smallsetminus\!(\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M_{\mathcal{C},1})\rightarrow)
its restriction
\alpha^{\ast}(M)
can be written in such a way.
This implies (b). ∎

Remark
.

If
d\!\leq\!r\!-\!2
, then Proposition
5.2.1
provides an indecomposable representation
M\in\rep(K_{r},d)
such that
M
is not
(d\!+\!1)
-homogeneous. Let
\mathcal{C}_{M}
be the AR-component containing
M
. As “most” vertices of the cone
\mathcal{C}_{M}\cap\repp(K_{r},d)
are
(d\!+\!1)
-homogenous, we see that the natural analogue of
[
29
, (2.3)]
does not hold in this context.

In 1960 Schwarzenberger
[
60
, (1.2)]
raised the question, whether all uniform vector bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
are homogeneous. The first counterexample was given by Elencwajg
[
27
]
in 1979. The following
result implies that every indecomposable inhomogeneous Steiner bundle gives rise to families of inhomogeneous, but
(r\!-\!2)
-homogeneous indecomposable vector bundles of
1
-type.

Corollary 5.2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Gamma(K_{r})
be a component that contains a representation
M\in\mathcal{C}
that is not homogeneous. Then every
X\in(\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M_{\mathcal{C},1})\!\rightarrow)
is

(r\!-\!1)
-homogeneous, but not homogeneous.

Proof.

Since preprojective and preinjective representations are homogeneous, it follows that
\mathcal{C}
is regular. By Corollary
5.2.4
, the cone
(\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M_{\mathcal{C},1})\!\rightarrow)\subseteq\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)

consists of
(r\!-\!1)
-homogeneous representations. As
M\in\mathcal{C}
is not homogeneous and
\GL(A_{r})
is connected,
[
29
, (2.3)]
ensures that all representations in
\mathcal{C}
are inhomogeneous. ∎

Remark
.

In the situation above, every
X\in(\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M_{\mathcal{C},1})\!\rightarrow)
gives rise to a family of Steiner bundles that are not homogeneous but
(r\!-\!2)
-homogeneous: In view of
[
29
]
,

\GL(A_{r})_{X}:=\{g\in\GL(A_{r})\ ;\ X^{(g)}\cong X\}

is a closed, proper subgroup of
\GL(A_{r})
, so that the variety
\GL(A_{r})/\GL(A_{r})_{X}
has dimension
\geq\!1
. Hence there are at least
|\mathbbm{k}|
pairwise non-isomorphic inhomogeneous but
(r\!-\!1)
-homogeneous
representations of dimension vector
\udim X
. Upon application of
\TilTheta
, we obtain a family of Steiner bundles, whose members are inhomogeneous but
(r\!-\!2)
-homogeneous.

5.3.
Constructions

In this section we turn to the existence of modules and bundles with certain prescribed properties. This contributes to understanding the conceptual
sources of several computations and ad hoc arguments in
[
51
]
.

Corollary 5.3.1
.

Let
d\!\geq\!2
. For every
r\!\geq\!d\!+\!1
and
n\!\geq\!d\!+\!2
, there exists
N\in\repp(K_{r},d)
such that

(a)

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{1}\!=\!n
, and

(b)

N
is not homogeneous and of minimal type.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
r
, starting with
r\!=\!d\!+\!1
. Let
n\!\geq\!d\!+\!2
, so that
\ell\!:=\!n\!-\!r\!+\!1\!\geq\!2
. We consider the Schwarzenberger module
M\!:=\!M_{\mathcal{S}}[\ell+r]\in\EKP(K_{r})\!=\!\repp(K_{r},1)
of dimension vector
\udim M\!=\!(\ell,\ell\!+\!r\!-\!1)
. Thanks to Theorem
2.2.3
the representation
N\!:=\!\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)
belongs to
\repp(K_{r},d)
. It has dimension vector
\udim N\!=\!(n,(r\!-\!1)n\!+\!d)
and
hence is of minimal type. Since
\ell\!\geq\!2
, Corollary
2.5.6
and its succeeding remark imply that
M
is not homogeneous. By Lemma
5.1.3
, the representation
N
is also not homogeneous.

Suppose the statements hold for some
r\!\geq\!d\!+\!1
. Given
n\!\geq\!d\!+\!2
, there is
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
of minimal type such that (a) and (b) hold. Proposition
2.3.7
provides
N\in\repp(K_{r+1},d)
of
minimal type such that
N|_{K_{r}}\cong M\!\!\oplus\!dP_{0}(r)
. In particular, (a) holds. If
N
is homogeneous, then its restriction
N|_{K_{r}}
inherits this property. As
\GL(A_{r})
is connected, this then holds for every
direct summand of
N|_{K_{r}}
, a contradiction.
16
16

16

One can show that the category of uniform representations is closed under taking direct summands. Hence one can also find
N
of minimal type that is not
uniform.
∎

Remark
.

Upon application of
\TilTheta_{d}
, Corollary
5.3.1
yields a generalization of
[
51
, (4.1)]
: One obtains inhomogeneous vector bundles
\mathcal{F}_{d}\in\StVect(\Gr_{d}(A_{r}))
with
c_{1}(\mathcal{F}_{d})\!=\!n\sigma_{1}
and
\rk(\mathcal{F}_{d})\!=\!\dim\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.

We provide a conceptual proof for the following generalization of
[
51
, (3.5)]
:

Proposition 5.3.2
.

Let
d\in\{2,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. The following statements hold:

(1)

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
be a Steiner bundle.

(a)

If
c_{1}(\mathcal{F})\!\geq\!d
and
\mathcal{F}
is
(d\!-\!1)
-homogeneous of
1
-type, then
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!\geq\!d(r\!-\!d)\!+\!(d\!-\!1)c_{1}(\mathcal{F})
.

(b)

If
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}
is not a direct summand of
\mathcal{F}
, then
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!\leq\!c_{1}(\mathcal{F})(r\!-\!1)
.

(2)

For each pair
(c,\ell)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
such that
\ell\!\geq\!d(r\!-\!d)\!+\!c(d\!-\!1)
there is
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
which is
(d\!-\!1)
-homogeneous of
1
-type and such that
(c_{1}(\mathcal{F}),\rk(\mathcal{F}))\!=\!(c,\ell)
.

Proof.

(1) Let
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
be such that
\TilTheta(M)\cong\mathcal{F}
.

(a) Lemma
5.2.3
implies
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
with
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!=\!c_{1}(\mathcal{F})\!\geq\!d
and
\Delta_{M}\!=\!\rk(\mathcal{F})
. Theorem
2.3.2
now yields
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!=\!\Delta_{M}(d)\!+\!(d\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\geq\!d(r\!-\!d)\!+\!(d\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)\!+\!(d\!-\!1)c_{1}(\mathcal{F})
.

(b) Since
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}
is not a direct summand of
\mathcal{F}
, the map
\psi_{M}
is surjective, so that there is a projective resolution

(0)\longrightarrow-\Delta_{M}(r)P_{0}(r)\longrightarrow(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})P_{1}(r)\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow(0).

In particular,
\Delta_{M}(r)\!\leq\!0
, whence
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!=\!\Delta_{M}\!=\!\Delta_{M}(r)\!+\!(r\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\leq\!c_{1}(\mathcal{F})(r\!-\!1)
.

(2) Let
(V_{1},V_{2})
be a pair of
\mathbbm{k}
-vector spaces such that
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\!=\!c
and
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{2}\!=\!c\!+\!\ell
. By assumption, we have
\Delta_{(V_{1},V_{2})}(d)\!=\!\ell\!-\!(d\!-\!1)c\!\geq\!d(r\!-\!d)
and
Proposition
2.2.4
provides
M\in\rep(K_{r};V_{1},V_{2})\cap\repp(K_{r},d)
. Hence
\mathcal{F}\!:=\!\TilTheta(M)
has the requisite properties. ∎

We continue by providing a general method of constructing uniform
1
-type Steiner bundles which are not homogeneous relative to certain subgroups of
\GL(A_{r})
.

Let
H\subseteq\GL(A_{r})
be a subgroup. Then
M\in\rep(K_{r})
is called
H
-homogeneous
, provided

M^{(h)}\cong M\ \ \ \ \ \forall\ h\in H.

There is an analogous notion of
H
-homogeneous vector bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
.

For
s\!<\!r
, we put
A_{s}\!:=\!\bigoplus_{j=1}^{s}\mathbbm{k}\gamma_{j}
and
A_{r-s}\!:=\!\bigoplus_{j=s+1}^{r}\mathbbm{k}\gamma_{j}
, so that
A_{r}\!=\!A_{s}\!\oplus\!A_{r-s}
. Given
M\in\rep(K_{s})
, we denote by
\Inf(M)\in\rep(K_{r})
the
representation with underlying pair of spaces
(M_{1},M_{2})
and structure map

\psi_{\Inf(M)}((a\!+\!b)\otimes m):=\psi_{M}(a\otimes m)\ \ \ \ \ \ a\in A_{s},b\in A_{r-s},m\in M_{1}.

Equivalently,

\Inf(M)(\gamma_{i})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}M(\gamma_{i})&1\!\leq\!i\!\leq\!s\\
0&s\!+\!1\!\leq\!i\!\leq\!r.\end{array}\right.

Note that

\iota:\GL(A_{s})\longrightarrow\GL(A_{r})\ \ ;\ \ h\mapsto h\!\oplus\!\id_{A_{r-s}}

is an injective homomorphism of algebraic groups such that

(\ast)\ \ \ \ \ \Inf(M)^{\iota(h)}=\Inf(M^{(h)})\ \ \ \ \ \forall\ h\in\GL(A_{s}).

In the sequel, we shall identify
\GL(A_{s})
with its image under
\iota
in
\GL(A_{r})
.

Remark
.

If
N\in\rep(K_{s})
is homogeneous, then (
\ast
) shows that
\Inf(N)
is
\GL(A_{s})
-homogeneous. However,
\Inf(N)
is usually not
s
-homogeneous.

Lemma 5.3.3
.

Let
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
. The following statements hold:

(1)

For
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, we have
\udim E(\mathfrak{v})\!=\!(d,rd\!-\!1)
,
\udim\sigma_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}))\!=\!(1,d)
and
\udim\tau_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}))\!=\!(r\!-\!d,1)
.

(2)

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
be regular such that
\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{M}\!\neq\!\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
.

(a)

We have
\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)\in\repp(K_{r},d)
.

(b)

If
d\!>\!1
, then
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)\in\repp(K_{r},d\!-\!1)
.

(c)

If
d\!>\!1
, then
\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)\in\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
.

Proof.

(1) Let
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
be such that
\im\alpha\!=\!\mathfrak{v}
. Directly from the definition we obtain

\udim D_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}))=\udim\tau_{K_{r}}(\coker\bar{\alpha})=\Phi_{r}\boldsymbol{.}(1,r\!-\!d)=(rd\!-\!1,d),

whence
\udim E(\mathfrak{v})\!=\!(d,rd\!-\!1)
. Writing
\sigma_{r}(a,b)\!:=\!(ra\!-\!b,a)
, we have
\udim\sigma_{K_{r}}(N)\!=\!\sigma_{r}(\udim N)
for every
N\in\rep(K_{r})
. The remaining assertions now follow by applying
\sigma_{r}

to
(d,rd\!-\!1)
and
(1,d)
, respectively.

(2) In view of Theorem
2.1.5
, our current assumption provides
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
with
\Hom_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}),M)

=\!(0)
.

(a) Let
\mathfrak{u}\in\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
be such that
\Hom_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{u}),\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M))\!\neq\!(0)
. Then
\Hom_{K_{r}}(\tau_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{u})),M)\!\neq\!(0)
and since

d\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\tau_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{u}))_{1}\!=\!d(r\!-\!d)\!>\!1\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\tau_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{u}))_{2}
, an application of Proposition
2.1.4
ensures that

0\neq\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\ker\psi_{\tau_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{u})),\mathfrak{v}}=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}),\tau_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{u})).

Since
\tau_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{u}))
is elementary, while
E(\mathfrak{v})
and
M
are regular, it follows from
[
8
, (2.1.1)]
that
\Hom_{K_{r}}(E(\mathfrak{v}),M)\!\neq\!(0)
, a contradiction.

(b) This is analogous to (a).

(c) Thanks to (b), we have
\sigma_{K_{r}}(\tau^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M))\cong\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)\in\repp(K_{r},d\!-\!1)\subseteq\EKP(K_{r})
. The assertion now follows from Theorem
2.2.3
. ∎

Our next result produces
(s\!-\!1)
-homogeneous
1
-type vector bundles
\mathcal{F}
that fail to be
\GL(A_{s})
-homogeneous.

Proposition 5.3.4
.

Let
r\!>\!s\!>\!d\geq\!1
. Suppose that
M\in\rep(K_{s})
is an indecomposable, non-simple representation such that
\mathcal{V}(K_{s},d)_{M}\!\neq\!\Gr_{d}(A_{s})
.
Then the following statements hold:

(1)

\Inf(M)
and
\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))
are regular, quasi-simple representations.

(2)

\sigma^{-2}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))\in\repp(K_{r},d)
.

(3)

We have
(\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))\!\rightarrow)\subseteq\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
. For each
N\in(\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))\!\rightarrow)
, the Steiner bundle
\TilTheta(N)\in\StVect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
is
(r\!-\!2)
-homogenous
of
1
-type, but not homogeneous.

(4)

Suppose that
M
is not homogeneous. For each
N\in(\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))\!\rightarrow)
, the Steiner bundle
\TilTheta(N)\in\StVect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
is
(s\!-\!1)
-homogeneous of
1
-type, but not

\GL(A_{s})
-homogeneous.

Proof.

(1) By
[
8
, (3.2.1), (3.2.2)]
, the representation
\Inf(M)\in\rep(K_{r})
is indecomposable, regular and quasi-simple. Therefore, the same holds for
\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))
.

(2) By assumption, there is
\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{s})
such that
\alpha^{\ast}(M)
is projective. Let
\iota:A_{s}\longrightarrow A_{r}
be the inclusion. Then
\iota\circ\alpha\in\Inj_{\mathbbm{k}}(A_{d},A_{r})
and
(\iota\circ\alpha)^{\ast}(\Inf(M))\cong\alpha^{\ast}(\iota^{\ast}(\Inf(M)))\cong\alpha^{\ast}(M)
, so that
\im(\iota\circ\alpha)\not\in\mathcal{V}(K_{r},d)_{\Inf(M)}
. The assertion now follows from (1) and Lemma
5.3.3
(2a).

(3) By (1),(2) and Theorem
2.2.3
(3) we know that
\sigma_{K_{r}}(\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M)))\in\EKP(K_{r})
is quasi-simple. Now Theorem
2.2.3
(2) yields
\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))\in\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
. We conclude with Theorem
4.3.6
that
(\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))\!\rightarrow)\subseteq\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
. As
\Inf(M)(\gamma_{r})\!=\!0
, the
representation
\Inf(M)
is not homogeneous. Hence Lemma
5.1.3
shows that
\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))
enjoys the same property. Consequently, every representation belonging to the (regular) component of

\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))
is not homogeneous. In conclusion, each bundle
\TilTheta(N)\in\StVect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
with
N\in(\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))\!\rightarrow)
is
(r\!-\!2)
-homogeneous of
1
-type and not
homogeneous.

(4) The arguments of Corollary
5.2.5
in conjunction with (
\ast
) show that the representations belonging to
(\sigma^{-3}_{K_{r}}(\Inf(M))\!\rightarrow)\subseteq\repp(K_{r},r\!-\!1)
are
(r\!-\!1)
-homogeneous and therefore belong to
\repp(K_{r},s).
Hence the corresponding Steiner bundles are
(s\!-\!1)
-homogeneous of
1
-type. Since
\GL(A_{s})
is connected, none of these representations is
\GL(A_{s})
-homogeneous. ∎

Example
.

Let
s\!=\!2
and consider
M\!=\!(\mathbbm{k},\mathbbm{k},(\id_{\mathbbm{k}},\id_{\mathbbm{k}}))\in\rep(K_{2})
. Then
\mathcal{V}(K_{2},1)_{M}\!\neq\!\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
M
is indecomposable, non-simple and not homogeneous. Proposition
5.3.4

thus provides Steiner bundles
\mathcal{F}\in\StVect(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
that are uniform but not
\GL(A_{2})
-homogeneous.

6.
Stability

Throughout this section, we assume that
r\!\geq\!3
. Let
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
be a torsion-free sheaf. By definition, the
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1},x}
-module
\mathcal{F}_{x}
is torsion-free for every
x\in\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
. If
\mathcal{F}\!\neq\!(0)
,
then
\rk(\mathcal{F})\!\neq\!0
and we denote by

\mu(\mathcal{F}):=\frac{c_{1}(\mathcal{F})}{\rk(\mathcal{F})}

the
slope
of
\mathcal{F}
. Recall that
\mathcal{F}
is called
semistable
(
stable
), provided

\mu(\mathcal{G})\leq\mu(\mathcal{F})\ \ \ \ (\mu(\mathcal{G})<\mu(\mathcal{F}))

for every proper subsheaf
(0)\subsetneq\mathcal{G}\subsetneq\mathcal{F}
(such that
\rk(\mathcal{G})\!<\!\rk(\mathcal{F})
).

In this section, we relate the stability of Steiner bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
to that of the corresponding representations of
\EKP(K_{r})\!=\!\repp(K_{r},1)
. Let
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
. As before, write
\TilTheta(M)\!:=\!\TilTheta_{1}(M)
and recall the defining exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow\widetilde{M_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\TilTheta_{M}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\widetilde{M_{2}}\longrightarrow\TilTheta(M)\longrightarrow(0),

where
\widetilde{M_{i}}\!=\!M_{i}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(i\!-\!2)
for
i\in\{1,2\}
. We continue to write
\Delta_{M}\!:=\!\Delta_{M}(1)
, so that Corollary
2.3.3
yields
\Delta_{M}\!\geq\!r\!-\!1
in case
M_{1}\!\neq\!(0)
.

We begin by recalling the See-Saw Lemma:

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
(0)\longrightarrow\mathcal{E}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow(0)
be a short exact sequence of non-zero torsion-free coherent sheaves. Then we have
\mu(\mathcal{E})\!\leq\!\mu(\mathcal{F})\!\leq\!\mu(\mathcal{G})
or
\mu(\mathcal{G})\!\leq\!\mu(\mathcal{F})\!\leq\!\mu(\mathcal{E})
. Moreover,
\mu(\mathcal{H})\!=\!\mu(\mathcal{F})
for
\mathcal{H}\in\{\mathcal{E},\mathcal{G}\}
implies
\mu(\mathcal{E})\!=\!\mu(\mathcal{F})\!=\!\mu(\mathcal{G})
.

Proof.

The Whitney sum formula implies

\mu(\mathcal{F})\!=\!\frac{\rk(\mathcal{E})\mu(\mathcal{E})+\rk(\mathcal{G})\mu(\mathcal{G})}{\rk(\mathcal{F})}.

If
\mu(\mathcal{F})\!\leq\!\mu(\mathcal{E})
and
\mu(\mathcal{F})\!<\!\mu(\mathcal{G})
, the foregoing formula yields

\mu(\mathcal{F})=\frac{\rk(\mathcal{E})\!+\!\rk(\mathcal{G})}{\rk(\mathcal{F})}\mu(\mathcal{F})<\frac{\rk(\mathcal{E})\mu(\mathcal{E})\!+\!\rk(\mathcal{G})\mu(\mathcal{G})}{\rk(\mathcal{F})}=\mu(\mathcal{F}),

a contradiction. Thus,
\mu(\mathcal{G})\!\leq\!\mu(\mathcal{F})
, and the other assertions follow analogously.∎

6.1.
Stability for
\EKP
-representations and Steiner bundles

The main result of this section recasts
[
44
, (5.1)]
, which was formulated for
\mathbbm{k}\!=\!\mathbb{C}
, into our set-up.

Definition
.

For
(0)\!\neq\!M\in\EKP(K_{r})
, we define

\mu(M):=\frac{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}{\Delta_{M}}.

We say that
M
is
stable
, provided
\mu(N)\!<\!\mu(M)
for every subrepresentation
(0)\subsetneq N\subsetneq M
. We say that
M
is
semistable
, provided
\mu(N)\!\leq\!\mu(M)
for every
subrepresentation
(0)\subsetneq N\subsetneq M
.

We identify the Chow ring
A(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
with the truncated polynomial ring
\mathbb{Z}[X]/(X^{r})
. Theorem
3.2.3
and Corollary
3.3.2
then show that

\mu(M)=\mu(\TilTheta(M))

for every non-zero
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
. In particular,
\mu(\TilTheta(M))\!\geq\!0
for every such
M
.

Proposition 6.1.1
.

Let
M\in\EKP(K_{r})\!\smallsetminus\!\{(0)\}
. The following statements hold:

(1)

If
\TilTheta(M)
is semistable, then
M
is semistable.

(2)

If
\TilTheta(M)
is stable, then
M
is stable.

Proof.

Let
(0)\subsetneq N\subsetneq M
be a proper submodule with canonical inclusion
\iota:N\hookrightarrow M
. There results a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{N_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{N_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta(N)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{M_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\iota}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta(\iota)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\TilTheta_{M}}

with exact rows. In view of
\msCoker\tilde{\iota}_{1}\cong\widetilde{(M/N)_{1}}
the Snake Lemma provides a monomorphism
\msKer\TilTheta(\iota)\hookrightarrow\widetilde{(M/N)_{1}}
. By definition,

\widetilde{(M/N)_{1}}\cong(M_{1}/N_{1})\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(-1)
is semistable (see
[
53
, (1.2.4)]
) and with slope
\mu(\widetilde{(M/N)_{1}})\!=\!-1
whenever
\widetilde{(M/N)_{1}}\!\neq\!(0).

Since
\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
is an abelian category, there is an exact sequence

(\ast)\ \ \ \ \ \ (0)\longrightarrow\msKer\TilTheta(\iota)\longrightarrow\TilTheta(N)\longrightarrow\msIm\TilTheta(\iota)\longrightarrow(0)

and
\TilTheta
being faithful entails
\msIm\TilTheta(\iota)\!\neq\!(0)
.

(1) Suppose that
\TilTheta(M)
is semistable. If
\msKer\TilTheta(\iota)\!=\!(0)
, we have
\mu(N)\!=\!\mu(\TilTheta(N))\!=\!\mu(\msIm\TilTheta(\iota))\!\leq\!\mu(\TilTheta(M))\!=\mu(M)
. Alternatively,

\mu(\msKer\TilTheta(\iota))\!\leq\!-1\!<\!\mu(\TilTheta(N))
and Lemma
6.1
in conjunction with (
\ast
) implies
\mu(N)\!=\!\mu(\TilTheta(N))\!<\!\mu(\msIm\TilTheta(\iota))\!\leq\!\mu(\TilTheta(M))\!=\mu(M)
. Hence
M
is semistable.

(2) Suppose that
\TilTheta(M)
is stable. Then
M
is indecomposable and the assumption
N_{1}\!=\!M_{1}
either implies
M\!=\!P_{0}(r)\!=\!N
, or
N_{2}\subseteq M_{2}\!=\im\psi_{M}\!=\!\im\psi_{N}\subseteq N_{2}
, a
contradiction. If
\rk(\TilTheta(N))\!\geq\!\rk(\TilTheta(M))
, we obtain
\Delta_{N}\!\geq\!\Delta_{M}
, whence
\mu(N)\!<\!\mu(M)
. We may therefore assume that
\rk(\TilTheta(N))\!<\!\rk(\TilTheta(M))
.

If
\msKer\TilTheta(\iota)\!=\!(0)
, we have
\rk(\msIm\TilTheta(\iota))\!=\!\rk(\TilTheta(N))\!<\!\rk(\TilTheta(M))
as well as
\mu(N)\!=\!\mu(\TilTheta(N))\!=\!\mu(\msIm\TilTheta(\iota))\!<\!\mu(\TilTheta(M))\!=\!\mu(M)
.
Alternatively, we may proceed as in (1). ∎

Remark
.

There is a slope
\mu
, defined for non-zero, torsion-free coherent sheaves of
\Gr_{d}(A_{r})
, such that

\mu(\TilTheta_{d}(M))=\mu(M)

for all
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)\!\smallsetminus\!\{(0)\}
. In view of
[
11
]
, the bundle
\mathcal{U}_{(r,d)}
is semistable relative to this slope, so that the foregoing result may be generalized to this context.

Lemma 6.1.2
.

Let
M\in\repp(K_{r},d)
be non-projective and of minimal type. Then
M
is stable.

Proof.

Let
N\subsetneq M
be a proper submodule. We shall show that
\mu(N)\!<\!\mu(M)
. Decomposing

N=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}N_{i}

into its indecomposable constituents, Lemma
6.1
implies
\mu(N)\leq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\mu(N_{i})
. Hence we may assume
N
to be indecomposable.

If
N
is projective, then
\mu(N)\!\leq\!\frac{1}{r-1}
. Assuming
\mu(N)\!\geq\!\mu(M)
, we have
d(r\!-\!d)\!+\!(d\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}=\!\Delta_{M}\!\geq\!(r\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}
, so that

(r\!-\!d)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\leq\!d(r\!-\!d)
. Hence
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\leq\!d
, and the remark at the beginning of Section
2.2
shows that
M
is projective, a contradiction.

We may thus assume that
N
is not projective, so that Corollary
2.3.3
(1) implies
\Delta_{N}(d)\!\geq\!\Delta_{M}(d)
. In view of Corollary
2.3.3
(2), the non-projective representation
M
is indecomposable.
The assumption
N_{1}\!=\!M_{1}
thus entails
N_{2}\!=\!\psi_{M}(A_{r}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!N_{1})\!=\!\psi_{M}(A_{r}\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!M_{1})\!=\!M_{2}
, a contradiction. Consequently,

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{1}\Delta_{M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{1})(\Delta_{M}(d)\!+\!(d\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})=\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{1}\Delta_{M}(d)\!+\!(d\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{1}

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\Delta_{N}(d)\!+\!(d\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{1}=(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1})(\Delta_{N}(d)\!+\!(d\!-\!1)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\Delta_{N},

whence
\mu(N)\!<\!\mu(M)
. As a result,
M
is stable. ∎

Remark
.

For
d\!=\!1
and
\Char(\mathbbm{k})\!=\!0
, the foregoing result is a consequence of Proposition
6.1.1
(2) and
[
12
, (2.7)]
.

6.2.
The distribution of slopes

For
N\in\rep(K_{r})
with
N_{1}\!\neq\!(0)
we put

\nu(N):=\frac{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{2}}{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}N_{1}}

and note that

\mu(M)=\frac{1}{\nu(M)\!-\!1}

for every
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
such that
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}\!\neq\!0
. Given
r\!\geq\!3
, recall that
L_{r}\!=\!\frac{r+\sqrt{r^{2}\!-\!4}}{2}
. We have
\frac{1}{L_{r}}\!=\!\frac{r\!-\!\sqrt{r^{2}\!-\!4}}{2}
and
r\!-\!L_{r}\!=\!\frac{1}{L_{r}}
.

Proposition 6.2.1
.

Let
M,N\in\EKP(K_{r})
be indecomposable with
M\not\cong P_{0}(r)
.

(1)

We have
\lim_{n\to\infty}\nu(\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M))\!=\!L_{r}
.

(2)

The sequence
(\mu(\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M)))_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
of slopes converges to
\frac{1}{L_{r}-1}
.

(3)

If
M
is regular, then the sequence
(\mu(\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M)))_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is strictly decreasing.

(4)

If
M
is regular and
N
is preprojective, then
\mu(N)\!<\!\frac{1}{L_{r}-1}\!<\!\mu(M)
.

Proof.

We write
\udim\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M)\!=:\!(x_{n},y_{n})
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and recall that

\udim\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(X)=(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}X_{2},r\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}X_{2}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}X_{1})

for each representation
X
without
S_{1}
as a direct summand. Theorem
2.2.3
yields
\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M)\in\EKP(K_{r})
and
\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M)
is regular or preprojective
with
x_{n}\!\neq\!0
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we set

d_{n}(M):=\nu(\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M))

and note that
\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M)\in\EKP(K_{r})
implies
d_{n}(M)\!>\!1
.

(1) We first consider the case where
M
is preprojective. Kac’s Theorem implies
0\!<\!q_{r}(\udim\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M))

=\!q_{r}(x_{n},y_{n})\!=\!x_{n}^{2}\!+\!y_{n}^{2}\!-\!rx_{n}y_{n}
, whence

(\ast)\ \ \ \ \ \ d_{n}(M)=\frac{y_{n}}{x_{n}}>r\!-\!\frac{x_{n}}{y_{n}}=\frac{ry_{n}\!-\!x_{n}}{y_{n}}=d_{n+1}(M)>1.

Now let
M
be regular and note that
f\!:=\!X^{2}\!-\!rX\!+\!1\in\mathbb{R}[X]
has roots
L_{r},\frac{1}{L_{r}}
. As
r\!\geq\!3
, Kac’s Theorem yields
0\!>\!x_{n}^{2}\!+\!y_{n}^{2}\!-\!rx_{n}y_{n}
, so that
f(d_{n}(M))\!<\!0
and
d_{n}(M)\!<\!L_{r}
.
Moreover, the computation of (
\ast
) implies
d_{n}(M)\!<\!d_{n+1}(M)
.

We have shown that the sequence
(d_{n}(M))_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
converges in both cases and the identity

d_{n+1}(M)=\frac{ry_{n}-x_{n}}{y_{n}}=r\!-\!\frac{1}{d_{n}(M)}

implies that
d\!:=\!\lim_{n\to\infty}d_{n}(M)
is a root of
f
. Since
\frac{1}{L_{r}}\!<\!1\!<\!d_{n}(M)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, it follows that
d\!=\!L_{r}
.

(2) We have
\mu(\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M))\!=\!\frac{1}{d_{n}(M)-1}
. Since
d_{n}(M)\!>\!1
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we conclude with (1) that
(\mu(\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M)))_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
converges to
\frac{1}{L_{r}-1}
.

(3) Let
M
be regular. As noted in the proof of (1), the sequence
(d_{n}(M))_{n\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is strictly increasing, so that the identity
\mu(\sigma^{-n}_{K_{r}}(M))\!=\!\frac{1}{d_{n}(M)-1}
implies the assertion.

(4) Let
M
be regular. Then
1\!<\!d_{0}(M)\!<\!L_{r}
and therefore
\mu(M)\!=\!\frac{1}{d_{0}(M)-1}\!>\!\frac{1}{L_{r}-1}
. If
N\!=\!P_{0}(r)
, then
\mu(N)\!=\!0
. Alternatively,
d_{0}(N)
is well-defined and
q_{r}(\udim N)\!>\!0

yields
d_{0}(N)\!>\!L_{r}
. This shows
\mu(N)\!=\!\frac{1}{d_{0}(N)-1}\!<\!\frac{1}{L_{r}-1}
. ∎

We continue by recording three important consequences.

Corollary 6.2.2
.

Let
M\in\EKP(K_{r})
be indecomposable.

(1)

The representation
M
is (semi)stable if and only if
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)\in\EKP(K_{r})
is (semi)stable.

(2)

The following statements are equivalent:

(a)

All representations in
\{\sigma_{K_{r}}^{z}(M);z\in\mathbb{Z}\}\cap\EKP(K_{r})
are (semi)stable
17
17

17

For
M=P_{\ell}(r)
we only consider
z\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq-\ell}
.
.

(b)

The representation
M
is (semi)stable.

(3)

If
M
is regular and semistable, then
M
is quasi-simple.

Proof.

Let
Z\in\EKP(K_{r})
. Recall that we have
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(Z)\in\EKP(K_{r})
and

\udim\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(Z)=(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}Z_{2},r\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}Z_{2}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}Z_{1}).

Hence we obtain for
U,V\in\EKP(K_{r})
:

(\ast)\quad\mu(\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(U))\leq(<)\mu(\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(V))\Leftrightarrow\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}U_{1}\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{2}\leq(<)\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}V_{1}\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}U_{2}\Leftrightarrow\mu(U)\leq(<)\mu(V).

(1) Since
\mu(P_{0}(r))=0<\mu(P_{1}(r))
and
\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(P_{\ell}(r))=P_{\ell+1}(r))
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we obtain
\mu(P_{\ell}(r))<\mu(P_{\ell+1}(r))
for all
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. Clearly,
P_{0}(r)
is stable. Now we fix
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
and let
(0)\subsetneq X\subsetneq P_{\ell}(r)
be a subrepresentation. We fix a direct summand
Y\subseteq X
of maximal slope. Then Lemma
6.1
yields
\mu(Y)\geq\mu(X)
. Since the only proper indecomposable subrepresentations of
P_{\ell}(r)
are (up to isomorphism)
P_{0}(r),\ldots,P_{\ell-1}(r)
, we conclude
\mu(P_{\ell}(r))\!>\!\mu(P_{\ell-1}(r))\!\geq\!\mu(Y)\!\geq\!\mu(X)
. Hence every preprojective indecomposable representation is stable and we only have to consider the case that
M
is regular.

At first we assume that
M
is stable. Let
(0)\subsetneq X\subsetneq N\!:=\!\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)
, and assume that
X
is indecomposable. If
X
is preprojective, then Proposition
6.2.1
(4) implies
\mu(X)\!<\!\mu(N)
.
Alternatively,
X
is regular. Since
\sigma_{K_{r}}
is left exact and an equivalence on regular modules, there is a proper inclusion
(0)\!\neq\!\sigma_{K_{r}}(X)\hookrightarrow M
. As
M
is stable, we obtain
\mu(\sigma_{K_{r}}(X))\!<\!\mu(M)
and
(\ast)
implies
\mu(X)\!\leq\!\mu(Y)
. If
X
is not indecomposable, we consider an indecomposable summand
Y\subseteq X
of maximal slope and argue as in the preprojective case. This shows that
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)
is stable.

Now we assume that
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)
is stable and let
(0)\subsetneq X\subsetneq M
be a subrepresentation of maximal slope. We show that
\mu(X)\!<\!\mu(M)
. If
M/X\in\add(I_{0}(r))
, then we find
a\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\udim X=(\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}-a,\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2})
and conclude with
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{2}\neq 0
that

\mu(X)=\frac{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}-a}{\Delta_{M}+a}<\frac{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}M_{1}}{\Delta_{M}}=\mu(M).

Hence we may assume that
M/X\!=\!Q\!\oplus\!aI_{0}(r)
with
a\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
Q\in\rep_{1}(K_{r})
non-zero. We find a subrepresentation
0\subsetneq Y\subsetneq M
with
\udim Y\!=\!\udim X\!+\!(a,0)
and maximality of
\mu(X)
implies

\frac{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}X_{1}+a}{\Delta_{X}-a}=\mu(Y)\leq\mu(X)=\frac{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}X_{1}}{\Delta_{X}}.

This can only happen if
a\!=\!0
. Hence we have a short exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow X\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow Q\longrightarrow(0)

in
\rep_{1}(K_{r})
. Since
\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}:\rep_{1}(K_{r})\longrightarrow\rep_{2}(K_{r})
is exact (cf. Section
2.2
), we obtain a proper inclusion
(0)\neq\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(X)\hookrightarrow\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)
and
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}(M)
being stable in conjunction with
(\ast)
implies that
\mu(X)<\mu(M)
. Hence
M
is stable.
The arguments concerning semistability are analogous.

(2) This is a direct consequence of (1).

(3) We write
M\!=\!N_{[\ell]}
with quasi-socle
N
and assume that
\ell\!\geq\!2
. General AR-theory provides a short exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow M\longrightarrow\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(N_{[\ell\!-\!1]})\longrightarrow(0)

in
\EKP(K_{r})
and the See-Saw Lemma for representations give us
\mu(N)\!\leq\!\mu(M)\!\leq\!\mu(\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(N_{[\ell\!-\!1])})
.
Moreover,
N_{[\ell\!-\!1]}
being a subrepresentation of
M\!=\!N_{[\ell]}
and Proposition
6.2.1
(3) imply

\mu(M)\leq\mu(\tau_{K_{r}}^{-1}(N_{[\ell\!-\!1]}))<\mu(N_{[\ell\!-\!1]})\leq\mu(M),

a contradiction. Hence
\ell=1
and
M
is quasi-simple.
∎

Remarks
.

(1) Owing to Corollary
6.2.2
, a cone of a regular component either contains no (semi)stable representations or all of its quasi-simple vertices are (semi)stable.

(2) Let
r\geq 3
and
B_{1},B_{2}
be non-isomorphic indecomposable representations with dimension vector
(1,1)
. Clearly,
B_{1},B_{2}
are elementary representations and by
[
46
, (1.4)]

B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are
\Hom
-orthogonal. We conclude
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Ext^{1}_{K_{r}}(B_{2},B_{1})\!=\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{K_{r}}(B_{2},B_{1})\!-\!q_{r}(1,1)\!=\!r\!-\!2\!>\!0
. In particular, we find a non-split short exact sequence

(\ast)\quad(0)\longrightarrow B_{1}\longrightarrow N\longrightarrow B_{2}\longrightarrow(0)

and conclude with
[
2
, (VIII.2.8)]

\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\End_{K_{r}}(N)\!<\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\End(B_{1}\!\oplus\!B_{2})\!=\!2
. Hence
N
is a brick with dimension vector
(2,2)
and regular by Kac’s Theorem. Since regular bricks for
K_{r}
are quasi-simple (see
[
45
, (9.2),(9.4)]
), we conclude that every quasi-simple vertex in the regular component
\mathcal{C}
of
N
is a brick.

There exists
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\tau^{-n}_{K_{r}}(B_{1}),\tau^{-n}_{K_{r}}(B_{2}),\tau^{-n}_{K_{r}}(N)\in\EKP(K_{r})
. Recall that
\tau^{-n}_{K_{r}}
is an exact endo-functor of the category
\reg(K_{r})
of regular representations.
In view of (
\ast
), there results an exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow\tau_{K_{r}}^{-n}(B_{1})\longrightarrow\tau^{-n}_{K_{r}}(N)\longrightarrow\tau^{-n}_{K_{r}}(B_{2})\longrightarrow(0),

whose (nonzero) extreme terms have equal dimension vectors and hence equal slopes. The See-Saw Lemma now shows that
\tau^{-n}_{K_{r}}(N)
is not stable. By Corollary
6.2.2
(2),
\mathcal{C}\cap\EKP(K_{r})
does not contain any stable vertices.

Proposition 6.2.3
.

Let
E\in\EKP(K_{r})
be elementary. Then
E
is stable.

Proof.

Let
(0)\subsetneq X\subsetneq E
be a proper indecomposable subrepresentation. In view of Proposition
6.2.1
we may assume that
X
is regular. Thanks to
[
45
, (4.4)]
, the factor module
E/X
is
preinjective. Consequently,
\Delta_{E/X}\!<\!0
, so that
\Delta_{E}\!=\!\Delta_{X}\!+\!\Delta_{E/X}\!<\!\Delta_{X}
. As
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}X_{1}\neq\!0
, we obtain

\mu(X)<\frac{\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}X_{1}}{\Delta_{E}}\leq\mu(E).

As in the proof of Corollary
6.2.2
, this implies the assertion. ∎

6.3.
Filtrations

As before, we assume that
r\!\geq\!3
and let

\sigma_{r}\!:=\!\begin{pmatrix}r&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{Z})

be the invertible matrix that describes the
\sigma_{K_{r}}
-orbits on the level of the Grothendieck group
K_{0}(\rep(K_{r}))
: For
M\in\rep_{2}(K_{r})
we have
\udim\sigma_{K_{r}}(M)\!=\!\sigma_{r}.\udim M
, while

\udim\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(N)\!=\!\sigma_{r}^{-1}.\udim N
for
N\in\rep_{1}(K_{r})
, cf. Section
2.2
.

We denote by
\lfloor\ \rfloor:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
the floor function.

Lemma 6.3.1
.

Let
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\}
be such that
q_{r}(a,b)\!<\!0
and
b\!=\!\lfloor aL_{r}\rfloor
. Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
be an indecomposable representation with
\udim M\!=\!\sigma_{r}^{-\ell}.(a,b)
for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
M
is regular and there is a filtration

(0)=M_{0}\subsetneq M_{1}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq M_{n-1}\subsetneq M_{n}=M

such that for each
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
the filtration factor
M_{i}/M_{i-1}
is elementary and belongs to
\EKP(K_{r})
.

Proof.

As
q_{r}(\udim M)\!=\!q_{r}(a,b)\!<\!0
, Kac’s Theorem ensures that the representation
M
is regular. We define
N\!:=\!\sigma_{K_{r}}^{\ell}(M)
and note that
\udim N\!=\!(a,b)
. Thanks to
[
9
, (5.2(1))]

there is a filtration

(0)=N_{0}\subsetneq N_{1}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq N_{n-1}\subsetneq N_{n}=N

such that for each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
the filtration factor
E^{j}:=N_{j}/N_{j-1}
is elementary. By the same token, there is at most one
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
E^{i}
is not in
\EKP(K_{r})
and in this case there is
\mathfrak{v}\in\Gr_{1}(A_{r})
such that
E^{i}\cong E(\mathfrak{v})
. We have
\udim E(\mathfrak{v})=(1,r\!-\!1)
and therefore
\udim\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(E^{i})=(r\!-\!1,r^{2}\!-\!r\!-\!1)
. The representation
X\!:=\!\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(E^{i})
satisfies

q_{r}(\udim X)\!+\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}X_{2}\!-\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}X_{1}=(r\!-\!2)(r\!-\!1)\geq 1

and
[
9
, (3.1)]
ensures that
X\in\EKP(K_{r})
. Since
\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}
is exact on the category of regular representations and
\EKP(K_{r})\!=\!\repp(K_{r},1)
is closed under
\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-1}
(see Theorem

2.2.3
), we conclude that

(0)=\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-\ell}(N_{0})\subsetneq\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-\ell}(N_{1})\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-\ell}(N_{n-1})\subsetneq\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-\ell}(N_{n})=M

is the desired filtration. ∎

For
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}_{0}
and
d\in\{1,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}
we put

\Delta_{(a,b)}(d):=b\!-\!da.

Lemma 6.3.2
.

Let
(a,b)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}\!\smallsetminus\!\{0\}
be such that
q_{r}(a,b)\!<\!0
and
q_{r}(a,b)\!+\!\Delta_{(a,b)}(r\!-\!1)\!\geq\!1
. For
\ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we let
(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\!:=\!\sigma_{r}^{-\ell}.(a,b)
. Then the
following statements hold:

(1)

We have
q_{r}(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\!+\Delta_{(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})}(r\!-\!1)\geq\!1
.

(2)

Let
M
be an indecomposable representation with dimension vector
(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})
. Then
M
has a filtration such that each filtration factor is an elementary representation belonging to
\EKP(K_{r})
. In particular,

M\in\EKP(K_{r})
.

Proof.

Let
(x,y)\in\mathbb{N}^{2}
. Direct computation shows that
\Delta_{\sigma_{r}^{-1}.(x,y)}(r\!-\!1)=\Delta_{(x,y)}
.

(1) We prove the statement by induction on
\ell\!\geq\!0
, the case
\ell\!=\!0
being trivial. For
\ell\!>\!0
we obtain

\displaystyle q_{r}(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\!+\!\Delta_{(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})}(r\!-\!1)

\displaystyle=q_{r}(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\!+\!\Delta_{\sigma_{r}^{-1}.(a_{\ell-1},b_{\ell-1})}(r\!-\!1)

\displaystyle=q_{r}(a_{\ell-1},b_{\ell-1})\!+\!\Delta_{(a_{\ell-1},b_{\ell-1})}

\displaystyle>q_{r}(a_{\ell-1},b_{\ell-1})\!+\!\Delta_{(a_{\ell-1},b_{\ell-1})}(r\!-\!1)\geq 1.

(2) In view of Lemma
6.3.1
, it suffices to show that
(x,y)\!:=\!\sigma_{r}.(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})
satisfies
q_{r}(x,y)\!<\!0
and
y\!=\!\lfloor xL_{r}\rfloor
. Clearly, we have
q_{r}(x,y)\!=\!q_{r}(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\!=\!q_{r}(a,b)\!<\!0
.
By definition, we obtain

q_{r}(x,y)\!+\!y\!-\!x=q_{r}(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\!+\!\Delta_{\sigma_{r}.(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})}=q_{r}(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\!+\!\Delta_{(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})}(r\!-\!1),

so that (1) implies
q_{r}(x,y)\!+\!y\!-\!x\!\geq\!1
. As
q_{r}(x,y)\!<\!0
, we have
x\!<\!y
and
[
9
, (4.2)]
yields
y\!=\!\lfloor xL_{r}\rfloor
. ∎

Corollary 6.3.3
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Gamma(K_{r})
be a regular component and assume that
X\in\mathcal{C}
is quasi-simple such that
q_{r}(\udim X)\!+\!\Delta_{X}(r\!-\!1)\!\geq\!1
. Then every representation in

(X\!\rightarrow)
possesses a filtration whose filtration factors are elementary and belong to
\EKP(K_{r})
.

Proof.

Let
M\in(X\!\rightarrow)
, then each of the filtration factors
M_{[i]}/M_{[i-1]}
of the quasi-composition series of
M
is isomorphic to
\tau^{-\ell_{i}}_{K_{r}}(X)\cong\sigma_{K_{r}}^{-2\ell_{i}}(X)
for some

\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. By Lemma
6.3.2
, each of these representations possesses a filtration such that each filtration factor is elementary and in
\EKP(K_{r})
.∎

Remark
.

Let
M\in\rep(K_{r})
be regular indecomposable. In view of Lemma
4.3.3
and
q_{r}(\udim\sigma^{-1}_{K_{r}}(M))\!=\!q_{r}(\udim M)
, it follows that every regular
AR-component
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Gamma(K_{r})
contains a quasi-simple representation
X
such that
q_{r}(\udim X)\!+\!\Delta_{X}(r\!-\!1)\!\geq\!1
.

6.4.
The Harder-Narasimhan filtration

Definition
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
be a torsion-free coherent sheaf. There exists a uniquely determined filtration, the
Harder-Narasimhan filtration (cf.
[
48
, Section 1.2]
),

(0)=\mathcal{F}_{0}\subsetneq\mathcal{F}_{1}\subsetneq\mathcal{F}_{2}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\subsetneq\mathcal{F}_{t}=\mathcal{F}

that satisfies the following two properties:

(1)

Each quotient
\mathcal{F}^{i}\!:=\!\mathcal{F}_{i}/\mathcal{F}_{i-1}
is semistable (
i\in\{1,\ldots,t\}
).

(2)

We have
\mu(\mathcal{F}^{1})\!>\!\mu(\mathcal{F}^{2})\!>\cdots>\!\mu(\mathcal{F}^{t-1})\!>\!\mu(\mathcal{F}^{t})
.

Remark
.

The sheaf
\mathcal{F}
is semistable if and only if
t\!=\!1
.

Lemma 6.4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
be a non-zero, torsion-free sheaf that is not semistable. There exists a non-zero subsheaf
\mathcal{U}\subsetneq\mathcal{F}
with the following properties:

(a)

\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U}
is semistable,

(b)

\mu(\mathcal{U})\!>\!\mu(\mathcal{F})
,

(c)

\Hom_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(\mathcal{S},\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U})\!=\!(0)
for each semistable sheaf
\mathcal{S}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
with
\mu(\mathcal{F})\!\leq\!\mu(\mathcal{S})
.

Proof.

We consider the Harder-Narasimhan filtration

(0)=\mathcal{F}_{0}\subsetneq\mathcal{F}_{1}\subsetneq\mathcal{F}_{2}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\subsetneq\mathcal{F}_{t}=\mathcal{F}

with
t\!\geq\!2
and set
\mathcal{U}\!:=\!\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
. Then (a) holds by definition. Using Lemma
6.1
and the defining property (2), induction on
s
implies that
\mu(\mathcal{F}_{s-1})\!>\!\mu(\mathcal{F}_{s})\!>\!\mu(\mathcal{F}^{s})
for
s\in\{2,\ldots,t\}
. Consequently, (b) holds as well. Lemma
6.1
also yields

\mu(\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U})=\mu(\mathcal{F}^{t})<\mu(\mathcal{F})\leq\mu(\mathcal{S})

for each semistable sheaf
\mathcal{S}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
with
\mu(\mathcal{F})\!\leq\!\mu(\mathcal{S})
.
Let
f:\mathcal{S}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U}
be a morphism for such a sheaf
\mathcal{S}
. As
\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{S}
are semistable, the assumption
f\!\neq\!0
in conjunction with Lemma
6.1
implies

\mu(\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U})\geq\mu(\im f)\geq\mu(\mathcal{S}),

which contradicts
\mu(\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U})\!<\!\mu(\mathcal{S})
.∎

6.5.
The Beilinson Spectral Sequence

For
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
and
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we write
\mathcal{F}(n)
for the
n
-th Serre twist of
\mathcal{F}
.

We denote by
\Omega
the cotangent bundle of
\mathbb{P}^{r-1}
(see
[
7
, (6.10)]
), and, for
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, we let
\Omega^{m}\!:=\!\bigwedge^{m}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}\Omega
be
the
m
-th exterior power of
\Omega
, where
\Omega^{0}\!:=\!\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}
.

Lemma 6.5.1
.

Let
-r\!<\!b\!\leq\!0
and
i\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
. For
m\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
we have

\HH^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r-1},\Omega^{m}(m\!+\!b))\cong\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbbm{k}&\text{if}\ i\!=\!m\!=\!-b\\
(0)&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

For
i\!\geq\!r
there is nothing to show (see
[
7
, (3.10.5)]
). Hence we assume that
0\!\leq\!i\!\leq\!r\!-\!1
. By Bott’s formula (see
[
7
, (6.10.1)]
) the only cases to consider are

•

i\!=\!0
,
0\!\leq\!m\!<\!r
and
m\!+\!b\!>\!m
: This can not happen since
b\!\leq\!0
.

•

m\!+\!b\!=\!0
and
m\!=\!i
: Then
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\HH^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r-1},\Omega^{m}(m\!+\!b))\!=\!1
.

•

i\!=\!r\!-\!1
,
0\!\leq\!m\!<\!r
,
m\!+\!b\!\leq\!m\!-\!r
: This can not happen since
b\!>\!-r
. ∎

Corollary 6.5.2
.

(cf.
[
23
, (3.2)]
)
Let
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
be a coherent sheaf,
-(r\!-\!1)\!<\!d\!\leq\!0
and

(0)\longrightarrow\widetilde{V_{1}}(d)\longrightarrow\widetilde{V_{2}}(d)\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}\longrightarrow(0)

be a short exact sequence. If
\HH^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r-1},\mathcal{F}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}\!\Omega^{m}(m))\!\neq\!(0)
, then
i\!=\!-d
and
m\in\{-d,-(d\!-\!1)\}
,

Proof.

Upon tensoring the exact sequence over
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}
with the vector bundle
\Omega^{m}(m)
, we obtain the exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow V_{1}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\Omega^{m}(m\!+\!d\!-\!1)\longrightarrow V_{2}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\Omega^{m}(m\!+\!d)\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}\!\Omega^{m}(m)\longrightarrow(0).

The long exact sequence in cohomology provides an exact sequence

V_{2}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\HH^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r-1},\Omega^{m}(m\!+\!d))\longrightarrow\HH^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r-1},\mathcal{F}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}\!\Omega^{m}(m))\longrightarrow V_{1}\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\HH^{i+1}(\mathbb{P}^{r-1},\Omega^{m}(m\!+\!d\!-\!1)).

Since
-r\!<\!d\!-\!1,d\!\leq\!0
, Lemma
6.5.1
shows, that the assumption
\HH^{i}(\mathbb{P}^{r-1},\mathcal{F}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}\!\Omega^{m}(m))\!\neq\!(0)
entails either
i\!=\!m\!=\!-d
or

i\!+\!1\!=\!m\!=-(d\!-\!1)
. Hence
(i,m)\!=\!(-d,-d)
or
(i,m)\!=\!(-d,-(d\!-\!1))
. ∎

Theorem 6.5.3
.

(see
[
41
, (8.28)]
) Let
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
be a coherent sheaf. There is a second quadrant spectral sequence
E\!=\!E_{\mathcal{F}}
with first page

E^{pq}_{1}=\HH^{q}(\mathbb{P}^{r-1},\mathcal{F}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}\!\Omega^{-p}(-p))\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(p)

for
(-p,q)\in\{0,\ldots,r\!-\!1\}^{2}
and zero otherwise, which converges to

E^{p+q}\Rightarrow\begin{cases}\mathcal{F},\ \text{for}\ p\!+\!q\!=\!0\\
(0),\ \text{for}\ p\!+\!q\!\neq\!0.\end{cases}

Moreover, this sequence is natural in
\mathcal{F}
.

Definition
.

Given
-(r\!-\!1)\!<\!d\!\leq\!0
and
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
, we denote the differential
d_{1}^{d-1,-d}
on the first page of the spectral sequence
E_{\mathcal{F}}
by

\alpha_{\mathcal{F},d}:E_{1}^{d-1,-d}\longrightarrow E_{1}^{d,-d}.

Corollary 6.5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a Steiner bundle,
-(r\!-\!1)\!<\!d\!\leq\!0
. There exists a short exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow E_{1}^{d-1,-d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{\mathcal{F}(d),d}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{1}^{d,-d}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}(d)\longrightarrow(0).

Proof.

By Corollary
6.5.2
we know that the first page of the spectral sequence
E_{\mathcal{F}(d)}
has at most two non-zero entries
E_{1}^{d-1,-d}\!=\!\HH^{-d}(\mathbb{P}^{r-1},\mathcal{F}(d)\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}\!\Omega^{-(d-1)}(-(d\!-\!1)))\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(d\!-\!1)
and
E_{1}^{d,-d}\!=\!\HH^{-d}(\mathbb{P}^{r-1},\mathcal{F}(d)\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}\!\Omega^{-d}(-d))\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(d)
. In particular,
E_{\mathcal{F}(d)}
collapses at
E_{2}
, and we have
E_{\infty}\!=\!E_{2}
. Since
E_{1}^{d+1,-d}\!=\!(0)\!=\!E_{1}^{d-2,-d}
, we obtain

E_{2}^{d,-d}=\ker d_{1}^{d,-d}/\im\alpha_{\mathcal{F}(d),d}=E_{1}^{d,-d}/\im\alpha_{\mathcal{F}(d),d}=\coker\alpha_{\mathcal{F}(d),d}\ \ \text{and}\ \ E_{2}^{d-1,-d}=\ker\alpha_{\mathcal{F}(d),d}

while all other terms vanish. Now Theorem
6.5.3
implies
\ker\alpha_{\mathcal{F}(d),d}\!=\!(0)
and
\mathcal{F}(d)\cong\coker\alpha_{\mathcal{F}(d),d}
.
There results the short exact sequence

(0)\longrightarrow E_{1}^{d-1,-d}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha_{\mathcal{F}(d),d}}}{{\longrightarrow}}E_{1}^{d,-d}\longrightarrow\mathcal{F}(d)\longrightarrow(0).\qed

6.6.
Stability in
\EKP(K_{3})
and
\StVect(\mathbb{P}^{2})

In terms of its methods, this section is based on those set forth in
[
24
, (IV.3)]
(see also
[
25
, (5.1.2)]
).

Lemma 6.6.1
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{r-1})
be torsion-free,
b\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then
\mathcal{F}
is (semi)stable if and only if the same is true for
\mathcal{F}(b)
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(b)\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}\!-
induces an exact auto-equivalence on the category of coherent sheaves. Given a torsion-free coherent sheaf
\mathcal{G}\!\neq\!(0)
, the sheaf
\mathcal{G}(b)
is locally
isomorphic to
\mathcal{G}
and hence also torsion-free and of the same rank. We conclude with
[
7
, (7.5.1)]

\mu(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}(b)\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{r-1}}\!\mathcal{G})=\mu(\mathcal{G}(b))=\frac{c_{1}(\mathcal{G}(b))}{\rk(\mathcal{G})}=\frac{c_{1}(\mathcal{G})\!+\!b\rk(\mathcal{G})}{\rk(\mathcal{G})}=\mu(\mathcal{G})\!+\!b.

Hence the equivalence respects the order of slopes. ∎

Proposition 6.6.2
.

Let
M\in\EKP(K_{3})
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)

If
\Delta_{M}(2)\!\geq\!0
and
M
is semistable, then
\TilTheta(M)
is semistable.

(2)

If
\Delta_{M}(2)\!>\!0
and
M
is stable, then
\TilTheta(M)
is stable.

Proof.

We begin the proof by listing several properties that both cases have in common.

Given
\mathcal{U}\in\Coh(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, we consider the differentials

\alpha_{\mathcal{U},-1}\colon\HH^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{U}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\Omega^{2}(2))\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-2)\longrightarrow\HH^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{U}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\Omega^{1}(1))\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-1)

and

\delta_{\mathcal{U},-1}\colon\HH^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{U}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\Omega^{2}(2))\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-2)\longrightarrow\HH^{2}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{U}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\Omega^{1}(1))\!\otimes_{\mathbbm{k}}\!\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-1)

from the first page of the Beilinson spectral sequence
E_{\mathcal{U}}
, see Theorem
6.5.3
.

Let
\mathcal{F}\!:=\!\TilTheta(M)(-1)
and
\mathcal{U}\subsetneq\mathcal{F}
be a subsheaf. The inclusion
\mathcal{U}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{F}

induces
\mathbbm{k}
-linear maps

\tau_{i}:\HH^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{U}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\Omega^{i}(i))\longrightarrow\HH^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{F}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\Omega^{i}(i))

for
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Throughout, we assume that
\mathcal{U}\subsetneq\mathcal{F}
is a non-zero subsheaf such that

(\star)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \Hom_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\ell),\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U})\!=\!(0)\ \ \text{for all}\ \ \ell\!\geq\!0.

(a)
\tau_{1}
is injective
.

Recall that
\HH^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{G})
is naturally isomorphic to the global section of
\mathcal{G}
for every
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
-module (see
[
7
, (3.10)]
). Hence the isomorphism
\Omega^{1}(1)^{\vee}\cong\mathcal{T}(-1)
and
[
7
, (6.1.3)]
imply

\HH^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{2},(\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U})\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\Omega^{1}(1))\cong\HH^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{2},(\Omega^{1}(1)^{\vee})^{\vee}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!(\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U}))\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{T}(-1),\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U}).

Application of the left exact functor
\Hom_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-,\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U})
to the Euler sequence
(0)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-1)\longrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}^{3}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}(-1)\longrightarrow(0)
yields
\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{T}(-1),\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U})\!\leq\!\dim_{\mathbbm{k}}\Hom_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}^{3},\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\star)}}{{=}}0
. Hence we have
\HH^{0}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}},(\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U})\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\Omega^{1}(1))\!=\!(0)
. The long exact sequence in cohomology yields the exactness of

\HH^{0}(\mathbb{P}^{2},(\mathcal{F}/\mathcal{U})\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\Omega^{1}(1))\longrightarrow\HH^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{U}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\Omega^{1}(1))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tau_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\HH^{1}(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{F}\!\otimes_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\Omega^{1}(1)).

Consequently,
\tau_{1}
is injective.
\diamond

(1) Suppose that
